Zeros and critical points of Gaussian fields: cumulants asymptotics and limit theorems
Michele Ancona, Louis Gass, Thomas Letendre, Michele Stecconi

Abstract
Let f : ℝ d → ℝ k f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k} be a smooth centered stationary Gaussian field and ℬ ⊂ ℝ d \mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} be a bounded Borel set. In this paper, we determine the asymptotics as R → ∞ R\to\infty of all the cumulants of the ( d − k ) (d-k) -dimensional volume of f − 1 ​ ( 0 ) ∩ R ​ ℬ f^{-1}(0)\cap R\mathcal{B} . When k = 1 k=1 , we obtain similar asymptotics for the number of critical points of f f in R ​ ℬ R\mathcal{B} . Our main hypotheses are some regularity and non-degeneracy of the field, as well as mild integrability conditions on the first derivatives of its covariance kernel. As corollaries of these cumulants estimates, we deduce a strong Law of Large Numbers and a Central Limit Theorem for the nodal volume (resp. the number of critical points) of a regular and non-degenerate enough field whose covariance decays fast enough at infinity. Our results hold more generally for a one-parameter family ( f R ) (f_{R}) of Gaussian fields admitting a stationary local scaling limit as R → ∞ R\to\infty , for example Kostlan polynomials in the large degree limit. They also hold for the random measures of integration over the vanishing locus of f R f_{R} as R → + ∞ R\to+\infty .



Keywords:

cumulants; Gaussian fields; Kac–Rice formula; Kergin interpolation; limit theorems; random submanifolds.

MSC 2020:

60D05; 60F05; 60F15; 60F25; 60G15; 60G60.

1
Introduction

1.1
Zero set of a stationary Gaussian field in a growing set

Let us begin by describing our results in a model case. The general framework we consider will be introduced in Section
1.2
below. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered stationary
\mathcal{C}^{2}
Gaussian field. Under mild non-degeneracy assumptions, its zero set
Z=f^{-1}(0)
is almost-surely a codimension-
k
submanifold in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The Euclidean metric of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
then induces a volume measure on
Z
, which is its
(d-k)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. Let
\mathbb{B}
denote the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
R>0
, we let
R\mathbb{B}=\{Rx\mid x\in\mathbb{B}\}
. It is a classical problem in probability to study the asymptotic distribution as
R\to+\infty
of the random variables
\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathbb{B}\right)
, where the volume is computed with respect to the
(d-k)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. More generally, we can consider the same question where the unit ball
\mathbb{B}
is replaced by any bounded Borel set
\mathcal{B}
.

Letting
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
denote the expectation of a random variable
X
, the classical Kac–Rice formula, cf.
[
1
,
11
,
31
,
51
]
, implies that:

\forall R>0,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\right]=\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B}),

(1.1)

where the volume of
\mathcal{B}
is computed with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\gamma_{1}(f)
is a constant depending only on the distribution of
f
. Variance asymptotics and a Central Limit Theorem (CLT) were obtained for the length of random curves (
d=2
and
k=1
) in
[
34
]
. Similar results for the volume of random hypersurfaces (
k=1
) were obtained in
[
35
]
, see also
[
19
]
. Proving that
\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap\mathcal{B}\right)
admits a finite
p
-th moment is already a delicate problem, that received a lot of attention, in several contexts, over the past years, see for instance
[
6
,
12
,
13
,
42
]
. In
[
4
,
23
]
, the authors gave simple sufficient conditions on the field
f
ensuring that
\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)
admits a finite
p
-th moment for any bounded Borel set
\mathcal{B}
and
R>0
. Our first result establishes the large
R
asymptotics of the
p
-th central moment of
\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)
, see Theorem
1.1
.

Let us introduce some notation in order to state our main hypotheses. Given a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we denote by
\lvert\alpha\rvert=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{d}
and by
\partial^{\alpha}
the partial derivative
\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
. If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
then
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}
and we simply let
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}=\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)}
. For all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
r(x,y)=\Cov\left(f(x),f(y)\right)
the covariance operator of
f(x)
and
f(y)
, that is, the linear map from
\mathbb{R}^{k}
to itself whose matrix is equal to
\mathbb{E}\left[f(x)\,\prescript{\text{t}}{}{\!f(y)}\right]
. If
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{q}
for some
q\in\mathbb{N}
, then its covariance kernel
r
admits continuous partial derivatives
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q
and
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q
. Moreover, since
f
is stationary, we have
r^{\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=r^{\alpha,\beta}(0,y-x)
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[1,+\infty]
. Assuming that
f
is at least of class
\mathcal{C}^{q}
, we introduce the following hypotheses. We will comment on these after the statements of our main theorems, see Section
1.4
.

Non-degeneracy (ND
(q)
).

The field
f
is
q
-non-degenerate
, that is: for any
l\in\{1,\dots,q+1\}
, any
m_{1},\dots,m_{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
m_{1}+\dots+m_{l}=q+1
, and any family of pairwise distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the centered Gaussian vector
\left(\partial^{\alpha}f(x_{i})\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant l,\lvert\alpha\rvert<m_{i}}
is non-degenerate.

Decay of correlations in the limit (DCL
(q,s)
).

There exist
\omega>0
and an even bounded function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
such that:

•

we have
g(x)\xrightarrow[\lVert x\rVert\to+\infty]{}0
;

•

the function
g_{\omega}:x\mapsto\sup_{\lVert y\rVert\leqslant\omega}g(x+y)
belongs to
L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

•

for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(0,z)\right\rVert\leqslant g(z)
.

In the following, we denote by
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda\right)
the real-valued centered Gaussian distribution of variance
\lambda\geqslant 0
. Recalling that the expected volume of
Z
in a growing set is given by Equation (
1.1
), our first result is the following.

Theorem 1.1

(Central moments asymptotics for volumes)
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
be an integer and
Z
be the zero set of a centered stationary Gaussian field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{2p-1}
. We assume that (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) and (
DCL
(
2p-1,2
)
) hold. Then, there exists
\gamma_{2}(f)\geqslant 0
, depending only on the distribution of
f
, such that, for any bounded Borel subset
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)-R^{d}\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})}{R^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{p}\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\left(\gamma_{2}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)^{p}\right].

Remark 1.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.1
, we have
\gamma_{1}(f)>0
. The constant
\gamma_{2}(f)
is non-negative as a limit variance, and we expect it to be positive for a generic field. Conditions ensuring that
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
are stated in
[
26
]
. Under our assumptions, if
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
, where
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
are independent real-valued stationary isotropic fields on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
, see
[
26
, Thm. 1.7]
. Recall also that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)^{p}\right]=0
if and only if
p
is odd.

Let us now focus on the important case of gradient fields. Note that it is not directly covered by Theorem
1.1
since, if
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the gradient of some Gaussian field
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, then
f
does not satisfy (
ND
(
q
)
) for any
q\geqslant 1
. Indeed,
\left(\partial_{j}f(0)\right)_{1\leqslant j\leqslant d}=\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h(0)\right)_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}
is supported on the set of symmetric matrices, hence is degenerate as a Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. In order to deal with gradient fields, we introduce a variation on our non-degeneracy hypothesis (
ND
(
q
)
) adapted to these fields.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
. We assume that
f=\nabla h
, where
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a centered stationary Gaussian field at least of class
\mathcal{C}^{q+1}
. The following condition depends only on
f
but is more conveniently stated in terms of
h
.

\nabla
-non-degeneracy (
\nabla
-ND
(q)
).

The field
h
such that
\nabla h=f
satisfies the following: for any
l\in\{1,\dots,q+1\}
, any
m_{1},\dots,m_{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
m_{1}+\dots+m_{l}=q+1
, and any pairwise distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Gaussian vector
\left(\partial^{\alpha}h(x_{i})\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant l,0<\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant m_{i}}
is non-degenerate.

This alternative non-degeneracy hypothesis allows us to prove the analogue of Theorem
1.1
for the number of critical points of
h
. Note that, in this case,
Z
is a locally finite set, and its
0
-dimensional Hausdorff measure is its counting measure.

Theorem 1.3

(Central moments asymptotics for critical points)
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
be an integer and
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{2p}
centered stationary Gaussian field. Let
f=\nabla h
and
Z=f^{-1}(0)
. We assume that (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
) and (
DCL
(
2p-1,2
)
) hold. Then, there exists
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
, depending only on the distribution of
f
, such that, for any bounded Borel subset
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\card\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)-R^{d}\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})}{R^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right)^{p}\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\left(\gamma_{2}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)^{p}\right],

where
\gamma_{1}(f)
is the constant appearing in Equation (
1.1
).

Remark 1.4
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.3
, we have
\gamma_{1}(f)>0
. Note that, in this case, we always have
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
.

In the case where the field
f
is smooth and non-degenerate at any order, we recover a CLT as
R\to+\infty
, by the method of moments. If
f
is the gradient of an isotropic field, a similar result was proved by Nicolaescu
[
49
, Thm. 2.2]
, under slightly different hypotheses.

Theorem 1.5

(Central Limit Theorem, stationary case)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
centered stationary Gaussian field such that (
DCL
(
q,2
)
) holds for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. We assume that, either (
ND
(
q
)
) holds for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
, or
f
is the gradient of some
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) holds for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
Z=f^{-1}(0)
. Then, for any bounded Borel subset
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the following convergence in distribution:

\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)-R^{d}\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})}{R^{\frac{d}{2}}}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\gamma_{2}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})\right).

The Borel–Cantelli Lemma and a monotonicity argument also yield a Law of Large Numbers as
R\to+\infty
for the volume of
f^{-1}(0)
in nice enough growing sets. Recall that a set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be star-shaped with respect to
0
if, for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
and
t\in[0,1]
, we have
tx\in\mathcal{B}
.

Theorem 1.6

(Law of Large Numbers, stationary case)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{3}
centered stationary Gaussian field such that (
DCL
(
3,2
)
) holds. We assume that, either (
ND
(
3
)
) holds, or
f
is the gradient of some field
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and (
\nabla
-
ND
(
3
)
) holds. Let
Z=f^{-1}(0)
. Then, for any bounded Borel subset
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is star-shaped with respect to
0
, we have the following almost-sure convergence:

\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{a.s.}}\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B}).

1.2
Cumulants asymptotics

The results from Section
1.1
are special cases of results obtained in a general framework that we now introduce. As above, let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
denote the dimension of the ambient space and
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
be the almost-sure codimension of the random submanifolds we are interested in. Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered stationary Gaussian field. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open set and
\Omega\subset U
be a non-empty open subset at positive distance from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus U
. That is,
\inf\left\{\lVert y-x\rVert\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x\in\Omega,y\notin U\right\}>0
, where
\lVert\cdot\rVert
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset(0,+\infty)
be an unbounded set. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
f_{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
centered Gaussian field from
RU=\{Rx\mid x\in U\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We let
r_{R}
denote its covariance kernel, which is defined of
RU\times RU
. Under mild assumptions, the zero set
Z_{R}=f_{R}^{-1}(0)
is almost-surely a submanifold of codimension
k
in
RU
. As such, integrating over
Z_{R}
with respect to the
(d-k)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d-k}
defines a Radon measure on
RU
(i.e., a Borel measure which is finite on compact subsets), that we also denote by
Z_{R}
. For all
\phi\in L^{1}(RU)
, we denote by

\langle Z_{R}\,,\phi\rangle=\int_{Z_{R}}\phi(x)\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d-k}(x),

(1.2)

which is an almost-surely well-defined random variable, see Section
4.1
for details. Quantities of the type
\langle Z_{R}\,,\phi\rangle
are called the
linear statistics
of
Z_{R}
.

In this paper, we are interested in the asymptotic behavior of the random measure
Z_{R}
as
R\to+\infty
, under the assumption that
f_{R}
converges towards
f
, in a sense to be made precise below. We study this asymptotic behavior through the asymptotic distribution of some linear statistics. In order to obtain converging objects, we have to consider a scaled version of
Z_{R}
, denoted by
\nu_{R}
, which is the random Radon measure on
U
defined by:

\forall\phi\in L^{1}(U),\qquad\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle=\frac{1}{R^{d}}\left\langle Z_{R}\,,\phi\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rangle.

(1.3)

Alternatively,
\nu_{R}
can be described as the measure of integration over the submanifold
\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}\subset U
, up to a scaling factor
\frac{1}{R^{k}}
since we consider
(d-k)
-dimensional submanifolds. Note that we normalized
\nu_{R}
so that
\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]
converges as
R\to+\infty
. For example, if
\mathcal{B}\subset U
is a bounded Borel set and
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
is its indicator function then, by (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), we have:

\langle\nu_{R}\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle=\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z_{R}\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}.

(1.4)

If moreover
f_{R}
is the restriction
f_{|RU}
of
f
to
RU
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, then the linear statistics in (
1.4
) are random variables of the form studied in Section
1.1
, and their expectation is given by (
1.1
).

Let
q\in N
and
s\in[1,+\infty]
. We already introduced Hypotheses (
ND
(
q
)
), (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) and (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) in Section
1.1
. In the setting of the present section, we introduce an additional set of hypotheses. We will comment on these in Section
1.4
below.

Regularity (Reg
(q)
).

The centered Gaussian fields
f
and
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
all are of class
\mathcal{C}^{q}
.

Assuming that (
Reg
(
q
)
) holds, we can state our other hypotheses as follows.

Local scaling limit (ScL
(q)
).

For all non-empty compact set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following holds:

\sup_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \sup_{x\in\Omega}\ \sup_{w,z\in\Gamma}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(Rx+w,Rx+z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)\right\rVert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

Uniform decay of correlations (DC
(q,s)
).

There exist
\omega>0
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
such that:

•

the function
g
is even, bounded, and
g(x)\xrightarrow[\lVert x\rVert\to+\infty]{}0
;

•

the function
g_{\omega}:x\mapsto\sup_{\lVert y\rVert\leqslant\omega}g(x+y)
belongs to
L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

•

for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, for all
x,y\in RU
, we have
\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(x,y)\right\rVert\leqslant g(y-x)
.

Given
R\in\mathcal{R}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(U)
, we denote by
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
the
mixed cumulant
of the family of random variables
\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
, if it is well-defined. The precise definition of this mixed cumulant being a bit technical, we postpone it until Section
2.2
. For now, let us say that
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
is defined as a universal polynomial in the mixed moments
\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{a\in A}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{a}\rangle\right]\right)_{A\subset\{1,\dots,p\}}
. For example,
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1})=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{1}\rangle\right]
, and
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
is the covariance of
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{2}\rangle
. Conversely, the mixed moments can be expressed as universal polynomials in the mixed cumulants, so that studying the cumulants of a family of random variables is equivalent to studying its moments or its central moments. In particular, the classical method of moments can be translated into the method of cumulants, see Proposition
2.15
. The main interest of cumulants is that they are designed to detect Gaussianity: a family of random variables is jointly Gaussian if and only if its mixed cumulants of order at least
3
vanish, while the mixed cumulants of order
1
and
2
describe its mean and covariance. The asymptotic behavior of the cumulants of a sequence of random variables can thus be understood as a form of quantitative Central Limit Theorem. In this spirit, the first main result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.7

(Cumulants asymptotics for zero sets)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered stationary Gaussian field. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
f_{R}:RU\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered Gaussian field and
\nu_{R}
be the measure defined by (
1.3
). Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume that Hypotheses (
Reg
(
\max(2,2p-1)
)
), (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) and (
ScL
(
2p-1
)
) hold. Then, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
we have the following.

1.

If (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) holds, then there exists
R_{p}\in\mathcal{R}
, not depending on
\left(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\right)
, such that for all
R\in[R_{p},+\infty)\cap\mathcal{R}
the mixed cumulant
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
is well-defined and finite.

2.

If
p\geqslant 3
and (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
) holds, then
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})=o\left(R^{-\frac{pd}{2}}\right)
as
R\to+\infty
.

3.

If
p=1
, or if
p\geqslant 2
and (
DC
(
2p-1,\frac{p}{p-1}
)
) holds, then there exists
\gamma_{p}(f)\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

R^{d(p-1)}\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{p}(f)\int_{\Omega}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!x.

Moreover,
\gamma_{p}(f)
depends only on the distribution of
f
, it is explicit, and
\gamma_{1}(f)>0
.

In the case where the ambient dimension is
d=1
, Theorem
1.7
was proved in
[
25
]
by refining a result of
[
3
]
. More generally, there is a huge literature concerning zeros of Gaussian fields in dimension
1
, and we refer to the introductions of
[
3
,
25
]
for a discussion of related results in this setting. As in the model case, Theorem
1.7
does not cover the important case where
f
and the fields
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
are gradient fields. Our second main result deals precisely with this case.

Theorem 1.8

(Cumulants asymptotics for critical points)
.

Let us consider a centered stationary Gaussian field
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f=\nabla h
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
h_{R}:RU\to\mathbb{R}
be a centered Gaussian field,
f_{R}=\nabla h_{R}
and
\nu_{R}
be the measure on
U
defined by (
1.3
). Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume that Hypotheses (
Reg
(
\max(2,2p-1)
)
), (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
) and (
ScL
(
2p-1
)
) hold. Then, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
we have the following.

1.

If (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) holds, then there exists
R_{p}\in\mathcal{R}
, not depending on
\left(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\right)
, such that for all
R\in[R_{p},+\infty)\cap\mathcal{R}
the cumulant
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
is well-defined and finite.

2.

If
p\geqslant 3
and (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
) holds, then
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})=o\left(R^{-\frac{pd}{2}}\right)
as
R\to+\infty
.

3.

If
p=1
, or if
p\geqslant 2
and (
DC
(
2p-1,\frac{p}{p-1}
)
) holds, then there exists
\gamma_{p}(f)\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

R^{d(p-1)}\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{p}(f)\int_{\Omega}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!x.

Moreover,
\gamma_{p}(f)
depends only on the distribution of
f
, it is explicit,
\gamma_{1}(f)>0
and
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
.

Remark 1.9
.

Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
describe the asymptotic behavior of
\nu_{R}
restricted to the subset
\Omega\subset U
, since we consider test-functions in
L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Note that this class of test-functions contains the indicator functions of bounded Borel subsets of
\Omega
, so that the associated linear statistics completely characterize the restriction of
\nu_{R}
to
\Omega
.

1.3
Limit theorems and other corollaries

The cumulant asymptotics in Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
imply that the random measures
(\nu_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
satisfy a Law of Large Numbers and a Central Limit Theorem as
R\to+\infty
. They also translate into corollaries that can be understood as asymptotic equidistribution results for the random submanifolds
Z_{R}
. This section gathers these various corollaries.

First, our cumulant asymptotics translates into the following moment asymptotics.

Corollary 1.10

(Moment asymptotics)
.

In the same setting as Theorem
1.7
(resp. Theorem
1.8
), let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and assume that (
Reg
(
\max(2,2p-1)
)
), (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
)) and (
ScL
(
2p-1
)
) hold. Then, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have the following.

1.

If (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) holds, then there exists
R_{p}\in\mathcal{R}
, not depending on
\left(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\right)
, such that for all
R\in[R_{p},+\infty)\cap\mathcal{R}
the mixed moment
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right\rvert\right]
is finite.

2.

If (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
) holds, then we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)^{p}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!x

(1.5)

and

R^{\frac{pd}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right]\right)\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{2}(f)^{\frac{p}{2}}\hskip-6.45831pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\{a_{j},b_{j}\})_{1\leqslant j\leqslant\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\\
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\{a_{j},b_{j}\}=\{1,\dots,p\}\end{subarray}}\!\prod_{j=1}^{\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{a_{j}}(x)\phi_{b_{j}}(x)\dmesure\!x,

(1.6)

where
\gamma_{1}(f)
and
\gamma_{2}(f)
are the constants appearing in Theorem
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
3
), and the sum in (
1.6
) is indexed by the partitions in pairs of
\{1,\dots,p\}
. In particular, if
p
is odd, the right-hand side of (
1.6
) vanishes.

Then, Markov’s inequality yields the following concentration result.

Corollary 1.11

(Concentration)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.7
(resp. Theorem
1.8
), let
\varepsilon>0
and
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. For all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, if (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
)), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
) and (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) hold, then we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right)=O\left(R^{-pd}\right),

where
\gamma_{1}(f)
is the constant appearing in Theorem
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
3
).

Recall that, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, we denoted by
Z_{R}
the zero set of
f_{R}
and that
\nu_{R}
is, up to a normalization factor, the measure of integration over the homothetic copy
\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}\subset U
. A particular case of Corollary
1.11
yields the following equidistribution result.

Corollary 1.12

(Hole probability)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.7
(resp. Theorem
1.8
), let
\mathcal{O}
be a non-empty open subset of
\Omega
. For all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, if (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
)), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
) and (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) hold, then we have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}\cap\mathcal{O}=\emptyset\right)=O\left(R^{-pd}\right).

For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, the random measure
\nu_{R}
is almost surely Radon, that is, finite on compact subsets. Equivalently, it defines a positive continuous linear form on the space
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(U)
of continuous functions with compact support in
U
, endowed with its standard topology. This point of view allows us to state a functional Law of Large Numbers for
(\nu_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
, which is an almost-sure equidistribution result.

Theorem 1.13

(Law of Large Numbers)
.

In the same setting as Theorem
1.7
(resp. Theorem
1.8
), let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and assume that (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
)), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
) and (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) hold. Let
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be a sequence of numbers in
\mathcal{R}
such that
\sum_{n\geqslant 1}R_{n}^{-pd}<\!+\infty
. Then, for all
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have the almost-sure convergence:

\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\text{a.s.}}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x,

(1.7)

where
\gamma_{1}(f)
appears in Theorem
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
3
) and
\dmesure\!x
is the Lebesgue measure on
\Omega
.

Moreover, almost-surely,
\nu_{R_{n}}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\text{weak}-*}\gamma_{1}(f)\dmesure\!x
as Radon measures. That is, almost-surely, the convergence in (
1.7
) holds simultaneously for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\Omega)
.

Finally, we deduce a functional CLT from our cumulant asymptotics. In the following, we denote by
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
the space of test-function on
\Omega
, that is, the space of smooth compactly supported functions on
\Omega
. We also denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
the space of generalized functions on
\Omega
, which is the topological dual of
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
, endowed with its usual topology.

Theorem 1.14

(Central Limit Theorem)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.7
(resp. Theorem
1.8
), we assume that (
Reg
(
q
)
), (
ND
(
q
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
)), (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,2
)
) hold for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the random vector
R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle\right]\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
converges in distribution as
R\to+\infty
to the centered Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with covariance matrix

\gamma_{2}(f)\,\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{j}(x)\dmesure\!x\end{pmatrix}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n},

where
\gamma_{2}(f)
is the constant appearing in Theorem
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
3
).

Moreover, we have the following convergence in distribution in the space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
:

R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\nu_{R}-\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(f)}W,

where
W
is the standard Gaussian White Noise on
\Omega
and, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, the generalized function
\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]
is defined by
\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]\,,\phi\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
.

1.4
Discussion of the main hypotheses and examples

Let us take some time to discuss the hypotheses of our main theorems. First, note that our assumptions satisfy some monotonicity with respect to the parameters. Given
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p\leqslant q
, if (
Reg
(
q
)
) (resp. (
ND
(
q
)
), resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
), resp. (
ScL
(
q
)
)) holds then so does (
Reg
(
p
)
) (resp. (
ND
(
p
)
), resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
p
)
), resp. (
ScL
(
p
)
)). Similarly, for all
s\in[1,+\infty]
, if (
DC
(
q,s
)
) (resp. (
DCL
(
q,s
)
)) holds then so does (
DC
(
p,s
)
) (resp. (
DCL
(
p,s
)
)), with the same
\omega
and
g
. These last two hypotheses are also monotonous with respect to their second parameter. Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
s,t\in[1,+\infty]
be such that
s\leqslant t
. If (
DC
(
q,s
)
) (resp. (
DCL
(
q,s
)
)) holds, then the function
g_{\omega}
appearing in its statement belongs to
L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence, (
DC
(
q,t
)
) (resp. (
DCL
(
q,t
)
)) also holds, with the same parameter
\omega
and the same function
g
.

Assumption (
Reg
(
q
)
) is self-explanatory. In this paper, we consider fields
f
and
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
at least of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
. This is part of a classical set of hypotheses ensuring that the vanishing loci of these fields are almost-surely submanifolds of the right dimension. Higher regularity assumptions are used to control the degeneracy of centered Gaussian vectors of the type
\left(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{p})\right)
, as the distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
collide with one another. Our approach relies on such a control, already to prove the finiteness of the moments or cumulants we study.

Hypothesis (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) is a quantitative decorrelation assumption for the stationary field
f
and its partial derivatives of order at most
q
. Note that this condition holds if and only if it holds for some parameter
\omega>0
and the bounded even function
g:z\mapsto\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(0,z)\right\rVert
. Let us also stress the fact that it is not enough to assume that
g\in L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, even in the simpler setting of Section
1.1
, we need to control a fuzzy version on the correlation kernel
r
. Thus, we have to assume that
g_{\omega}\in L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is strictly stronger than
g\in L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in general.

The meaning of (
ND
(
q
)
) may be less obvious, so let us give some examples in the case of a scalar field, i.e., with
k=1
. Recalling that
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a stationary Gaussian field, it satisfies (
ND
(
0
)
) if and only if
\Var\left(f(0)\right)>0
. It satisfies (
ND
(
1
)
) if and only if
\left(f(0),\nabla_{0}f\right)
is non-degenerate in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
and, for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(0),f(z)\right)
is non-degenerate in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If
f
satisfies (
ND
(
q
)
) then, for any distinct
x_{0},\dots,x_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x_{0}),\dots,f(x_{q})\right)
is non-degenerate, and so is
\left(\partial^{\alpha}f(0)\right)_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q}
. More generally, (
ND
(
q
)
) entails the non-degeneracy of Gaussian vectors formed by evaluating partial derivatives of
f
on
l
pairwise distinct geometric points, up to orders that depend on the multiplicities
(m_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant l}
of these points.

The prototypical example of a field satisfying the previous assumptions is the Bargmann–Fock field
f_{\text{BF}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, whose distribution is described by its correlation kernel
r_{\text{BF}}:(x,y)\mapsto e^{-\frac{\lVert y-x\rVert^{2}}{2}}
. This field is smooth because
r_{\text{BF}}
is smooth. Its correlations have Gaussian decay, so that it satisfies (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[1,+\infty]
. It is also true, but not obvious, that
f_{\text{BF}}
satisfies (
ND
(
q
)
) for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that the distribution of a centered stationary Gaussian field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with covariance kernel
r
is characterized by its spectral measure, which is the finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose Fourier transform equals
z\mapsto r(0,z)
. Adapting the argument of
[
3
, Lem. A.2]
to fields on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
shows that a sufficient condition for
f
to satisfy (
ND
(
q
)
) for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
is that: the support of its spectral measure is not contained in the vanishing locus of a non-zero analytic function. This condition is satisfied by
f_{\text{BF}}
, whose spectral measure is the standard Gaussian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and more generally by any field whose spectral measure contains an open ball in its support. If
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
are independent real-valued Gaussian fields that satisfy (
ND
(
q
)
), then
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
also satisfies (
ND
(
q
)
). For example,
k
independent copies of
f_{\text{BF}}
define a smooth field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
that satisfies (
ND
(
q
)
) and (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[1,+\infty]
.

The previous discussion shows that fields satisfying (
ND
(
q
)
) for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
are fairly generic. Yet, there are interesting examples that do not have this property. For example, the Berry field
f_{\text{Ber}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is best described by saying that its spectral measure is the uniform measure on the unit Euclidean sphere, does not satisfy (
ND
(
2
)
) since
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}^{2}f_{\text{Ber}}=f_{\text{Ber}}
almost-surely.

Assumption (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) is a version of (
ND
(
q
)
) adapted to gradient fields, for which (
ND
(
1
)
) never holds because of the Schwarz Theorem. It means that the only relations between partial derivatives of
f
are those coming from the fact that
f=\nabla h
, for some stationary Gaussian field
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. Given
p\in\mathbb{N}
, Hypothesis (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
) in Theorems
1.3
and
1.8
, is implied by the fact that
h
satisfies (
ND
(
4p-1
)
), but not by the fact that
h
satisfies (
ND
(
q
)
) for some
q<4p-1
. In particular, the gradient field
\nabla f_{\text{BF}}
satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
1.3
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

In the general framework of Section
1.2
, the fact that
U
is convex is a technical assumption used in some interpolation procedure, see Section
3
. It could probably be removed, at the price of some technicalities. The assumption that
\Omega
is at positive distance from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus U
is however important. It ensures that, for any compact subset
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
R\Omega+\Gamma\subset RU
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough. This is a necessary condition for Hypothesis (
ScL
(
q
)
) to make sense.

Hypothesis (
ScL
(
q
)
) means that, for all
x\in\Omega
, the family of Gaussian fields
\left(f_{R}(Rx+\cdot)\right)_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
converges in distribution as
R\to+\infty
towards
f
, locally uniformly in the
\mathcal{C}^{q}
-sense. Moreover, this convergence holds uniformly with respect to
x\in\Omega
. This type of convergence naturally occurs by considering the local scaling limits, in nice charts, of some classical families of Gaussian fields on manifolds, see
[
54
, Sect. 2.2]
. Examples of this kind include the Cut-Off (or Band-Limited) ensembles of Random Waves
[
24
,
29
,
30
,
40
,
48
,
52
,
59
]
and the complex Fubini–Study model
[
5
,
2
,
22
,
27
,
28
,
39
]
, of which the Kostlan polynomials discussed in Section
1.5
are a special case. The Monochromatic Random Waves
[
18
,
36
,
43
,
47
,
58
,
59
]
also give rise to a local scaling limit of the type (
ScL
(
q
)
), but their scaling limit is the Berry field, which is degenerate in the sense that it does not satisfy (
ND
(
2
)
).

The meaning of (
DC
(
q,s
)
) is the same as that of (
DCL
(
q,s
)
), but we control the correlations of the fields
f_{R}
uniformly with respect to
R
, that is, by the same function
g
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
. Note that, if (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,s
)
) hold, then (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) holds, with the same
\omega
and
g
. Indeed, let
x\in\Omega
. For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q
and
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q
, we have
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(Rx,Rx+z)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(0,z)
, by (
ScL
(
q
)
). By (
DC
(
q,s
)
), for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, we have
\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(Rx,Rx+z)\rVert\leqslant g(z)
, hence
\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(0,z)\rVert\leqslant g(z)
.

The model case of Section
1.1
fits in our general framework by considering
\mathcal{R}=(0,+\infty)
,
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}=\Omega
and
f_{R}=f
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
. In this case,
Z_{R}=Z=f^{-1}(0)
for all
R>0
, and the random measures
\nu_{R}
are related to volumes by (
1.4
). Moreover, we have
r_{R}=r
for all
R>0
. Hence, (
ScL
(
q
)
) is tautologically satisfied for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
, and (
DC
(
q,s
)
) is equivalent to (
DCL
(
q,s
)
) for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[1,+\infty]
. Thus, Theorems
1.1
and
1.8
are special cases of Corollary
1.10
.
2
, and Theorem
1.5
is a special case of Theorems
1.14
. Translating Theorem
1.13
in this model case yields a statement which is strictly weaker than Theorem
1.6
. The proof of Theorem
1.6
requires an additional monotonicity argument, see Section
10.2
.

In order to prove the almost-sure convergence in Theorem
1.13
, we have to extract a subsequence
\left(\nu_{R_{n}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
, where
R_{n}
goes to infinity fast enough. In view of Theorem
1.6
, one might wonder whether this almost-sure convergence holds for the whole sequence
(\nu_{R})_{R>0}
as
R\to+\infty
or not. Actually, it is not even clear that such a question makes sense in our general setting. Indeed, we cannot rule out the possibility that there exists a random positive sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
going to infinity such that, say with positive probability,
\nu_{t_{n}}
is ill-defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

1.5
Kostlan polynomials

The techniques developed in this paper allow to deal with some models of Gaussian fields on manifolds. In this section, we state results analogous to those of Sections
1.2
and
1.3
for such a model: Kostlan polynomials on the sphere, in the large degree limit.

For any
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, we let
\lvert\alpha\rvert=\alpha_{0}+\dots+\alpha_{d}
and
\binom{n}{\alpha}=\frac{n!}{\alpha_{0}!\dots\alpha_{d}!}
if
\lvert\alpha\rvert=n
. We also denote by
X=(X_{0},\dots,X_{d})
and
X^{\alpha}=X_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\dots X_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
. A
Kostlan polynomial

[
33
]
of degree
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
is a random homogeneous polynomial in
(d+1)
variables, of the form:

\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=n}a_{\alpha}\sqrt{\binom{n}{\alpha}}X^{\alpha},

(1.8)

where
\left(a_{\alpha}\right)_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=n}
are independent real standard Gaussian variables. Let
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
denote the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
, and
\mathbb{S}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
be the unit sphere endowed with its Euclidean metric. The Kostlan polynomial (
1.8
) defines a smooth centered Gaussian field on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
. Its covariance kernel is
(x,y)\mapsto\langle x\,,y\rangle^{n}=\cos\left(\dist(x,y)\right)^{n}
, where
\dist(x,y)
stands for the geodesic distance between
x
and
y
on the sphere. In particular, this field is stationary, in the sense that its distribution is invariant under the action of the orthogonal group
O_{d+1}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we denote by
P_{n}:\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
the centered Gaussian field whose components are
k
independent Kostlan polynomials of degree
n
. Kostlan
[
33
, Thm. 5.1]
proved that
P_{n}^{-1}(0)
is almost-surely a submanifold of codimension
k
in
\mathbb{S}^{d}
and that
\mathbb{E}\left[\Vol_{d-k}\left(P_{n}^{-1}(0)\right)\right]=n^{\frac{k}{2}}\Vol_{d-k}\left(\mathbb{S}^{d-k}\right)
. Let
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}
denote the random Radon measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
whose linear statistics are defined by:

\forall\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d}),\qquad\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi\right\rangle=n^{-\frac{k}{2}}\int_{P_{n}^{-1}(0)}\phi(x)\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d-k}(x).

(1.9)

In order to be consistent with the notation of Section
1.2
, the measure
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}
should rather be denoted by
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{\sqrt{n}}
. But then, the notation would be inconsistent with the whole literature on the subject. Here, we chose to stay consistent with the existing literature, and use the nicer-looking
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}
. The expectation and asymptotic variance of the linear statistics (
1.9
) were studied in
[
7
,
9
,
20
,
38
,
41
,
39
]
. In particular, it is known that:

\forall\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d}),\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi\right\rangle\right]=\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi(x)\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}(x),

(1.10)

where
\gamma_{1}=\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(\mathbb{S}^{d-k}\right)}{\Vol_{d}\left(\mathbb{S}^{d}\right)}
, and that there exists
\gamma_{2}>0
such that, for all
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, we have:

n^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}(x)\right)\right]\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}(x),

(1.11)

cf.
[
39
, Thm. 1.6 and 1.8]
.

Kostlan polynomials being either even or odd, their values on antipodal points are always fully correlated. In order to avoid long-range correlations, one usually works in the real projective space, and then deduces results on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
from the projective ones. In the context of the following discussion, it is equivalent to consider only pairs of points
(x,y)\in\mathbb{S}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d}
at distance at most
\frac{\pi}{2}
, that is, such that
\langle x\,,y\rangle\geqslant 0
.

It is well-known that Kostlan polynomials admit the Bargmann–Fock field
f_{\text{BF}}
as their local scaling limit. More precisely, let us consider any point on the sphere. In the exponential chart around this point, a Kostlan polynomial of degree
n
reads as a Gaussian field on the ball
\frac{\pi}{2}\mathbb{B}
of center
0
and radius
\frac{\pi}{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Rescaling by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, we obtain a centered Gaussian field on
\sqrt{n}\frac{\pi}{2}\mathbb{B}
whose covariance kernel is:

r_{n}:(x,y)\longmapsto\left(\cos\left(\frac{\lVert x\rVert}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\lVert y\rVert}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+\sin\left(\frac{\lVert x\rVert}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\lVert y\rVert}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left\langle\frac{x}{\lVert x\rVert}\,,\frac{y}{\lVert y\rVert}\right\rangle\right)^{n}.

(1.12)

From (
1.12
), one can check that
r_{n}(x,y)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}e^{-\frac{\lVert y-x\rVert^{2}}{2}}=r_{\text{BF}}(x,y)
point-wise. Actually, this convergence holds locally uniformly in the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-sense, which is reminiscent of (
ScL
(
q
)
). It is also well-known that Kostlan polynomials decorrelate exponentially fast at scale
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
. By stationarity, it is enough to observe that (
1.12
) implies that
\left\lvert r_{n}(0,x)\right\rvert\leqslant e^{-\frac{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}{\pi}}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
x\in\sqrt{n}\frac{\pi}{2}\mathbb{B}
. Similar estimates hold for all the partial derivatives of
r_{n}
, which is reminiscent of (
DC
(
q,s
)
).

Using the previous two facts, our methods yield the following result, which was conjectured in
[
2
, Conj. 1.16]
.

Theorem 1.15

(Moments asymptotics for Kostlan polynomials)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
P_{n}:\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the centered Gaussian field whose components are
k
independent Kostlan polynomials and let
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}
be defined by (
1.9
). Let
p\geqslant 3
be an integer. Then, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
we have the following.

n^{\frac{pd}{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{i}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}\right)\right]\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{2}(f)^{\frac{p}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\{a_{j},b_{j}\})_{1\leqslant j\leqslant\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\\
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\{a_{j},b_{j}\}=\{1,\dots,p\}\end{subarray}}\!\prod_{j=1}^{\lfloor\frac{p}{2}\rfloor}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{a_{j}}\phi_{b_{j}}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d},

where
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are the constants appearing in (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) respectively, and the sum is indexed by the partitions in pairs of
\{1,\dots,p\}
. In particular, for all
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, we have:

n^{\frac{pd}{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi\right\rangle-\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}\right)^{p}\right]\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\left(\gamma_{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi^{2}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)^{p}\right].

As in the Euclidean case, this result implies a functional CLT. In particular, we recover the CLT for volumes established in
[
8
,
9
,
20
]
, by the method of moments.

Theorem 1.16

(Central Limit Theorem for Kostlan polynomials)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.15
, for all
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, the random vector
n^{\frac{d}{4}}\left(\left\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{i}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
converges in distribution as
n\to+\infty
to the centered Gaussian with covariance matrix
\gamma_{2}\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}\end{pmatrix}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
. Moreover, we have
n^{\frac{d}{4}}\left(\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\right]\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}W
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, where
W
is the standard Gaussian White Noise on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
.

The functional Law of Large Numbers for Kostlan polynomials was already established in dimension
d\neq 2
, see
[
2
,
39
]
. Theorem
1.15
implies that it also holds in dimension
d=2
, so that we obtain the following result.

Theorem 1.17

(Law of Large Numbers for Kostlan polynomials)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.15
, for all
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
we have
\langle\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\,,\phi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\text{a.s.}}\gamma_{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}\phi\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}
. Moreover, almost-surely, we have
\nu^{\text{Kos}}_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\text{weak}-*}\gamma_{1}\dmesure\!\mathcal{H}^{d}
as Radon measures.

1.6
Strategy of proof

In this section, we sketch the main steps of the proof of the cumulants asymptotics in Theorem
1.7
. In order to keep things as simple as possible, we consider random hypersurfaces (
d\geqslant 2
and
k=1
) in
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}=\Omega
with
\mathcal{R}=(0,+\infty)
. We also focus on the case
p=2
, with
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. When considering cumulants of order
p\geqslant 3
, the main additional difficulty is that we have to deal with some intricate combinatorics. Part of it comes from the combinatorics of cumulants. Another part comes from the fact that we consider the integral of some function on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{p}
, and we need to keep track of how its arguments
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}
are clustered together in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Apart from these combinatorics, understanding the case
p=2
is enough to understand our strategy of proof.

Let us consider the setting of Theorem
1.7
in this case. We want to compute the large
R
asymptotics of
\kappa_{2}(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
, which is the covariance of the random variables
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{2}\rangle
, defined by (
1.3
). As usual in this business, the first step of the proof is to apply the Kac–Rice formula for moments and derive an integral expression for
\kappa_{2}(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
. We obtain that

\kappa_{2}(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\frac{1}{R^{2d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}\left(\frac{x}{R}\right)\phi_{2}\left(\frac{y}{R}\right)\mathcal{F}_{2}\left(f_{R},x,y\right)\dmesure\!x\dmesure\!y.

(1.13)

Here, the density
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f_{R},\cdot)
is defined by
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f_{R},x,y)=\rho_{2}(f_{R},x,y)-\rho_{1}(f_{R},x)\rho_{1}(f_{R},y)
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\rho_{1}(f_{R},\cdot)
and
\rho_{2}(f_{R},\cdot)
are the first two Kac–Rice densities associated with
f_{R}
. Under our assumptions, at least for
R
large enough,
\rho_{1}(f_{R},\cdot)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\rho_{2}(f_{R},\cdot)
is continuous outside of the diagonal
{{\boxslash}}=\{(x,x)\mid x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
but admits a pole along
{{\boxslash}}
.

Model case.

The next step is to understand the asymptotics of (
1.13
) in the model case where
f_{R}=f
for all
R>0
. By stationarity of
f
and a change of variable, we can rewrite (
1.13
) as:

R^{d}\kappa_{2}(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}\left(x\right)\phi_{2}\left(x+\frac{z}{R}\right)\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,z)\dmesure\!x\dmesure\!z.

We want to prove that this quantity converges to
\gamma_{2}(f)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)\dmesure\!x
as
R\to+\infty
, where
\gamma_{2}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,z)\dmesure\!z
. The difficulty here is to prove that
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,\cdot)
is integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. First, this function is locally integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Indeed, the stationarity of
f
implies that
\rho_{1}(f,\cdot)
equals some constant
\gamma_{1}(f)>0
, while it is known that
\rho_{2}(f,\cdot)
is locally integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see
[
4
,
23
]
.

It remains to prove the integrability of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,\cdot)
at infinity. In order to do this, we factor
\mathcal{F}_{2}
through a space of symmetric matrices. For all
(x,y)\notin{{\boxslash}}
, the value of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,x,y)
only depends on the variance matrix
\Sigma(f,x,y)
of
\left(f(x),\nabla_{x}f,f(y),\nabla_{y}f\right)
, which is positive thanks to (
ND
(
3
)
). Thus, there exists a function
F_{2}
on the open cone of positive symmetric matrices of size
2(d+1)
such that
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,x,y)=F_{2}\left(\Sigma(f,x,y)\right)
for all
(x,y)\notin{{\boxslash}}
. A matrix in the domain of
F_{2}
can be written by blocks as
\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{x}&\Sigma_{xy}\\
\,\prescript{\text{t}}{}{\!\Sigma_{xy}}&\Sigma_{y}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where the blocks are square of size
d+1
. We prove that, if
\Sigma_{xy}=0
, then
F_{2}(\Sigma)=0
and some part of the differential of
F_{2}
at
\Sigma
also vanishes. Since
F_{2}
is actually smooth, a Taylor expansion shows that
\left\lvert F_{2}(\Sigma)\right\rvert
is controlled by
\lVert\Sigma_{xy}\rVert^{2}
, uniformly on compact sets. Thus,
\lvert\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,x,y)\rvert
is controlled by the square norm of the off-diagonal blocks of
\Sigma(f,x,y)
. The corresponding coefficients are partial derivatives of
r
evaluated on
(x,y)
, hence they are controlled by
g(y-x)
. Finally,
\left\lvert\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,z)\right\rvert
is controlled by
g(z)^{2}
, say uniformly on the domain
\lVert z\rVert\geqslant 1
. Since we assumed (
DC
(
3,2
)
), this proves the integrability of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,0,\cdot)
at infinity.

General case.

Now that we understand the model case, we have to explain why replacing
f_{R}
by the limit field
f
in (
1.13
) only contributes an error term going to
0
as
R\to+\infty
. For this, we split the integral in two parts: the integral over
{{\boxslash}}_{1}=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lVert y-x\rVert\leqslant 1\right\}
and the integral over its complement.

•

On
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus{{\boxslash}}_{1}
, we prove that the covariance matrix
\Sigma(f_{R},x,y)
converges to
\Sigma\left(f,x,y\right)
, uniformly with respect to
(x,y)\notin{{\boxslash}}_{1}
. Then, the regularity of
F_{2}
and the same estimates as above allow us to prove that replacing
f_{R}
by
f
in the integral over
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus{{\boxslash}}_{1}
only contributes an error term.

•

On
{{\boxslash}}_{1}
, the situation is trickier, mostly because the pole of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
along the diagonal plays havoc with our estimates. In order to deal with the singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,\cdot)
along the diagonal, we use the Kergin interpolant of
f
at
x
and
y
, which is a multivariate polynomial that interpolates both
f
and
\nabla f
at
x
and
y
. For any
(x,y)\notin{{\boxslash}}
, this Kergin interpolant defines a centered Gaussian vector in the space
\mathbb{R}_{3}[X]
of polynomials of degree at most
3
in
d
variables, whose variance operator is denoted by
\Sigma^{\prime}(f,x,y)
. We can recover
\left(f(x),\nabla_{x}f,f(y),\nabla_{y}f\right)
from the Kergin interpolant of
f
and some geometric quantities depending only on
(x,y)
. We leverage this fact to write
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,x,y)
as
\Upsilon_{2}(x,y)F_{2}^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{\prime}(f,x,y)\right)
, where the function
\Upsilon_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is locally integrable, continuous outside of
{{\boxslash}}
and singular along
{{\boxslash}}
, and
F_{2}^{\prime}
is a smooth map on the set of positive symmetric operator on
\mathbb{R}_{3}[X]
. Formally,
F_{2}^{\prime}
also depends on
(x,y)
through some geometric parameters but, since we are only sketching the proof, let us sweep this under the rug. The function
\Upsilon_{2}
is the universal part of the Kac–Rice density on
{{\boxslash}}_{1}
: it does not depend on the field, and it fully accounts for the pole of
\mathcal{F}_{2}(f,\cdot)
along
{{\boxslash}}
. On the other hand, the distribution of the Kergin interpolant of
f
at
x
and
y
does not degenerate as
x
and
y
approach one another. Thus, we can deal with
F_{2}^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{\prime}(f_{R},x,y)\right)
in the same way we dealt with
F_{2}\left(\Sigma(f_{R},x,y)\right)
previously. We prove that it converges to
F_{2}^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{\prime}(f,x,y)\right)
uniformly with respect to
(x,y)\in{{\boxslash}}_{1}
, and this is enough to prove that replacing
f_{R}
by
f
in (
1.13
) only contributes an error term. This completes our sketch of the proof that
R^{d}\kappa_{2}(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{2}(f)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)\dmesure\!x
.

1.7
Organization of the paper

Section
2
introduces combinatorial tools that are used throughout the paper. In particular, we recall the definition of the cumulants of a family of random variables in that section. Section
3
is concerned with Kergin interpolation, and more precisely with describing the distribution of the Kergin interpolant of a Gaussian field. We discuss various forms of the Kac–Rice formulas in Section
4
, and explain how to write the corresponding densities as functions on a space of symmetric operators. In Section
5
, we prove a key estimate for the Kac–Rice densities for cumulants. Section
6
introduces a family of finite graphs that allows us to rewrite the estimate of Section
5
more nicely. In Section
7
, we discuss Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities, and the estimates they yield for the Kac–Rice densities. Section
8
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
1.7
. In Section
9
, we explain briefly how to adapt the content of the previous sections to deal with the case of gradient fields and prove Theorem
1.8
. Finally, Section
10
contains the proofs of Theorem
1.6
and of the limit theorems stated in Section
1.3
.

2
Partitions, cumulants and diagonals

The purpose of this section is to introduce notation, mostly related with partitions, that will appear in many places in the paper. We also recall some basic properties of the objects we introduce. Section
2.1
is concerned with partitions of a finite set and the partial order on the set of these partitions. In Section
2.2
, we recall the definition and some properties of the cumulants of a family of random variables. Finally, Section
2.3
introduces notation for strata of the diagonal in a product space and Section
2.4
studies a thick version of these strata.

2.1
Partitions of a finite set

In this section we first introduce notation related with finite sets and their partitions. Then, we recall some useful facts about the canonical partial order on a set of partitions.

We denote by
\mathbb{N}
(resp.
\mathbb{N}^{*}
) the set of non-negative (resp. positive) integers. Given
m
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m\leqslant n
, we denote by
\llbracket m,n\rrbracket=[m,n]\cap\mathbb{N}
the associated integer interval.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, we denote either by
\card(A)
or by
\lvert A\rvert
its cardinality. We also introduce the following notation for the set of unordered pairs of elements of
A
.

Definition 2.1

(Set of pairs)
.

We denote by
A^{\curlywedge 2}=\left\{\{a,b\}\subset A\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\neq b\right\}
.

This set naturally indexes coefficients above the diagonal in a symmetric matrix whose rows and columns are indexed by
A
. It is also related to the set of edges of a graph with vertex set
A
.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
B
and
C
be non-empty disjoint subsets of
A
. The map
(b,c)\mapsto\{b,c\}
is a canonical injection of
B\times C
into
A^{\curlywedge 2}
. Using this injection, we often consider
B\times C
as a subset of
A^{\curlywedge 2}
in the following.

Next, we introduce the set of partitions of
A
and its partial order.

Definition 2.3

(Partition)
.

A
partition
of
A
is a family
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{m}\}
of non-empty disjoint subsets such that
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}I_{i}=A
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{p}
) the set of partitions of
A
(resp.
\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
).

Definition 2.4

(Blocks and induced partition)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a partition of
A
, let
a\in A
and
B\subset A
.

•

An element
I\in\mathcal{I}
is called a
block
of
\mathcal{I}
.

•

We denote by
[a]_{\mathcal{I}}
the only block of
\mathcal{I}
that contains
a
.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{I}_{B}=\left\{I\cap B\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\in\mathcal{I}\right\}\setminus\left\{\emptyset\right\}
the partition of
B

induced
by
\mathcal{I}
.

Definition 2.5

(Partial order on partitions)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
if for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
there exists
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
J\subset I
. In this case, we say that
\mathcal{J}
is
finer
than
\mathcal{I}
, or that
\mathcal{J}

refines

\mathcal{I}
, or that
\mathcal{I}
is
coarser
than
\mathcal{J}
. We denote by
\mathcal{J}<\mathcal{I}
the fact that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\neq\mathcal{I}
.

It is a classical fact that
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A},\leqslant\right)
is a partially ordered set, and even a lattice (see
[
50
, Sect. 2.2]
). In particular it admits a maximum, equal to
\{A\}
, and a minimum, equal to
\left\{\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
. Moreover, any two partitions of
A
have a coarsest common refinement, called their meet.

Definition 2.6

(Meet)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote by
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}
the coarsest element of
\mathcal{P}_{A}
that refines both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
. The partition
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}
is called the
meet
of
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
and is described by:

\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}=\left\{I\cap J\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\in\mathcal{I},J\in\mathcal{J}\right\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}=\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{I}_{J}=\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{J}_{I}.

More generally, let
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{I}_{m}\right\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote the coarsest partition refining all the elements of
\mathcal{Q}
, by
\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{I}_{i}=\left\{\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}I_{i}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,I_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}\right\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
. Accordingly, we denote
\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}=\left\{\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
for the minimum of
\mathcal{P}_{A}
.

We will need to consider the set obtained by forming the disjoint union of two copies of
A
. The notation we use is the following.

Definition 2.7

(Doubled sets and partitions)
.

For all
B\subset A
, we denote by
2B=\{0,1\}\times B
. Given
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote by
2\mathcal{I}=\left\{2I\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\in\mathcal{I}\right\}
, which is a partition of
2A
.

Note that, if
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{2A}
is such that
[(0,a)]_{\mathcal{J}}=[(1,a)]_{\mathcal{J}}
for all
a\in A
, then there exists a unique
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{J}=2\mathcal{I}
.

We conclude this section by expliciting two bijections. They will be useful in computations to re-index sums indexed by sets of partitions.

Lemma 2.8

(Two bijections)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, then the following two maps are bijections:

1.

the map
\mathcal{J}\mapsto\left\{\mathcal{I}_{J}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}J\in\mathcal{J}\right\}
from
\left\{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}\right\}
to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
;

2.

the map
\mathcal{J}\mapsto\left(\mathcal{J}_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
from
\left\{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\right\}
to
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{P}_{I}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be coarser than
\mathcal{I}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{J}=\left\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\subset J\right\}\subset\penalty\ \mathcal{I}
for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
, see Definition
2.4
. Moreover
\mathcal{I}=\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{I}_{J}
, so that the map in Item
1
is well-defined. Given
K\in\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we have
K\subset\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
so that
\bigsqcup_{I\in K}I\subset A
is a union of blocks of
\mathcal{I}
. Hence,

\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\longmapsto\left\{\bigsqcup_{I\in K}I\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}K\in\mathcal{K}\right\}\in\left\{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}\right\}

is a well-defined map. One can check that it is the converse map of the one in Item
1
, which proves that both maps are bijections.

If
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, then
\mathcal{J}_{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{I}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, by definition. Hence, the map from Item
2
is well-defined. The converse map is
\left(\mathcal{K}_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mapsto\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{K}_{I}
, which proves that it is bijective.
∎

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, there exists a unique
J\in\mathcal{J}
such that
I\subset J
. The partition
\mathcal{J}
corresponds to some
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
through the bijection of Lemma
2.8
.
1
. The block
J
then corresponds to
[I]_{\mathcal{K}}
, in the sense that
J=\bigsqcup_{K\in[I]_{\mathcal{K}}}K
. This shows that the following is consistent with Definition
2.4
and Lemma
2.8
.
1
.

Definition 2.9

(Block with respect to a coarser partition)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we denote be
[I]_{\mathcal{J}}\in\mathcal{J}
the only block of
\mathcal{J}
that contains
I
.

2.2
Cumulants

In this section, we recall how cumulants of random variables are defined from their moments, as well as some of their properties.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\emptyset\neq B\subset A
, we denote by
\mu_{\mathcal{J}}=(-1)^{\lvert\mathcal{J}\rvert-1}\left(\lvert\mathcal{J}\rvert-1\right)!
for all
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
. Let us consider two sequences
\left(m_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
and
\left(\kappa_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
of numbers, indexed by non-empty subsets of
A
. The following result relies on the theory of Möbius functions. We will not go into the details of this theory, and we refer the interested reader to
[
50
, Sect. 2.5]
.

Proposition 2.10

(Möbius inversion)
.

The following two statements are equivalent:

1.

for all non-empty
B\subset A
, we have
\kappa_{B}=\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}m_{J}
;

2.

for all non-empty
B\subset A
, we have
m_{B}=\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa_{J}
.

Proof.

For all non-empty
B\subset A
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
we introduce the auxiliary notation
\widetilde{m}_{\mathcal{I}}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}m_{I}
and
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathcal{I}}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\kappa_{I}
. Let us assume that Statement
1
holds. For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
, we have:

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathcal{I}}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{\mathcal{J}_{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{I}}\left(\mu_{\mathcal{J}_{I}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{I}}m_{J}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}_{I}}\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}m_{J}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}_{I}}\right)\widetilde{m}_{\mathcal{J}},

where we used Lemma
2.8
.
2
to get the second equality. Thus, Statement
1
implies

\forall\ \emptyset\neq B\subset A,\ \forall\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{B},\qquad\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}_{I}}\right)\widetilde{m}_{\mathcal{J}}.

(2.1)

Actually, (
2.1
) is equivalent to Statement
1
since
\kappa_{B}=\widetilde{\kappa}_{\left\{B\right\}}=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\{B\}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}m_{J}
. Similarly, let us assume that Statement
2
holds. For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
, we have:

\widetilde{m}_{\mathcal{I}}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(\sum_{\mathcal{J}_{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{I}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{I}}\kappa_{J}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa_{J}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathcal{J}},

where we used Lemma
2.8
.
2
to get the second equality. Hence, Statement
2
is equivalent to:

\forall\ \emptyset\neq B\subset A,\ \forall\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{B},\qquad\widetilde{m}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathcal{J}},

(2.2)

the converse implication following from
m_{B}=\widetilde{m}_{\left\{B\right\}}=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\{B\}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa_{J}
.

To conclude the proof, we need to check that (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) are equivalent. This is done in
[
50
, pp. 19–20]
, where it is stated as the equivalence between (2.5.25) and (2.5.26), using the coefficients defined by (2.5.22).
∎

Lemma 2.11

(Cancellation property)
.

Let
\left(m_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
be a sequence of numbers. Let us assume that there exists
C\subset A
such that
\emptyset\neq C\neq A
; and
m_{B}=m_{B\cap C}m_{B\setminus C}
for all
B\subset A
, with the convention that
m_{\emptyset}=1
. Then, we have
\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}m_{J}=0
.

Proof.

Denoting by
\kappa_{B}=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}m_{J}
for all
B\subset A
, the sequences
\left(m_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
and
\left(\kappa_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
satisfy the equivalent conditions in Proposition
2.10
. We want to prove that
\kappa_{A}=0
.

The proof is a computation involving Möbius inversion, for which we refer to the proof of
[
55
, Prop. 4.1]
. Note that, in
[
55
]
, the author considers the moments and cumulants of a family of random variables. However, the computation we are interested in only uses: the fact that moments and cumulants satisfy the polynomial relations in Proposition
2.10
, and a multiplicativity property for moments of independent random variables similar to our hypothesis. Thus, it extends verbatim to our setting.
∎

We can now focus on the case where the sequences
\left(m_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
and
\left(\kappa_{B}\right)_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
are respectively the moments and cumulants of a family of random variables. We start by defining these quantities, see
[
50
, Sect. 3.2]
for example.

Definition 2.12

(Moments and cumulants)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let
(Y_{a})_{a\in A}
be a family of
L^{p}
random variables, we denote their
joint moment
and
joint cumulant
respectively by:

\displaystyle m\left((Y_{a})_{a\in A}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{a\in A}Y_{a}\right]

and

\displaystyle\kappa\left((Y_{a})_{a\in A}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J}Y_{j}\right].

If
Y
is an
L^{p}
random variable, we denote its
p
-th moment
and
p
-th cumulant
respectively by:

\displaystyle m_{p}(Y)=m(\underbrace{Y,\dots,Y}_{p\ \text{times}})=\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{p}\right]

and

\displaystyle\kappa_{p}(Y)=\kappa(\underbrace{Y,\dots,Y}_{p\ \text{times}})=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\lvert J\rvert}\right].

Example 2.13

(Cumulants of Gaussian variables)
.

Let
Y\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda\right)
be a centered Gaussian variable in
\mathbb{R}
of variance
\lambda\geqslant 0
. By Definition
2.12
, we have
\kappa_{1}(Y)=0
and
\kappa_{2}(Y)=\lambda
. A generating function argument shows that
\kappa_{p}(Y)=0
for all
p\geqslant 3
, see
[
50
, Sect. 3.1]
for example.

Let
(Y_{a})_{a\in A}
be a family of random variables at least of class
L^{p}
, where
p=\card(A)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Setting
m_{B}=m\left((Y_{b})_{b\in B}\right)
and
\kappa_{B}=\kappa\left((Y_{b})_{b\in B}\right)
for all non-empty
B\subset A
, we get two sequences of the form studied above. We thus obtain the following.

Corollary 2.14
.

For all non-empty
B\subset A
, we have
m\left((Y_{b})_{b\in B}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa\left((Y_{j})_{j\in J}\right)
. In particular, if
Y
is an
L^{p}
random variable then
m_{p}(Y)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa_{\lvert J\rvert}(Y)
.

Proof.

The sequences
(m_{B})_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
and
(\kappa_{B})_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
were defined so that the first condition in Proposition
2.10
is satisfied. Hence so is the second one.
∎

We conclude this section by recalling the method of cumulants, which is a classical tool to prove Central Limit Theorems.

Proposition 2.15

(Method of cumulants)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset(0,+\infty)
be an unbounded set,
(Y_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
be a family of real random variables and
\lambda\geqslant 0
. For all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume that
Y_{R}
is
L^{p}
for all
R
large enough (possibly depending on
p
) and that

\kappa_{p}(Y_{R})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\kappa_{p}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda\right)\right)=\begin{cases}\lambda,&\text{if}\ p=2;\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
Y_{R}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda\right)
in distribution.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. By Corollary
2.14
, the
p
-th moment of a random variable is expressed as a universal polynomial in its cumulants of order at most
p
. Thus, our hypothesis implies that
m_{p}(Y_{R})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}m_{p}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda\right)\right)
, where the left-hand side is well-defined for
R
large enough.

The conclusion follows by the standard method of moments
[
15
, Ex. 30.1 and Thm. 30.2]
. Note that, in
[
15
]
, the author assumes that
Y_{R}
has a finite
p
-th moment for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. However, his proof only uses the fact that
Y_{R}
has a finite
p
-th moment for
R
large enough, possibly depending on
p
. Also, he only deals with the case
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{N}^{*}
, but this implies the general case.
∎

2.3
Product spaces and diagonals

In this section, we introduce notation concerning diagonals in product spaces, that will be used throughout the paper. We also recall the relation between the power measure and the factorial power measure of a submanifold in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see Lemma
2.25
below.

Definition 2.16

(Set-indexed products)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
Z
be any set.

•

We denote by
Z^{A}
the Cartesian product of
\card(A)
copies of
Z
indexed by
A
.

•

A generic element in
Z^{A}
is denoted by
\underline{x}=\left(x_{a}\right)_{a\in A}
.

•

For all
\emptyset\neq B\subset A
, we denote by
\underline{x}_{B}=\left(x_{b}\right)_{b\in B}
the projection of
\underline{x}\in Z^{A}
onto
Z^{B}
.

•

Recalling that
2B=\{0,1\}\times B
, we denote by
\underline{x}_{2B}=\left(x_{b}\right)_{(i,b)\in 2B}\in Z^{2B}
.

•

Let
\left(\phi_{a}\right)_{a\in A}
be functions from
Z
to
\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\phi_{A}^{\otimes}:\underline{x}\mapsto\prod_{a\in A}\phi_{a}(x_{a})
from
Z^{A}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 2.17
.

The point
\underline{x}_{2B}
corresponds to
\left(\underline{x}_{B},\underline{x}_{B}\right)
under the canonical bijection
Z^{2B}\simeq Z^{B}\times Z^{B}
. In the following, we identify
Z^{2B}
with
Z^{B}\times Z^{B}
through this bijection and write
\underline{x}_{2B}=(\underline{x}_{B},\underline{x}_{B})
.

Recalling the notation introduced in Section
2.1
, we define the strata of the diagonal in
Z^{A}
.

Definition 2.18

(Diagonals)
.

We denote the
large diagonal
in
Z^{A}
by

{{\boxslash}}=\left\{(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in Z^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\exists a,b\in A\ \text{such that}\ a\neq b\ \text{and}\ x_{a}=x_{b}\right\}.

For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote by
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}
the
stratum
of
{{\boxslash}}
where multiplicities are described by
\mathcal{I}
:

{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}=\left\{(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in Z^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall a,b\in A,\left(x_{a}=x_{b}\iff[a]_{\mathcal{I}}=[b]_{\mathcal{I}}\right)\right\}.

The sets
Z
and
A
will always be clear from the context. With this notation, we have:

\displaystyle Z^{A}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}

and

\displaystyle{{\boxslash}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{I}\,>\,\bigwedge\!\mathcal{P}_{A}}{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}

(2.3)

Remark 2.19
.

When
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}=\left\{\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
, the notation
{{\boxslash}}_{(\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A})}
is misleading, since it denotes the complement of
{{\boxslash}}
in
Z^{A}
. Therefore, we avoid this notation and use
Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
instead.

Definition 2.20

(Diagonal inclusions)
.

For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we denote by
\iota_{\mathcal{I}}:Z^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}\subset Z^{A}
the bijection defined by
\iota_{\mathcal{I}}:\left(y_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mapsto\left(y_{[a]_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)_{a\in A}
.

Let us now focus on the case where
Z
is a submanifold of dimension
n
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
restricts into a Riemannian metric on
Z
, and thus induces a canonical volume measure
\nu
on
Z
, which coincides with its
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. This gives rise to natural measures on
Z^{A}
.

Definition 2.21

(Power measure and factorial power measure)
.

For any non-empty finite set
A
, we denote by
\nu^{A}
the product measure
\nu\otimes\dots\otimes\nu
on
Z^{A}
, and by
\nu^{[A]}
its restriction to
Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
.

Remark 2.22
.

In this setting,
Z^{A}
(resp.
Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
) is a submanifold of dimension
n\lvert A\rvert
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, and
\nu^{A}
(resp.
\nu^{[A]}
) is its canonical volume measure induced by the Euclidean metric on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
.

Example 2.23
.

If
n=0
then
Z
is a discrete closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu=\sum_{x\in Z}\delta_{x}
, where
\delta_{x}
stands for the unit Dirac mass at
x
. The product
Z^{A}
is also
0
-dimensional and we have:

\displaystyle\nu^{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underline{x}\in Z^{A}}\delta_{\underline{x}}

and

\displaystyle\nu^{[A]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underline{x}\in Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}}\delta_{\underline{x}}.

The power measure
\nu^{A}
is the one that appears when we consider a product of linear statistics of
Z
. Indeed,
\prod_{a\in A}\langle\nu\,,\phi_{a}\rangle=\left\langle\nu^{A}\,,\phi_{A}^{\otimes}\right\rangle
for any reasonable test-functions
(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
. The factorial power measure
\nu^{[A]}
is the one that can be dealt with using Kac–Rice formulas, see Section
4.1
. The next result relates these two measures. When
n>0
the relation is trivial, but if
n=0
some combinatorics are involved. The following notation will allow us to deal with both cases at once.

Definition 2.24

(Indexing set of partitions)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\mathcal{P}_{A,n}=\mathcal{P}_{A}
if
n=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{A,n}=\{\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}\}
if
n\geqslant 1
. If
A=\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
with
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we let
\mathcal{P}_{p,n}=\mathcal{P}_{A,n}
.

Lemma 2.25

(Power vs factorial power)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\nu
be the canonical volume measure of a
n
-dimensional submanifold
Z
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, we have
\nu^{A}=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,n}}(\iota_{\mathcal{I}})_{*}\nu^{[\mathcal{I}]}
.

Proof.

If
n=0
, by Equation (
2.3
), Definition
2.20
and Example
2.23
, we have:

\nu^{A}=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\sum_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\delta_{\underline{x}}=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\sum_{\underline{y}\in Z^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}}\delta_{\iota_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{y})}=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}(\iota_{\mathcal{I}})_{*}\left(\sum_{\underline{y}\in Z^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}}\delta_{\underline{y}}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,n}}(\iota_{\mathcal{I}})_{*}\nu^{[\mathcal{I}]}.

Recalling Definition
2.1
, if
n>0
the diagonal
{{\boxslash}}=\bigcup_{\{a,b\}\in A^{\curlywedge 2}}\left\{\underline{x}\in Z^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}x_{a}=x_{b}\right\}
is a finite union of submanifolds of positive codimension in
Z^{A}
. Hence, it is negligible for the Riemannian volume
\nu^{A}
of
Z^{A}
, and
\nu^{A}=\nu^{[A]}
. Here,
\iota_{(\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A})}:Z^{(\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A})}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
is a diffeomorphism, and an isometry. Indeed, it corresponds to the identity of
Z^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
under the canonical identification of
\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}=\left\{\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
with
A
. Hence,

\nu^{A}=\nu^{[A]}=\left(\iota_{(\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A})}\right)_{*}\left(\nu^{[\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}]}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,n}}(\iota_{\mathcal{I}})_{*}\nu^{[\mathcal{I}]}.\qed

2.4
Thick diagonals
