In this section we define sets which can be thought of as thickenings of the strata of the diagonal. In a related context, sets of this kind were introduced in
[
5
, Def. 4.10]
and used in
[
2
,
3
]
and
[
25
]
. Here, we deviate a bit from these previous works in order to take into account some convexity issues.

Let
\left(V,\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle\right)
be a Euclidean space, that is a finite-dimensional vector space equipped with a Euclidean inner product. Let
\lVert\cdot\rVert
denote the Euclidean norm associated with
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
, we denote by
\dist(U,U^{\prime})=\inf\left\{\lVert u-u^{\prime}\rVert\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}u\in U,u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}\right\}
the distance between the sets
U
and
U^{\prime}\subset V
.

Definition 2.26

(Simplex)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, we denote the
standard simplex
in
\mathbb{R}^{A}
by
{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}=\left\{\underline{t}=(t_{a})\in[0,1]^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{a\in A}t_{a}=1\right\}
. If
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
for some
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
{{\blacktriangle}}_{q}={{\blacktriangle}}_{\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket}
denote the standard simplex of dimension
q
.

Definition 2.27

(Convex hull)
.

Given
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in V^{A}
and
\underline{t}=(t_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
, we denote by
\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}=\sum_{a\in A}t_{a}x_{a}\in V
. We denote the
convex hull
of
\left\{x_{a}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
by
\conv(\underline{x})=\left\{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}\right\}
.

Definition 2.28

(Clustering partitions)
.

Let
\eta\geqslant 0
, for all
\underline{x}\in V^{A}
, we denote by

\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})=\left\{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall I,J\in\mathcal{I}\ \text{such that}\ I\neq J\ \text{we have}\ \dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)>\eta\right\}.

Lemma 2.29

(Stability by meet)
.

For all
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
we have
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
. In particular,
\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
and is the finest element of
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
and let
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}
be distinct. There exist
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
and
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}
such that
K_{1}=I_{1}\cap J_{1}
and
K_{2}=I_{2}\cap J_{2}
, see Definition
2.6
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
I_{1}\neq I_{2}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{i}})\subset\conv(\underline{x}_{I_{i}})
. Hence, since
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
,

\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{2}})\right)\geqslant\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{I_{2}})\right)>\eta.

Thus,
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
. Then, since
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
is finite and stable by meet, we have
\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
.
∎

Example 2.30
.

If
V=\mathbb{R}
and
A=\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
, with
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=\left(-1,0,3,6\right)
and
\eta=2
, the elements of
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
are the following partitions of
\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
, the finest being the last one:

\displaystyle\left\{\{1,2,3,4\}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{\{1,2,3\},\{4\}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{\{1,2\},\{3,4\}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\left\{\{1,2\},\{3\},\{4\}\right\}.

Definition 2.31

(Thick diagonals)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\eta\geqslant 0
. For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
we define
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}=\left\{\underline{x}\in V^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})=\mathcal{I}\right\}
. Note that
V^{A}=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
.

Example 2.32
.

Let us give some examples.

•

If
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\bigwedge\!\mathcal{P}_{A},\eta}
, then
\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
and
\lVert x_{a}-x_{b}\rVert>\eta
for all
a\neq b\in A
. Conversely, if
\lVert x_{a}-x_{b}\rVert>\eta
for all
a\neq b\in A
, then
\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
which implies
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\bigwedge\!\mathcal{P}_{A},\eta}
. Thus,

{{\boxslash}}_{\bigwedge\!\mathcal{P}_{A},\eta}=\left\{\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in V^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall a,b\in A\ \text{such that}\ a\neq b,\ \text{we have}\ \lVert x_{a}-x_{b}\rVert>\eta\right\}.

•

Let us assume that
\eta=0
. Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and let
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}\subset V^{A}
, see Definition
2.18
. There exists a unique
\underline{y}=(y_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in V^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
such that
\underline{x}=\iota_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{y})
, see Definition
2.20
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
we have
\conv(\underline{x}_{I})=\{y_{I}\}
. Since
\underline{y}\in V^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we have
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
, and no proper refinement of
\mathcal{I}
belongs to
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
. Hence
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
, and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},0}
. Thus
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}\subset{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},0}
for any
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. By Equation (
2.3
) and Definition
2.31
, this implies that
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}}={{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},0}
for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
.

Remark 2.33
.

Let
\underline{x}\in V^{A}
. For all
B\subset A
, we have
\left(\underline{x}_{2A}\right)_{2B}=\underline{x}_{2B}=\left(\underline{x}_{B},\underline{x}_{B}\right)
, which implies that
\conv\left((\underline{x}_{2A})_{2B}\right)=\conv(\underline{x}_{B})
. Hence, given any
\eta\geqslant 0
, we have
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{2A})=\{2\mathcal{I}\mid\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\}
. Thus
\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{2A})=2\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, since
\mathcal{I}\mapsto 2\mathcal{I}
is increasing. That is
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
if and only if
\underline{x}_{2A}=(\underline{x},\underline{x})\in{{\boxslash}}_{2\mathcal{I},\eta}
. In particular, for all
\eta\geqslant 0
,

\left\{(\underline{x},\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in V^{A}\right\}=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}{{\boxslash}}_{2\mathcal{I},\eta}\subsetneq V^{2A}.

The following lemma partially describes the behavior of the thick diagonals from Definition
2.31
when the scale parameter
\eta
varies.

Lemma 2.34

(Monotonicity of thick diagonals)
.

Let
0\leqslant\delta\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant\eta
, for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we have:

\displaystyle{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}

\displaystyle\subset\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}}{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}

and

\displaystyle{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\subset{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}\in V^{A}
and let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
. By Definition
2.28
, for all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
such that
I\neq J
we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)>\eta\geqslant\delta
. Hence
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\underline{x})
. Thus
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\subset\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\underline{x})
, which implies that
\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\geqslant\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\underline{x})
.

Let us fix
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}
, we denote by
\mathcal{K}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
so that
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{K},\eta}
. Then we have
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\underline{x})\leqslant\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})=\mathcal{K}
. Thus,
\underline{x}\in\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}}{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}
, which proves the first inclusion.

If
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
then
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\underline{x})\leqslant\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}(\underline{x})\leqslant\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})=\mathcal{I}
. Hence
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}(\underline{x})=\mathcal{I}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\varepsilon}
. This proves the second inclusion.
∎

Lemma 2.35

(Projection of thick diagonals)
.

Let
\eta\geqslant 0
and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
. Then, for all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
, we have
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J},\eta}
.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
and
J\in\mathcal{J}
. Since
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{J}=\left\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\subset J\right\}
. Let
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}_{J}\subset\mathcal{I}
be such that
I_{1}\neq I_{2}
. Since
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{I_{2}})\right)>\eta
. Hence,
\mathcal{I}_{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
and
\mathcal{I}_{J}\geqslant\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
.

We prove that
\mathcal{I}_{J}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
by contradiction. If
\mathcal{I}_{J}>\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})\in\mathcal{P}_{J}
we define a partition
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{J})\sqcup\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, which is such that
\mathcal{K}<\mathcal{I}
. Let
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
be two distinct blocks of
\mathcal{K}
. If
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{J}
, then
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{2}})\right)>\eta
because
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
. If
K_{1},K_{2}\in\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
, then
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{2}})\right)>\eta
. If
K_{1}\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{J}
and
K_{2}\in\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
, there exists
I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}
such that
K_{2}\subset I
. Since
\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{2}})\subset\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
, we have once again
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{1}}),\conv(\underline{x}_{K_{2}})\right)>\eta
. Hence
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, which contradicts
\mathcal{K}<\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
.

Finally, we have proved that
\mathcal{I}_{J}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}_{J})
. That is,
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J},\eta}
.
∎

Lemma 2.36

(Closedness of the deepest stratum)
.

For all
\eta\geqslant 0
, the set
{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is closed in
V^{A}
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\geqslant 0
and let
\underline{x}\in V^{A}
be the limit of a sequence
\left(\underline{x}^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of points of
{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
. Our goal is to prove that
\{A\}
is the only element of
\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, see Definition
2.28
, which will imply that
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
and prove the result.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, for all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
such that
I\neq J
, we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)>\eta
. Hence there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\min_{I\neq J}\left(\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)-\eta\right)
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be large enough that
\lVert x_{a}-x_{a}^{(n)}\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
for all
a\in A
. Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
be such that
I\neq J
, let
\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{I}
and
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{J}
. We have:

\displaystyle\eta\leqslant\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\left(\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}-\underline{x}_{I}\right)\right\rVert+\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\underline{t}\cdot\left(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}-\underline{x}_{J}\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\varepsilon+\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}\right\rVert

so that
\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}\right\rVert\geqslant\eta+2\varepsilon
. We obtain
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right)\geqslant\eta+2\varepsilon
by taking the infimum over
\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{I}
and
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{J}
. Hence
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x}^{(n)})
, and
\mathcal{I}=\{A\}
since
\underline{x}^{(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
.
∎

We conclude this section by proving that if
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, then the components indexed by elements of a given block
I\in\mathcal{I}
cannot be too far from one another.

Lemma 2.37

(Clusters diameter)
.

Let
\eta\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, for all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
the following hold.

•

For all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
such that
I\neq J
we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)>\eta
.

•

For all
a,b\in A
such that
[a]_{\mathcal{I}}=[b]_{\mathcal{I}}
we have
\lVert x_{a}-x_{b}\rVert\leqslant\eta\card(A)
.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
. By Lemma
2.29
, we have
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
, which proves the first point, see Definitions
2.28
and
2.31
.

Let
a
and
b\in A
and let us assume that
\lVert x_{a}-x_{b}\rVert>\eta\card(A)\geqslant 0
. We will prove that
[a]_{\mathcal{I}}\neq[b]_{\mathcal{I}}
, which will yield the second point by contraposition. Let
\pi:x\mapsto\left\langle x-x_{a}\,,\frac{x_{b}-x_{a}}{\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert}\right\rangle
. Geometrically,
\pi
is defined by projecting orthogonally onto the line through
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
, then identifying this line with
\mathbb{R}
using the isometry sending
x_{a}
to
0
and
x_{b}
to
\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert
. The interval
\left[0,\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert\right]
has length
\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert>\eta\card(A)
and it contains at most
\card(A)
points of the form
\pi(x_{c})
with
c\in A
, among which its endpoints. Hence, there exists
t>0=\pi(x_{a})
such that
t+\eta<\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert=\pi(x_{b})
and
\left\{\pi(x_{c})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}c\in A\right\}\cap[t,t+\eta]=\emptyset
.

Let
J=\left\{c\in A\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\pi(x_{c})<t\right\}
and
\mathcal{J}=\{J,A\setminus J\}
. Note that
a\in J
and
b\in A\setminus J
so that
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. We have
A\setminus J=\left\{c\in A\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\pi(x_{c})\geqslant t\right\}=\left\{c\in A\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\pi(x_{c})>t+\eta\right\}
, thanks to our choice of
t
. Since
\pi
is affine, for all
x\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})
and
y\in\conv(\underline{x}_{A\setminus J})
we have
\pi(x)<t
and
\pi(y)>t+\eta
, hence

\eta<\pi(y)-\pi(x)=\left\langle y-x\,,\frac{x_{b}-x_{a}}{\lVert x_{b}-x_{a}\rVert}\right\rangle\leqslant\lVert y-x\rVert.

By compactness of the convex hulls we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{J}),\conv(\underline{x}_{A\setminus J})\right)>\eta
, i.e.
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})
. Thus, we have
\mathcal{I}=\bigwedge\mathcal{Q}_{\eta}(\underline{x})\leqslant\mathcal{J}
. Since
[a]_{\mathcal{J}}=J\neq(A\setminus J)=[b]_{\mathcal{J}}
, we have
[a]_{\mathcal{I}}\neq[b]_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

3
Polynomial interpolation and Gaussian fields

The goal of this section is to introduce the Kergin interpolant of a Gaussian field and to prove some of its properties. In Section
3.1
, we recall the definition of Kergin interpolation for a deterministic function, which relies on generalized divided differences. In Section
3.2
, we relate the covariance structure of a Gaussian field to that of its divided differences, that are themselves Gaussian fields. In Section
3.3
, we introduce a twisted Kergin interpolant, whose distribution is invariant by diagonal translations if the original field is stationary. Finally, Section
3.4
is dedicated to proving uniform estimates on the variance operator of the Kergin interpolant associated with a Gaussian field.

3.1
Divided differences and Kergin interpolation

In the following, given two open subsets
U
and
U^{\prime}
in some Banach spaces and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,U^{\prime})
the space of maps of class
\mathcal{C}^{q}
from
U
to
U^{\prime}
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open set. Given
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
we denote by
D^{q}_{x}f
its
q
-th differential at
x\in U
, which belongs to the space
\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
of symmetric
q
-linear maps from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set. Recall that
{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}
stands for the standard simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{A}
. We denote by
\varsigma_{A}
the Lebesgue measure on the hyperplane
\left\{(t_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{a\in A}t_{a}=1\right\}
, normalized so that
\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}}\dmesure\!\varsigma_{A}(\underline{t})=\frac{1}{\lvert A\rvert!}
.

Definition 3.1

(Divided differences)
.

Let
A
be a finite set of cardinality
q+1
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, we define the
divided difference
of
f
at
\underline{x}
by:

f[\underline{x}]=\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}}D^{q}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}}f\ \dmesure\!\varsigma_{A}(\underline{t})\in\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k}).

Remark 3.2
.

The divided difference
f[\underline{x}]
is the integral over
\conv(\underline{x})\subset U
(see Definition
2.27
) of
D^{q}f
, with respect to the push-forward of
\varsigma_{A}
by the standard parametrization
\underline{t}\mapsto\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}
. Since
\varsigma_{A}
is invariant under permutation of the vertices of
{{\blacktriangle}}_{A}
, the quantity
f[\underline{x}]
is invariant under permutation of the components of
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}
.

Example 3.3
.

Let us give two examples when
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. The second one explains the terminology “divided differences”.

•

If
x_{0}=\dots=x_{q}=y\in U
then
f[x_{0},\dots,x_{q}]=\frac{1}{q!}D^{q}_{y}f
.

•

If
d=1
then
\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{k}
canonically and, for any
\underline{x}\in U^{q+1}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we can compute
f[x_{0},\dots,x_{q}]=\frac{f[x_{1},\dots,x_{q}]-f[x_{0},\dots,x_{q-1}]}{x_{q}-x_{0}}
recursively from the values of
\left(f(x_{i})\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}
.

We denote by
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{d})
and by
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
the space of polynomials of degree at most
q
in
d
variables. The
Kergin polynomial
of a
\mathcal{C}^{q}
function
f
at
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
is the only polynomial in
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
interpolating the divided differences of
f
on all
(\underline{x}_{B})_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
, cf.
[
32
]
. Interpolating component-wise we get the following, where tuples in
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
are understood as polynomial maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Theorem 3.4

(Kergin interpolation)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{q})\in U^{q+1}
, there exists a unique
K(f,\underline{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
such that
K(f,\underline{x})[\underline{x}_{B}]=f[\underline{x}_{B}]
for all non-empty
B\subset\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. In particular, if
y\in U
appears with multiplicity at least
l+1
in
\underline{x}
then
D^{l}_{y}\left(K(f,\underline{x})\right)=D^{l}_{y}f
. Moreover,

K(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{i=0}^{q}f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}](X-x_{0},\dots,X-x_{i-1}).

(3.1)

Proof.

We apply
[
17
, Thm. 12.5]
with
m=0
to each component of
f
, see also
[
45
,
44
]
. This yields existence and uniqueness of
K(f,\underline{x})
as well as the claimed expression. If
y\in U
appears with multiplicity at least
l+1
in
\underline{x}
, let
B\subset\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
be such that
\lvert B\rvert=l+1
and
x_{i}=y
for all
i\in B
. Then, by Example
3.3
, we have
D^{l}_{y}\left(K(f,\underline{x})\right)=l!\left(K(f,\underline{x})[\underline{x}_{B}]\right)=l!f[\underline{x}_{B}]=D^{l}_{y}f
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

When
k=1
, we recover known interpolating polynomials as particular cases.

•

If
x_{0}=\dots=x_{q}=y\in U
then
K(f,\underline{x})
is the Taylor polynomial of degree
q
of
f
at
y
.

•

If
d=1
then
K(f,\underline{x})
is the Hermite interpolating polynomial of
f
at
x_{0},\dots,x_{q}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 3.6
.

The polynomial map
K(f,\underline{x})
does not depend on an ordering of
(x_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}
. Indeed, it is defined by interpolating a family of conditions which is invariant under permutation of
\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, see Remark
3.2
. This allows to make sense of
K(f,\underline{x})
for any
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, where
\card(A)=q+1
. Note however that each term of the sum in Equation (
3.1
) depends on the ordering of
(x_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}
.

The previous theorem is part of the standard knowledge concerning Kergin’s interpolation. From this point on, we will analyze how the Kergin interpolant behaves, with respect to partitioning the set of interpolating points. The next lemma shows that the family of Kergin interpolants on well-separated sets of points defines a surjective map. Recall that, given
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we defined
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})\subset\mathcal{P}_{A}
in Definition
2.28
.

Lemma 3.7

(Surjectivity of block-wise interpolation)
.

Let
A\neq\emptyset
be finite, let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
. The linear map
P\mapsto\left(K(P,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
is surjective from
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
, the convex hulls
\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
are disjoint compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Thus, for any
I\in\mathcal{I}
, there exists a cut-off function
\chi_{I}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
such that
\chi_{I}=1
on a neighborhood of
\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
and
\chi_{I}=0
on a neighborhood of
\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\{I\}}\conv(\underline{x}_{J})
.

Let
(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, we define
f=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\chi_{I}P_{I}
and
P=K(f,\underline{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
, for all non-empty
J\subset I
, we have:

P[\underline{x}_{J}]=K(f,\underline{x})[\underline{x}_{J}]=f[\underline{x}_{J}]=P_{I}[\underline{x}_{J}]

because
f=P_{I}
on a neighborhood of
\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\subset\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
, see Definition
3.1
. The uniqueness part in Theorem
3.4
shows that
K(P,\underline{x}_{I})=P_{I}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Hence the result.
∎

In the case where each geometrical point appears with multiplicity exactly
2
in
\underline{x}
, the previous result shows that we can interpolate the
1
-jets of any
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function on
q
pairwise distinct points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by a polynomial of degree at most
2q-1
. In the following, given vector spaces
V
and
W
, we denote by
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
the space of continuous linear maps from
V
to
W
and by
\mathcal{L}(V)=\mathcal{L}(V,V)
.

Corollary 3.8

(Surjectivity of
1
-jets evaluation)
.

Let
A\neq\emptyset
be finite and let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, the linear map
P\mapsto\left(\left(P(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}P\right)\right)_{a\in A}
is surjective from
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\right)^{A}
.

Proof.

Since
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, we have
\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}=\left\{\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
, see Definition
2.28
. Then, by Remark
2.33
, we have
2\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x}_{2A})
. Lemma
3.7
applied to
2\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}=\left\{\{0,1\}\times\{a\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}a\in A\right\}
and
\underline{x}_{2A}
then shows that
\varpi_{\underline{x}}:P\mapsto\left(K(P,x_{a},x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is surjective from
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
(\mathbb{R}_{1}[X]^{k})^{A}
.

For all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the map
P\mapsto\left(P(y),D_{y}P\right)
is an isomorphism from
\mathbb{R}_{1}[X]^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
whose inverse is given by
(z,\Lambda)\mapsto z+\Lambda(X-y)
. Hence
\vartheta_{\underline{x}}:\left(P_{a}\right)_{a\in A}\mapsto\left(\left(P_{a}(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}P_{a}\right)\right)_{a\in A}
is an isomorphism from
(\mathbb{R}_{1}[X]^{k})^{A}
to
\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\right)^{A}
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. By Example
3.5
, we know that
K(P,x_{a},x_{a})
is the Taylor polynomial of degree
1
of
P
at
x_{a}
. Hence
\vartheta_{\underline{x}}\circ\varpi_{\underline{x}}(P)=\vartheta_{\underline{x}}\left(\left(K(P,x_{a},x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}\right)=\left(\left(P(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}P\right)\right)_{a\in A}
and the map we are interested in is
\vartheta_{\underline{x}}\circ\varpi_{\underline{x}}:\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\to\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\right)^{A}
. It is surjective as the composition of two surjective maps.
∎

To conclude this section, we discuss the regularity of divided differences. Recall that
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is convex and open. Given a compact
\Gamma\subset U
, we denote by
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{q},\Gamma}=\max\left\{\lVert D^{i}_{z}f\rVert\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket,z\in\Gamma\right\}
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
, where the norm on
\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is the operator norm subordinated to the Euclidean norms on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We consider
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
endowed with the compact-open topology, that is the metric topology characterized by the fact that
f_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\penalty\ f
if and only if
\lVert f_{n}-f\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{q},\Gamma}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
for all compact subsets
\Gamma\subset U
.

Lemma 3.9

(Regularity of divided differences)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
. The map
\left(f,\underline{x}\right)\mapsto f[\underline{x}]
is continuous from
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times U^{A}
to
\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. Let us fix
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{q})\in U^{q+1}
, and prove the continuity of the map we are interested in at the point
(f,\underline{x})
. There exists
\varrho>0
such that, for all
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, the closed ball
\mathbb{B}_{i}
of center
x_{i}
and radius
\varrho
is contained in
U
. By Carathéodory’s Theorem,
\Gamma=\conv\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathbb{B}_{i}\right)\subset U
is a compact set.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that, for all
g\in\mathcal{O}
, we have
\lVert g-f\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{q},\Gamma}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Besides, by uniform continuity of
D^{q}f
on
\Gamma
, there exists
\delta\in(0,\varrho)
such that
\left\lVert D^{q}_{z}f-D^{q}_{w}f\right\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
for all
w,z\in\Gamma
such that
\lVert z-w\rVert\leqslant\delta
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{O}
and
y_{0},\dots,y_{q}\in U
be such that
\lVert y_{i}-x_{i}\rVert\leqslant\delta
for all
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. Since
\delta<\varrho
, we have
y_{0},\dots,y_{q}\in\Gamma
. By convexity, for any
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{q}
we have
\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}
and
\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}\in\Gamma
and
\lVert\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}\rVert\leqslant\delta
, so that

\left\lVert f[\underline{y}]-f[\underline{x}]\right\rVert\leqslant\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{q}}\left\lVert D^{q}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f-D^{q}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}}f\right\rVert\dmesure\!\varsigma_{q}(\underline{t})\leqslant\varepsilon.

On the other hand, since
g\in\mathcal{O}
and
\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}\in\Gamma
for all
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{q}
, we have:

\left\lVert g[\underline{y}]-f[\underline{y}]\right\rVert\leqslant\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{q}}\left\lVert D^{q}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}(g-f)\right\rVert\dmesure\!\varsigma_{q}(\underline{t})\leqslant\left\lVert g-f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{q},\Gamma}\leqslant\varepsilon.

Thus,
\left\lVert g[\underline{y}]-f[\underline{x}]\right\rVert\leqslant\left\lVert g[\underline{y}]-f[\underline{y}]\right\rVert+\left\lVert f[\underline{y}]-f[\underline{x}]\right\rVert\leqslant 2\varepsilon
for all
g\in\mathcal{O}
and
y_{0},\dots,y_{q}\in U
such that
\lVert y_{i}-x_{i}\rVert\leqslant\delta
for all
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. This proves the continuity at
(f,\underline{x})
and concludes the proof.
∎

3.2
Divided differences of a Gaussian field

In this section, we recall some elementary facts about Gaussian vectors and fields. Then, we study the divided differences associated with a Gaussian field, which are themselves Gaussian fields.

Definition 3.10

(Covariance operators)
.

Let
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
be centered Gaussian vectors taking values in Euclidean spaces
\left(V_{1},\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle\right)
and
\left(V_{2},\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle\right)
respectively. The
covariance operator
of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
is the only linear map
\Cov\left(Y_{1},Y_{2}\right)\in\mathcal{L}(V_{2},V_{1})
such that:

\forall v_{1}\in V_{1},\forall v_{2}\in V_{2},\quad\left\langle v_{1}\,,\Cov\left(Y_{1},Y_{2}\right)v_{2}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle Y_{1}\,,v_{1}\rangle\langle Y_{2}\,,v_{2}\rangle\right].

(3.2)

Denoting by
\sym(V_{1})
(resp.
\sym^{+}(V_{1})
) the space (resp. set) of self-adjoint (resp. positive self-adjoint) operators on
V_{1}
, the
variance operator
of
Y_{1}
is then
\Var\left(Y_{1}\right)=\Cov\left(Y_{1},Y_{1}\right)\in\sym(V_{1})
. This operator is non-negative, and we say that
Y_{1}
is
non-degenerate
if
\Var\left(Y_{1}\right)\in\sym^{+}(V_{1})
.

Let us introduce some notation in order to talk about the regularity of Gaussian fields and their covariance kernels.

Definition 3.11

(Multi-indices)
.

Let
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
be a
multi-index
.

•

We denote by
\lvert\alpha\rvert=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}
its
length
and by
\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{d}(\alpha_{i}!)
.

•

If
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we denote by
x^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
, and accordingly
X^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
.

•

Given
i\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
, we denote by
\partial_{i}
the partial derivative with respect to the
i
-th variable in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and by
\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
.

•

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we have
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}
and we denote by
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}=\partial^{(\alpha,\beta)}
, acting on maps on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open subset and
V
be some Euclidean space, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{q,q}(U\times U,V)
the space of maps
r:U\times U\to V
such that
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r:U\times U\to V
is well-defined and continuous for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q
and
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q
.

Definition 3.12

(Correlation kernel)
.

Let
f:U\to V
be a centered Gaussian field, where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open and
V
is Euclidean. Its
covariance kernel
is the map
r:U\times U\to\mathcal{L}(V)
defined by:

r:(x,y)\longmapsto\Cov\left(f(x),f(y)\right).

If
f:U\to V
is a
\mathcal{C}^{q}
centered Gaussian field then
r\in\mathcal{C}^{q,q}(U\times U,\mathcal{L}(V))
, see
[
11
, Chap. 1.4.3]
for example. In this case, we have
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r:(x,y)\mapsto\Cov\left(\partial^{\alpha}f(x),\partial^{\beta}f(y)\right)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
of length at most
q
. If
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f
is stationary then
r(x,y)=r(0,y-x)
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let us assume the open set
U
to be convex and let
f
be a centered Gaussian field. We first prove that the divided differences of
f
(see Definition
3.1
) define Gaussians fields.

Lemma 3.13

(Divided differences of a Gaussian field)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field. Then
f[\,\cdot\,]:U^{A}\to\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a continuous centered Gaussian field.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\underline{x}^{(1)},\dots,\underline{x}^{(n)}\in U^{A}
, the map
f\mapsto\left(f[\underline{x}^{(i)}]\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
is linear and continuous, hence
\left(f[\underline{x}^{(i)}]\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
is a centered Gaussian vector, see
[
16
, Lem. 2.2.2]
. This shows that
f[\,\cdot\,]
is a centered Gaussian field. It is continuous, once again by Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Let
(e_{1},\dots,e_{d})
stand for the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all multi-index
\alpha
of length
q
, we denote by
e_{\alpha}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{1},\dots,e_{d},\dots,e_{d})
, where
e_{i}
appears exactly
\alpha_{i}
times. We denote by
e_{\alpha}^{*}
the unique element of
\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
such that
e^{*}_{\alpha}(e_{\alpha})=1
and
e^{*}_{\alpha}(e_{\beta})=0
if
\lvert\beta\rvert=q
and
\beta\neq\alpha
. The family
(e_{\alpha}^{*}\otimes e_{i})_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=q,1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
is a basis of
\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\simeq\sym^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
and we endow this space with the only inner product making this basis orthonormal. Note that, for any
\mathcal{C}^{q}
function
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

D^{q}_{z}f=\sum_{i=1}^{k}D^{q}_{z}f_{i}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=q}D^{q}_{z}f_{i}(e_{\alpha})e_{\alpha}^{*}\right)\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=q,1\leqslant i\leqslant k}\partial^{\alpha}f_{i}(z)e_{\alpha}^{*}\otimes e_{i}.

(3.3)

Lemma 3.14

(Covariance of divided differences)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field, let
\underline{x}\in U^{m+1}
and
\underline{y}\in U^{n+1}
where
m\leqslant q
and
n\leqslant q
. Then, we have:

\Cov\left(f[\underline{x}],f[\underline{y}]\right)=\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\Cov\left(D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f,D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\right)\dmesure\!\underline{s}\dmesure\!\underline{t}.

Proof.

Let
u\in\sym^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
v\in\sym^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, by linearity and Equation (
3.2
), we have:

\displaystyle\left\langle u\,,\left(\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\Cov\left(D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f,D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\right)\dmesure\!\underline{s}\dmesure\!\underline{t}\right)v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\left\langle u\,,\Cov\left(D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f,D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\right)v\right\rangle\dmesure\!\underline{s}\dmesure\!\underline{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f\,,u\rangle\langle D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\,,v\rangle\right]\dmesure\!\underline{s}\dmesure\!\underline{t}.

The integrand being a product of two continuous Gaussian fields, Fubini’s Theorem applies and we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\langle D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f\,,u\rangle\dmesure\!\underline{s}\right)\left(\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\langle D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\,,v\rangle\dmesure\!\underline{t}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle f[\underline{x}]\,,u\rangle\langle f[\underline{y}]\,,v\rangle\right]=\left\langle u\,,\Cov\left(f[\underline{x}],f[\underline{y}]\right)v\right\rangle.

These computations holding for any
u
and
v
, this proves the result.
∎

Lemma 3.15

(Distance between covariances of divided differences)
.

Let
f
and
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be centered Gaussian fields, we denote respectively by
r
and
\widetilde{r}
their covariance kernels. Let
m
and
n\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, for all
\underline{x}\in U^{m+1}
and
\underline{y}\in U^{n+1}
we have the following estimates:

\left\lVert\Cov\left(f[\underline{x}],f[\underline{y}]\right)-\Cov\left(\widetilde{f}[\underline{x}],\widetilde{f}[\underline{y}]\right)\right\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{d}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert=m\\
\lvert\beta\rvert=n\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{y})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert,

where the norms are the operator norms subordinated to the Euclidean ones.

Proof.

Recall that we have defined an orthonormal basis
(e_{\alpha}^{*}\otimes e_{i})_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
of
\sym^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
(resp.
(e_{\beta}^{*}\otimes e_{j})_{\lvert\beta\rvert=n,1\leqslant j\leqslant k}
of
\sym^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
). Let

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,1\leqslant i\leqslant k}u_{\alpha,i}e_{\alpha}^{*}\otimes e_{i}

and

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lvert\beta\rvert=n,1\leqslant j\leqslant k}v_{\beta,j}e_{\beta}^{*}\otimes e_{j}.

For all
\alpha
(resp.
\beta
) of length
m
(resp.
n
) we denote by
u_{\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{\alpha,i}e_{i}
(resp.
v_{\beta}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{\beta,j}e_{j}
).

We first consider the field
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
. Let
w,z\in U
, by Equations (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) we have:

\displaystyle\left\langle u\,,\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}f,D^{n}_{z}f\right)v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle D^{m}_{w}f\,,u\right\rangle\left\langle D^{n}_{z}f\,,v\right\rangle\right]=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\ \sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant k}u_{\alpha,i}v_{\beta,j}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial^{\alpha}f_{i}(w)\partial^{\beta}f_{j}(z)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert=m\\
\lvert\beta\rvert=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}f(w)\,,u_{\alpha}\right\rangle\left\langle\partial^{\beta}f(z)\,,v_{\beta}\right\rangle\right]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert=m\\
\lvert\beta\rvert=n\end{subarray}}\left\langle u_{\alpha}\,,\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)v_{\beta}\right\rangle.

A similar formula holds for
\widetilde{f}
. Hence, by linearity,

\displaystyle\left\langle u\,,\left(\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}f,D^{n}_{z}f\right)-\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}\widetilde{f},D^{n}_{z}\widetilde{f}\right)\right)v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\left\langle u_{\alpha}\,,\left(\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r-\widetilde{r})(w,z)\right)v_{\beta}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\lVert u_{\alpha}\rVert\lVert v_{\beta}\rVert\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r-\widetilde{r})(w,z)\right\rVert.

If
\lVert u\rVert=1=\lVert v\rVert
, we have
\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m}\lVert u_{\alpha}\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\card\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lvert\alpha\rvert=m\right\}}\leqslant(m+1)^{\frac{d}{2}}\leqslant(q+1)^{\frac{d}{2}}
, and similarly
\sum_{\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\lVert v_{\beta}\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{\frac{d}{2}}
. Hence, the last term in the previous computation is bounded by
(q+1)^{d}\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r-\widetilde{r})(w,z)\right\rVert
. Thus,

\left\lVert\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}f,D^{n}_{z}f\right)-\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}\widetilde{f},D^{n}_{z}\widetilde{f}\right)\right\rVert\\
\begin{aligned} &=\max_{\lVert u\rVert=1=\lVert v\rVert}\left\langle u\,,\left(\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}f,D^{n}_{z}f\right)-\Cov\left(D^{m}_{w}\widetilde{f},D^{n}_{z}\widetilde{f}\right)\right)v\right\rangle\\
&\leqslant(q+1)^{d}\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert.\end{aligned}

Finally, by Lemma
3.14
, we obtain:

\left\lVert\Cov\left(f[\underline{x}],f[\underline{y}]\right)-\Cov\left(\widetilde{f}[\underline{x}],\widetilde{f}[\underline{y}]\right)\right\rVert\\
\begin{aligned} &\leqslant\int_{\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}}\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}}\left\lVert\Cov\left(D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}f,D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{y}}f\right)-\Cov\left(D^{m}_{\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}}\widetilde{f},D^{n}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{t}}\widetilde{f}\right)\right\rVert\dmesure\!\underline{s}\dmesure\!\underline{t}\\
&\leqslant(q+1)^{d}\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert=m,\lvert\beta\rvert=n}\ \max_{w\in\conv(\underline{x}),z\in\conv(\underline{y})}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert,\end{aligned}

since the simplices
{{\blacktriangle}}_{m}
and
{{\blacktriangle}}_{n}
have measure at most
1
.
∎

3.3
Twisted Kergin interpolant

In this section, we introduce a twisted Kergin interpolant which is well-behaved with respect to the action of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by diagonal translations.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, recall that the
diagonal action

\mathbb{R}^{d}\curvearrowright(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
is defined by:
\tau\cdot\underline{x}=\left(x_{a}+\tau\right)_{a\in A}
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
. Besides, given
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f
any function defined on
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\tau\cdot f:y\mapsto f(y+\tau)
the
\tau
-translate of
f
, defined on
U-\tau
. This defines families of compatible actions
\mathbb{R}^{d}\curvearrowright\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}\curvearrowright\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. More generally, if
f
is any function on a subset of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we denote by
\tau\cdot f:\underline{x}\mapsto f(\tau\cdot\underline{x})
.

As in the previous sections, we consider a convex open subset
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 3.16

(Translation equivariance of divided differences and Kergin interpolants)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
. For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have:

\displaystyle D^{q}(\tau\cdot f)=\tau\cdot D^{q}f,

\displaystyle(\tau\cdot f)[\underline{x}]=f[\tau\cdot\underline{x}]

and

\displaystyle K(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x})=\tau\cdot K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x}).

Proof.

Let
T_{\tau}:y\mapsto y+\tau
from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to itself, so that
\tau\cdot f=f\circ T_{\tau}
. Applying recursively the chain rule, we obtain
D^{l}\left(\tau\cdot f\right)=D^{l}\left(f\circ T_{\tau}\right)=(D^{l}f)\circ T_{\tau}=\tau\cdot D^{l}f
for all
l\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, where
D^{l}f
is seen as a map from
U
to
\sym^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
. For
l=q
this is the first point.

Let
B\subset A
be of cardinality
l+1
. Then, using the previous point,

(\tau\cdot f)[\underline{x}_{B}]=\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{B}}D^{l}_{\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{B}}(\tau\cdot f)\dmesure\!\varsigma_{B}(\underline{t})=\int_{\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{B}}D^{l}_{\tau+\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{B}}f\dmesure\!\varsigma_{q}(\underline{t})=f[\tau\cdot\underline{x}_{B}].

For
B=A
, this yields the second point.

Let
B\subset A
be non-empty, using the interpolation properties of the Kergin interpolant, we have:

K(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x})[\underline{x}_{B}]=(\tau\cdot f)[\underline{x}_{B}]=f[\tau\cdot\underline{x}_{B}]=K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})[\tau\cdot\underline{x}_{B}]=\left(\tau\cdot K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})\right)[\underline{x}_{B}],

where the last equality is obtained by applying the previous point to
K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})
. Thus,
K(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x})
and
\tau\cdot K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})
are two polynomial maps of degree at most
q
with the same divided differences on all
(\underline{x}_{B})_{\emptyset\neq B\subset A}
. The uniqueness part in Theorem
3.4
proves that
K(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x})=\tau\cdot K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})
.
∎

Let us now consider a centered Gaussian field
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
and assume that
f
is stationary. Let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by definition we have
\tau\cdot f=f
in distribution. Then, the second point in Lemma
3.16
shows that the map
\tau\cdot\left(f[\,\cdot\,]\right):\underline{x}\mapsto f[\tau\cdot\underline{x}]
is distributed as
f[\,\cdot\,]
. That is, the distribution of the field
f[\,\cdot\,]
is invariant under diagonal translations. Note that
f[\,\cdot\,]
is a priori not stationary, as we only control its behavior under translations by vectors of the kind
(\tau,\dots,\tau)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
.

The Kergin interpolant does not behave as nicely. If the distribution of
K(f,\cdot)
was invariant by diagonal translations, the stationarity of
f
and the third point in Lemma
3.16
would imply that

K(f,\underline{x})=K(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x})=\tau\cdot K(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\tau\cdot K(f,\underline{x})

in distribution, for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
. This does not hold in general. For example, consider the case
d=k=q=1
and
\underline{x}=(0,0)
with
\Var\left(f(0),f^{\prime}(0)\right)=\Id
. Then
K(f,0,0)=f(0)+f^{\prime}(0)X
and
\tau\cdot K(f,0,0)=f(0)+\tau f^{\prime}(0)+f^{\prime}(0)X
. But
\Var\left(f(0)+\tau f^{\prime}(0)\right)=1+\tau^{2}\neq\Var\left(f(0)\right)
for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, so that
\tau\cdot K(f,0,0)
is not distributed as
K(f,0,0)
. More fundamentally, a non-degenerate Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
cannot be invariant under the action of
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, because
\tau
acts non-trivially on
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
by an endomorphism whose only complex eigenvalue is
1
.

In order to recover a translation-invariant object, we introduce a twisted Kergin interpolant.

Definition 3.17

(Barycenter)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we denote by
\flat(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{\lvert A\rvert}\sum_{a\in A}x_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
(iso-)barycenter
of
\underline{x}
. Let
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}=\left(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})\right)_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the
centered version
of
\underline{x}
, so that
\flat(\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})=0
and
\underline{x}=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}
.

Remark 3.18
.

In general,
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}_{B}:=(x_{b}-\flat(\underline{x}))_{b\in B}
is not equal to
\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{B}}}:=(x_{b}-\flat(\underline{x}_{B}))_{b\in B}
for
B\subset A
.

Definition 3.19

(Twisted Kergin interpolant)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
. For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, we define
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x})=K(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f,\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot K(f,\underline{x})
, see Lemma
3.16
.

Example 3.20
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\flat(y,y)=y
and
\accentset{\bullet}{(y,y)}=0
. Hence
K(f,y,y)=f(y)+f^{\prime}(y)(X-y)
is the degree
1
Taylor polynomial of
f
at
y
, while
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,y,y)=K(y\cdot f,0)=f(y)+f^{\prime}(y)X
is the Taylor polynomial of
y\cdot f:z\mapsto f(z+y)
at
z=0
.

Lemma 3.21

(Translation equivariance of twisted Kergin interpolants)
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x}\right)=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x}\right)
.

Proof.

We compute, using the fact that
\flat(\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\flat(\underline{x})+\tau
and
\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\tau\cdot\underline{x}}}=\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}
,

\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x}\right)=K\left(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot(\tau\cdot f),\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\right)=K\left(\left(\flat(\underline{x})+\tau\right)\cdot f,\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\right)=K\left(\flat(\tau\cdot\underline{x})\cdot f,\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\tau\cdot\underline{x}}}\right)=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x}\right).\qed

Let us now introduce some auxiliary operators that will be useful to relate the divided differences of
f
to
K(f,\cdot)
and
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\cdot)
.

Definition 3.22

(Divided differences operators)
.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{q})\in U^{q+1}
, we denote by
\Delta_{\underline{x}}:\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\Psi_{\underline{x}}:\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
the linear maps:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\underline{x}}:f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}]\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{\underline{x}}:\left(S_{i}\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{i=0}^{q}S_{i}(X-x_{0},\dots,X-x_{i-1}).

As a consequence of Equation (
3.1
), for all
\underline{x}\in U^{q+1}
we have
K(\cdot,\underline{x})=\Psi_{\underline{x}}\circ\Delta_{\underline{x}}
. Moreover, Theorem
3.4
shows that the restriction of
\Delta_{\underline{x}}
to
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
is an isomorphism, whose inverse is
\Psi_{\underline{x}}
. We can also express
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x})
in terms of the previous maps.

Lemma 3.23

(Factorization of
\accentset{\bullet}{K}
)
.

For all
\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{q})\in U^{q+1}
we have
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x})=\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\circ\Delta_{\underline{x}}
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
, by Definitions
3.19
and
3.22
and Equation (
3.1
) we have:

\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x})=K(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f,\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})=\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\circ\Delta_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\left(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f\right).

For all
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, we have
(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f)[x_{0}-\flat(\underline{x}),\dots,x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x})]=f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}]
by the second point in Lemma
3.16
. This implies that
\Delta_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f)=\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f)
. Hence,
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x})=\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\circ\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f)
.
∎

Lemma 3.24

(Continuity of
\Psi
)
.

The map
\underline{x}\mapsto\Psi_{\underline{x}}
is continuous from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
to the space
\mathcal{L}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k}),\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}\right)
, endowed with its unique normed topology.

Proof.

The spaces under consideration being finite-dimensional, we can use any norms we fancy. On
\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, we work with the sup-norm associated with the operator norm on each factor. On
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
, we use the norm defined by
\lVert P\rVert=\max_{\lVert z\rVert\leqslant 1}\lVert P(z)\rVert
for all
P\in\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
. Finally, we use the associated operator norm on
\mathcal{L}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k}),\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Let
\varrho>0
and let
\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{q})
and
\underline{y}=(y_{0},\dots,y_{q})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
be such that
\lVert\underline{x}\rVert<\varrho
and
\lVert\underline{y}\rVert<\varrho
. Let
i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket
and
S_{i}\in\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\lVert z\rVert\leqslant 1
we have:

\lVert S_{i}(z-y_{0},\dots,z-y_{i-1})-S_{i}(z-x_{0},\dots,z-x_{i-1})\rVert\\
\begin{aligned} \leqslant&\lVert S_{i}(z-y_{0},z-y_{1},\dots,z-y_{i-1})-S_{i}(z-x_{0},z-y_{1},\dots,z-y_{i-1})\rVert+\dots&\\
&+\lVert S_{i}(z-x_{0},\dots,z-x_{i-2},z-y_{i-1})-S_{i}(z-x_{0},\dots,z-x_{i-2},z-x_{i-1})\rVert\\
\leqslant&\lVert S_{i}\rVert\sum_{0\leqslant j<i}\left(\lVert y_{j}-x_{j}\rVert\prod_{0\leqslant k<j}\lVert z-x_{k}\rVert\prod_{j<k<i}\lVert z-y_{k}\rVert\right)\leqslant\lVert S_{i}\rVert(1+\varrho)^{i-1}\sum_{0\leqslant j<i}\lVert y_{j}-x_{j}\rVert.\end{aligned}

Hence
\left\lVert S_{i}(X-y_{0},\dots,X-y_{i-1})-S_{i}(X-x_{0},\dots,X-x_{i-1})\right\rVert\leqslant\lVert S_{i}\rVert q(1+\varrho)^{q}\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{x}\right\rVert
.

Then, for all
S=(S_{i})_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}
such that
\lVert S_{i}\rVert\leqslant 1
for all
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi_{\underline{y}}(S)-\Psi_{\underline{x}}(S)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{q}\left\lVert S_{i}(X-y_{0},\dots,X-y_{i-1})-S_{i}(X-x_{0},\dots,X-x_{i-1})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant q^{2}(1+\varrho)^{q}\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{x}\right\rVert.

Thus
\lVert\Psi_{\underline{y}}-\Psi_{\underline{x}}\rVert\leqslant q^{2}(1+\varrho)^{q}\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{x}\right\rVert
. This proves the continuity of
\underline{x}\mapsto\Psi_{\underline{x}}
on the ball of center
0
and radius
\varrho
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
. Hence the result, since
\varrho
is arbitrary.
∎

Lemma 3.25

(Regularity of the twisted Kergin interpolant)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
.

1.

The map
\accentset{\bullet}{K}:\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times U^{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
is continuous.

2.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then the map
\underline{x}\mapsto\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x})_{|V}
is continuous from
U^{A}
to
\mathcal{L}\left(V,\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}\right)
endowed with its unique normed topology.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. By Lemma
3.9
, we know that
(f,\underline{x})\mapsto\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f)=\left(f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}]\right)_{0\leqslant i\leqslant q}
is continuous from
\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})\times U^{A}
to
\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Since
\underline{x}\mapsto\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}
is continuous, the map
(f,\underline{x})\mapsto\left(\psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}},\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f)\right)
is continuous by Lemma
3.24
. Then, the map
\left(\ell,v\right)\mapsto\ell(v)
is bilinear continuous from
\mathcal{L}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k}),\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}\right)\times\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
to
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
. Hence,
\accentset{\bullet}{K}:(f,\underline{x})\mapsto\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f)\right)
is continuous, which proves Item
1
.

Let
V\subset\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a finite-dimensional subspace. Let us equip
V
with an arbitrary Euclidean inner product and let
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
be an orthonormal basis of
V
. Then, for all
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, we have

\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x})_{|V}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\accentset{\bullet}{K}(v_{j},\underline{x})\langle v_{j}\,,\cdot\rangle,

and this quantity depends continuously on
\underline{x}
by the previous point. This proves Item
2
.
∎

Corollary 3.26

(Twisted interpolant of a Gaussian field)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field, then
\underline{x}\mapsto(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I}))_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
is a continuous centered Gaussian field on
U^{A}
with values in
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.13
, replacing the use of Lemma
3.9
by that of Lemma
3.25
.
1
.
∎

For all
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
making the basis
(X^{\alpha})_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q}
orthonormal. This induces an inner product on
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
such that the factors are orthogonal. Similarly, if
\mathcal{I}
is a partition of some finite set
A
, we endow
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
with the inner product that coincides with the previous one on each factor and makes these factors orthogonal. We can now define the following.

Definition 3.27

(Variance of twisted Kergin interpolants)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field. For all
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, we denote by:

\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})=\Var\left(\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\in\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

For all
I
and
J\in\mathcal{I}
, we also denote by

\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})=\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right):\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k},

so that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})
can be written as a block-operator
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
.

Remark 3.28
.

It follows from Corollary
3.26
that
\underline{x}\mapsto\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})
is continuous on
U^{A}
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.21
, if
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f
is stationary then, for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
,

\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\Var\left(\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\tau\cdot f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)=\Var\left(\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)=\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}).

We conclude this section by introducing projection operators that will allow us to relate
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})
to
\Sigma_{\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})
with
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Recall that we denoted by
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
the space of continuous linear maps from
V
to
W
. In the following, we also denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}(V,W)=\left\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}(V,W)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\Lambda\ \text{is surjective}\right\}
.

Definition 3.29

(Projection operators)
.

Let
A
be a finite set and
\emptyset\neq B\subset A
, we define

\displaystyle\Pi_{B}^{A}:\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\qquad\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}

\displaystyle(P,\underline{x})\qquad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P,\underline{x}_{B}\right).

For all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we also define
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}=\left(\Pi_{I}^{A}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
from
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
to
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
.

Lemma 3.30

(Properties of
\Pi_{B}^{A}
)
.

The projection operator
\Pi_{B}^{A}
satisfies the following properties.

1.

If
U
is an open convex,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, then
\Pi_{B}^{A}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}\right)=\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{B})
.

2.

For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, the map
\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is linear surjective from
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
.

3.

The map
\underline{x}\mapsto\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is continuous from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

4.

For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})
.

Proof.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open and convex. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, by Definition
3.19
we have
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x})=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot K(f,\underline{x})
. Hence
(-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x})=K(f,\underline{x})
and

\Pi_{B}^{A}(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x})=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(K(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{B}\right)=\flat(\underline{x}_{B})\cdot K\left(K(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{B}\right).

By Theorem
3.4
, for all non-empty
C\subset B
, we have
K\left(K(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{B}\right)[\underline{x}_{C}]=K(f,\underline{x})[\underline{x}_{C}]=f[\underline{x}_{C}]
, and the uniqueness of the Kergin interpolant yields
K\left(K(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{B}\right)=K(f,\underline{x}_{B})
. This implies that
\Pi_{B}^{A}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{B}\right)=\flat(\underline{x}_{B})\cdot K(f,\underline{x}_{B})=\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{B})
and proves Item
1
.

Let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we denote by
T_{\flat(\underline{x})}
the isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
defined as
T_{\flat(\underline{x})}:P\mapsto\flat(\underline{x})\cdot P
. Similarly, we denote by
T_{\flat(\underline{x}_{B})}:P\mapsto\flat(\underline{x}_{B})\cdot P
from
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to itself. For all
P\in\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
,

\Pi_{B}^{A}(P,\underline{x})=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P,\underline{x}_{B}\right)=\flat(\underline{x}_{B})\cdot K\left((-\flat(\underline{x})\cdot P,\underline{x}_{B}\right)=T_{\flat(\underline{x}_{B})}\circ K(\cdot,\underline{x}_{B})\circ T_{\flat(\underline{x})}^{-1}(P).

(3.4)

That is,
\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})=T_{\flat(\underline{x}_{B})}\circ K(\cdot,\underline{x}_{B})\circ T_{\flat(\underline{x})}^{-1}
. The linear map
K(\cdot,\underline{x}_{B})
is surjective from
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. Indeed, its restriction to
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
is the identity. Since
T_{\flat(\underline{x})}
and
T_{\flat(\underline{x}_{B})}
are isomorphisms, this proves Item
2
.

Let us now focus on Item
3
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. Recall that, for any
\underline{y}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
we have
\Psi_{\underline{y}}\circ\Delta_{\underline{y}}=\Id
on
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
, see Definition
3.22
and Theorem
3.4
. Let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, applying the previous equality with
\underline{y}=\tau\cdot 0
we obtain for all
P\in\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
:

(-\tau)\cdot P=\Psi_{\tau\cdot 0}\circ\Delta_{\tau\cdot 0}\left((-\tau)\cdot P\right)=\Psi_{\tau\cdot 0}(\Delta_{0}(P))

by the second point in Lemma
3.16
. Then, for all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
and
P\in\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
, we have

\Pi_{B}^{A}(P,\underline{x})=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P,\underline{x}_{B}\right)=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left(\Psi_{\flat(\underline{x})\cdot 0}(\Delta_{0}(P)),\underline{x}_{B}\right)

by applying the previous relation with
\tau=\flat(\underline{x})
. This shows that
\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})=\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{B})\circ\Psi_{\flat(\underline{x})\cdot 0}\circ\Delta_{0}
. By Lemma
3.24
and
3.25
.
2
, the map
\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\cdot,\underline{x}),\Psi_{\flat(\underline{x})\cdot 0},\Delta_{0}\right)
is continuous from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q+1}
to

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{\lvert B\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\times\mathcal{L}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k}),\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}\right)\times\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k},\prod_{i=0}^{q}\sym^{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\right).

Since
(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{3})\mapsto\Lambda_{1}\circ\Lambda_{2}\circ\Lambda_{3}
is continuous on this space, we finally get that
\underline{x}\mapsto\Pi_{B}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is continuous on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
.

Finally, let
P\in\mathbb{R}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
,
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by Lemma
3.21
we have:

\displaystyle\Pi_{B}^{A}(P,\tau\cdot\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\tau-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P,(\tau\cdot\underline{x})_{B}\right)=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\tau)\cdot\left((-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P\right),\tau\cdot\underline{x}_{B}\right)

\displaystyle=\accentset{\bullet}{K}\left((-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P,\underline{x}_{B}\right)=\Pi_{B}^{A}(P,\underline{x}).\qed

Lemma 3.31

(Surjectivity of
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}
)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
the map
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x}):\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\to\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
is surjective.

Proof.

Using the same notation as in the proof of Lemma
3.30
.
2
, Equation (
3.4
) shows that for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
we have
\Pi_{I}^{A}(\cdot,\underline{x})=T_{\flat(\underline{x}_{I})}\circ K(\cdot,\underline{x}_{I})\circ T_{\flat(\underline{x})}^{-1}
. Thus
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is the composition of the following three maps: the map
T_{\flat(\underline{x})}^{-1}:P\mapsto(-\flat(\underline{x}))\cdot P
, which is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
; the map
P\mapsto\left(K(P,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
, which is surjective by Lemma
3.7
since
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
; and finally
(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mapsto\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\cdot P_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
, which is an isomorphism of
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. Hence the result.
∎

3.4
Uniform estimates for the variance operator
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}

This section is dedicated to finding conditions ensuring the non-degeneracy of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})
, see Definition
3.27
, uniformly with respect to
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, see Definition
2.31
. We start by proving non-degeneracy results for projection operators. Then, we use them to deduce non-degeneracy results for variance operators.

Lemma 3.32

(Bounds on centered tuples)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set,
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta\geqslant 0
. Let
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
i\in I
, we have
\lVert x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\rVert\leqslant\card(A)\eta
, and
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}}\rVert\leqslant\card(A)^{2}\eta
.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
i\in I
. By Lemma
2.37
, for all
j\in I
, we have
\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert\leqslant\card(A)\eta
. Then, we have
\left\lVert x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\right\rVert=\left\lVert x_{i}-\frac{1}{\lvert I\rvert}\sum_{j\in I}x_{j}\right\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{\lvert I\rvert}\sum_{j\in I}\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert\leqslant\card(A)\eta
, see Definition
3.17
. Finally,
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}}\rVert\leqslant\sum_{i\in I}\lVert x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x})\rVert\leqslant\card(A)^{2}\eta
.
∎

Corollary 3.33

(Compactness of the deepest centered stratum)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\eta\geqslant 0
. Then, the set
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}:=\left\{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\flat(\underline{x})=0\right\}
is compact.

Proof.

Since
\flat
is continuous and
{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is closed by Lemma
2.36
, the set
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is closed in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
as the intersection of two closed sets. Then, if
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is such that
\flat(\underline{x})=0
, Lemma
3.32
yields
\left\lVert\underline{x}\right\rVert=\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\rVert\leqslant\card(A)^{2}\eta
. Hence,
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is also bounded, therefore compact.
∎

Given two vector spaces
V
and
W
, we denoted by
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}(V,W)
the space of continuous linear surjections from
V
to
W
and defined
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}
in Definition
3.29
. Recall that a set is said to be
relatively compact
in a topological space if its closure is compact.

Lemma 3.34

(Uniform non-degeneracy of projectors)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
0<\delta\leqslant\eta
, the set

\left\{\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Proof.

We proved in Corollary
3.33
that
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is compact. Hence, the set
\left\{\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}\right\}
is compact by Lemma
3.30
.
3
. By Lemma
3.30
.
4
, the map
\underline{x}\mapsto\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is translation-invariant. Thus, we have

\left\{\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}\right\}\subset\left\{\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}\right\}

and the left-hand side has compact closure in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. To conclude, we need to prove that its closure is contained in
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Let
(\underline{x}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}
such that
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x}^{(n)})\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\Pi
. By Lemma
3.30
.
4
, we can assume that
\flat(\underline{x}^{(n)})=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, by compactness of
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}
, we can assume that
\underline{x}^{(n)}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\underline{x}
. By continuity, we have
\Pi=\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})
, and we need to prove that it is surjective.

Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
be such that
I\neq J
. By contradiction, if
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)<\delta
, there exist
\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{I}
and
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{J}
such that
\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}\right\rVert<\delta
. This would imply that
\left\lVert\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}-\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}\right\rVert<\delta
for
n
large enough and contradict
\underline{x}^{(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}
, see Lemma
2.37
. Hence, for all
I\neq J
, we have
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\geqslant\delta>0
. Thus
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
, and
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\underline{x})
is surjective by Lemma
3.31
.
∎

Definition 3.35

(Block-diagonal operators)
.

Let
(\Lambda_{a})_{a\in A}\in\prod_{a\in A}\mathcal{L}(V_{a},W_{a})
be a finite family of linear maps. We denote the associated block-diagonal operator from
\prod_{a\in A}V_{a}
to
\prod_{a\in A}W_{a}
by
\bigoplus_{a\in A}\Lambda_{a}:(v_{a})_{a\in A}\mapsto\left(\Lambda_{a}(v_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
.

Corollary 3.36
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
and let
0<\delta\leqslant\eta
. Then, the set

\left\{\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.34
, for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
there exists a compact set
\Gamma_{J}
such that

\left\{\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\{J\},\eta}\right\}\subset\Gamma_{J}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

If
\left(\Lambda_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\in\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Gamma_{J}
then
\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{J}
is a block-diagonal operator with surjective diagonal blocks, hence it is surjective. By continuity of the map
(\Lambda_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{J}
, the set

\left\{\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{J}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall J\in\mathcal{J},\Lambda_{J}\in\Gamma_{J}\right\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)

(3.5)

is compact. By Lemma
2.35
, if
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}
then for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
we have
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\{J\},\eta}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\in\Gamma_{J}
. Hence
\left\{\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\right\}
is contained in the compact set on the left-hand side of Equation (
3.5
), which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us now consider the blocks of the variance operators introduced in Definition
3.27
.

Lemma 3.37

(Distance between covariances of twisted interpolants)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta\geqslant 0
, there exists
C\geqslant 0
with the following property: for any open convex
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for any centered Gaussian fields
f
and
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
, for any set
A
of cardinality
q+1
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, for any
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, we have:

\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(\widetilde{f},\underline{x})\right\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert,

where
r
and
\widetilde{r}
stand for the covariance kernels of
f
and
\widetilde{f}
respectively.

Proof.

Let
f
and
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be centered Gaussian fields. Let
m,n\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
and let us consider
x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y_{0},\dots,y_{n}\in U
. By Definition
3.22
, we have

\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(f)\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\Cov\left(f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}],f[y_{0},\dots,y_{j}]\right)\end{pmatrix}_{0\leqslant i\leqslant m,0\leqslant j\leqslant n}

as a block-operator, and similarly for
\widetilde{f}
. Using Lemma
3.15
, we obtain the following bound for the operator norm:

\left\lVert\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(f)\right)-\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(\widetilde{f}),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(\widetilde{f})\right)\right\rVert\\
\begin{aligned} &\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant i\leqslant m,0\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left\lVert\Cov\left(f[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}],f[y_{0},\dots,y_{j}]\right)-\Cov\left(\widetilde{f}[x_{0},\dots,x_{i}],\widetilde{f}[y_{0},\dots,y_{j}]\right)\right\rVert\\
&\leqslant(q+1)^{d+2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{y})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert.\end{aligned}

By Lemma
3.23
, we have
\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{y})\right)=\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\circ\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(f)\right)\circ\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}}^{*}
, where
\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}}^{*}
stands for the adjoint operator of
\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}}
. A similar formula holds for
\widetilde{f}
and, since we use the operator norm subordinated to the Euclidean ones, this yields:

\left\lVert\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{y})\right)-\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\widetilde{f},\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\widetilde{f},\underline{y})\right)\right\rVert\\
\begin{aligned} &\leqslant\left\lVert\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(f),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(f)\right)-\Cov\left(\Delta_{\underline{x}}(\widetilde{f}),\Delta_{\underline{y}}(\widetilde{f})\right)\right\rVert\left\lVert\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}}^{*}\right\rVert\\
&\leqslant(q+1)^{d+2}\left\lVert\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\Psi_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}}\right\rVert\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{y})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{r}(w,z)\right\rVert.\end{aligned}

By Lemma
3.24
, the map
\underline{x}\mapsto\Psi_{\underline{x}}
is continuous, hence bounded by some constant
C_{m}\geqslant 0
on the closed ball of center
0
and radius
(q+1)^{2}\eta
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m+1}
. Assuming that
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{2}\eta
and
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{y}}\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{2}\eta
, and denoting by
C=(q+1)^{d+2}\max_{0\leqslant m\leqslant q}C_{m}^{2}
, we obtain:
