\left\lVert\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{y})\right)-\Cov\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\widetilde{f},\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\widetilde{f},\underline{y})\right)\right\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{y})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r-\widetilde{r})(w,z)\right\rVert.

Now, let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
. Since
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})
is invariant under permutation of
I
(see Remark
3.6
and Definition
3.19
), we can choose an arbitrary ordering of
I
(resp.
J
) and write
\underline{x}_{I}
as
(x_{0},\dots,x_{\lvert I\rvert})
(resp.
\underline{x}_{J}
as
(y_{0},\dots,y_{\lvert J\rvert})
). By Lemma
3.32
, since
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, we have
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}}\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{2}\eta
and
\lVert\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{J}}}\rVert\leqslant(q+1)^{2}\eta
, so that the previous bound holds with
\underline{x}
replaced by
\underline{x}_{I}
and
\underline{y}
replaced by
\underline{x}_{J}
. Hence the result.
∎

Let us now focus on the case where
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a stationary field. The following is one of the key results of this section.

Proposition 3.38

(Uniform non-degeneracy of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
for stationary fields)
.

Let
A
be a finite set of cardinality
q+1
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a stationary centered Gaussian field satisfying (
ND
(
q
)
) and (
DCL
(
q,\infty
)
). Then, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
the set
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

The remainder of this section is mostly dedicated to the proof of Proposition
3.38
.
Given a Euclidean space
V
, a subset
\mathcal{O}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}(V)
if and only if it is bounded in
\sym(V)
and there exists
C>0
such that:
\forall\Lambda\in\mathcal{O}
,
\det(\Lambda)\geqslant C
. The next lemma takes care of the boundedness of
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}
. Obtaining a uniform lower bound on determinants is more involved, and is dealt with below.

Lemma 3.39

(Boundedness of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
for stationary fields)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
and
\eta\geqslant 0
, let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered stationary Gaussian field, for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
we have:

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert<+\infty.

Proof.

Given
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, it is enough to prove that
\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})
is bounded on
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
for all
I
and
J\in\mathcal{I}
, see Definition
3.27
. Let us fix
I
and
J\in\mathcal{I}
. By Lemma
3.37
applied on
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\widetilde{f}=0
, there exists
C\geqslant 0
such that, for all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
,

\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \max_{w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)\right\rVert,

where
r:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\sym(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is the covariance kernel of
f
. Let
\alpha
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
be of length at most
q
and let
w,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
u
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\lVert u\rVert=1=\lVert v\rVert
we have:

\displaystyle\left\langle u\,,\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}f(w)\,,u\right\rangle\left\langle\partial^{\beta}f(z)\,,v\right\rangle\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\partial^{\alpha}f(w)\,,u\right\rangle^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\partial^{\beta}f(z)\,,v\right\rangle^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\langle u\,,\partial^{\alpha,\alpha}r(w,w)u\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle v\,,\partial^{\beta,\beta}r(z,z)v\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\alpha}r(0,0)\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\partial^{\beta,\beta}r(0,0)\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the last inequality uses the stationarity of
f
. Taking the maximum over
u
and
v
of norm
1
yields that
\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)\right\rVert\leqslant\max_{\lvert\gamma\rvert\leqslant q}\left\lVert\partial^{\gamma,\gamma}r(0,0)\right\rVert
. Hence,

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\lvert\gamma\rvert\leqslant q}\left\lVert\partial^{\gamma,\gamma}r(0,0)\right\rVert.\qed

An important step on the way to proving Proposition
3.38
is the following.

Lemma 3.40

(Uniform non-degeneracy for well-separated clusters)
.

Let
A
be a finite set of cardinality
q+1
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a stationary centered Gaussian field satisfying (
ND
(
q
)
) and (
DCL
(
q,\infty
)
). Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\delta>0
, there exists
\eta\in(0,\delta]
such that
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Proof.

For all
\eta\in(0,\delta]
, we know by Lemma
3.39
that
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}
is bounded in
\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Thus, we only need to prove that there exists
\eta\in(0,\delta]
and
C>0
such that:
\forall\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}
,
\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\right)\geqslant C
. If it were not the case, there would exists a sequence
(\underline{x}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
such that
\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\right)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
and
\underline{x}^{(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\frac{1}{n}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let us assume that such a sequence exists and derive a contradiction. This is done in three steps. First, we replace
\left(\underline{x}^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by a nice subsequence. Second, we prove that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})
converges toward an operator admitting a block-diagonal structure with respect to some partition
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}
. And third, we prove that the diagonal blocks of the limit are positive-definite.

Step 1: Extracting a nice subsequence.

We prove by induction on
\card(A)
that there exists an increasing
\varphi:\mathbb{N}^{*}\to\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that, for all
a,b\in A
we have either
\lVert x_{a}^{\varphi(n)}-x_{b}^{\varphi(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
or
(x_{a}^{\varphi(n)}-x_{b}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
converges in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\card(A)=1
this is true.

If
\card(A)>1
, without loss of generality we can assume that
A=\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
. If
(x_{0}^{(n)}-x_{1}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is bounded, we can extract a subsequence that converges. Otherwise, we can extract a subsequence that diverges to infinity as
n\to+\infty
. Iterating this procedure, we obtain an increasing
\varphi_{0}
such that, for all
i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket
we have either
\lVert x_{0}^{\varphi_{0}(n)}-x_{i}^{\varphi_{0}(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
or
(x_{0}^{\varphi_{0}(n)}-x_{i}^{\varphi_{0}(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
converges in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let us denote by
B=\{i\in\llbracket 1,q\rrbracket\mid\lVert x_{0}^{\varphi_{0}(n)}-x_{i}^{\varphi_{0}(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty\}
. If
B=\emptyset
, it is enough to define
\varphi=\varphi_{0}
. If
B\neq\emptyset
, we have
\card(B)<\card(A)
and the induction hypothesis yields the existence of
\varphi_{1}
such that, for all
a,b\in B
, either
\lVert x_{a}^{\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}(n)}-x_{b}^{\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
or
(x_{a}^{\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}(n)}-x_{b}^{\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
converges. Then,
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{1}
is the subsequence we are looking for.

By Lemma
2.34
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\frac{1}{n}\leqslant\delta
, we have
\underline{x}^{\varphi(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\frac{1}{\varphi(n)}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\subset{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\frac{1}{n}}
, because
\varphi(n)\geqslant n
. Thus, up to considering a subsequence, we can assume that: for all
a
and
b\in A
we have either
\lVert x_{a}^{(n)}-x_{b}^{(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
or
(x_{a}^{(n)}-x_{b}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
converges.

Step 2: Block structure of the limit operator.

Let
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be defined by the property that
[a]_{\mathcal{J}}=[b]_{\mathcal{J}}
if and only if
(x_{a}^{(n)}-x_{b}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
converges. Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
and let
i,j\in I
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
\underline{x}^{(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\frac{1}{n}}
. By Lemma
2.37
, this implies that
\lVert x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\card(A)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
, hence
[i]_{\mathcal{J}}=[j]_{\mathcal{J}}
. This shows that, for all
a,b\in A
, if
[a]_{\mathcal{I}}=[b]_{\mathcal{I}}
then
[a]_{\mathcal{J}}=[b]_{\mathcal{J}}
, that is
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
.

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
j\in J
. By definition of
\mathcal{J}
, the following quantity converges in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
n\to+\infty
:

x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})=x_{j}^{(n)}-\frac{1}{\lvert J\rvert}\sum_{i\in J}x_{i}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{\lvert J\rvert}\sum_{i\in J}x_{j}^{(n)}-x_{i}^{(n)}.

We denote by
x_{j}
the limit of this sequence, which defines a vector
\underline{x}_{J}=(x_{j})_{j\in J}
such that
\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}}}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\underline{x}_{J}
. For all
i,j\in J
, we have

x_{i}-x_{j}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(x_{i}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right)-\left(x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}.

If
x_{i}=x_{j}
then, for all
n
large enough we have
\lVert x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}\rVert<\delta
. Since
\underline{x}^{(n)}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}
, this implies that
[i]_{\mathcal{I}}=[j]_{\mathcal{I}}
by Lemma
2.37
. Conversely, we have already seen that if
[i]_{\mathcal{I}}=[j]_{\mathcal{I}}
then
x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
, so that
x_{i}=x_{j}
. Thus
x_{i}=x_{j}
if and only if
[i]_{\mathcal{I}}=[j]_{\mathcal{I}}
, that is
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}
. By Definition
3.27
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
we have:

\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{I}^{I^{\prime}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\end{pmatrix}_{I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}=\Var\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}\left(f,\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}\right).

Since
f
is stationary, we have
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})=\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)}}})\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})
by Remark
3.28
.

Let
M\geqslant 0
be such that, for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
,
j\in J
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
\lVert x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\rVert\leqslant M
. Such a constant exists, since we consider a finite number of converging sequences. Let
J,J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
be such that
J\neq J^{\prime}
, Let
i\in J
and
j\in J^{\prime}
, we have:

\left\lVert\left(x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}\right)-\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right)\right\rVert\leqslant\left\lVert x_{i}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right\rVert+\left\lVert x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert\leqslant 2M.

By definition of
\mathcal{J}
we have
\lVert x_{i}^{(n)}-x_{j}^{(n)}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
, hence
\left\lVert\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
be such that
I\subset J
and
I^{\prime}\subset J^{\prime}
. Lemma
3.37
applied with
\widetilde{f}=0
yields the existence of
C\geqslant 0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{I^{\prime}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\right\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I^{\prime}}^{(n)})\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)\right\rVert.

Let
w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)})
and
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I^{\prime}}^{(n)})
, there exists
\underline{s}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{I}
and
\underline{t}\in{{\blacktriangle}}_{I^{\prime}}
such that
w=\underline{s}\cdot\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)}
and
z=\underline{t}\cdot\underline{x}_{I^{\prime}}^{(n)}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\lVert w-z\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{i\in I}s_{i}\left(x_{i}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right)-\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}t_{j}\left(x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right)+\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geqslant\left\lVert\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert-\left\lVert\sum_{i\in I}s_{i}\left(x_{i}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})\right)\right\rVert-\left\lVert\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}t_{j}\left(x_{j}^{(n)}-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geqslant\left\lVert\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert-2M.

Recall that we assumed the existence of an even bounded function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
going to
0
at infinity such that (
DCL
(
q,\infty
)
) holds. We deduce from this and the previous inequalities that

\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{I^{\prime}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\right\rVert\leqslant C\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I}^{(n)})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I^{\prime}}^{(n)})\end{subarray}}g(z-w)\leqslant C\sup\left\{g(y)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lVert y\rVert\geqslant\left\lVert\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert-2M\right\}.

Since
\left\lVert\flat(\underline{x}_{J}^{(n)})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J^{\prime}}^{(n)})\right\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
, the right-hand side goes to
0
as
n\to+\infty
. Hence,
\Sigma_{I}^{I^{\prime}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
for any
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
that are included in different blocks of
\mathcal{J}
.

Finally, we have proved that, as
n\to+\infty
, the operator
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{I^{\prime}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\right)_{I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}}
converges towards an operator which is block-diagonal with respect to
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}
, with diagonal blocks
\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
. Moreover, we have checked that
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}
for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
. By continuity,

\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}^{(n)})\right)=0.

Thus, there exists
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}
such that
\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right)=0
.

Step 3: Non-degeneracy of the limit.

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
\underline{x}_{J}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}
be as above. Recalling Definition
2.20
, there exists
\underline{y}=(y_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}_{J}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
such that
\underline{x}_{J}=\iota_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\underline{y})
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}
, we have
\underline{x}_{I}=(y_{I},\dots,y_{I})
, hence
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert<\lvert I\rvert}\frac{\partial^{\alpha}f(y_{I})}{\alpha!}X^{\alpha}
, see Example
3.20
.

We assumed that (
ND
(
q
)
) holds. Since
\lvert J\rvert\leqslant q+1
, we also have (
ND
(
\lvert J\rvert-1
)
). Applying this non-degeneracy hypothesis with
l=\lvert\mathcal{I}_{J}\rvert\leqslant\lvert J\rvert
and
m_{1},\dots,m_{l}
the cardinalities of the blocks of
\mathcal{I}_{J}
, we obtain that the Gaussian vector
\left(\partial^{\alpha_{I}}f(y_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J},\lvert\alpha_{I}\rvert<\lvert I\rvert}
is non-degenerate. Hence
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}
is non-degenerate, and its variance operator
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})
is positive-definite. This contradicts
\det\left(\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right)=0
and concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.38
.

We will prove, by a backward induction on
\mathcal{P}_{A}
, that the following property is satisfied for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
: there exists
\eta_{\mathcal{I}}>0
such that, for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{I}]
, the set
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. This will yield the result by letting
\eta_{0}=\min_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\eta_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, and let us assume that the previous property holds for all
\mathcal{J}>\mathcal{I}
. Note that this condition is satisfied if
\mathcal{I}=\{A\}
. Let us denote by
\delta=\min\left(\left\{\eta_{\mathcal{J}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{J}>\mathcal{I}\right\}\cup\{1\}\right)\in(0,1]
. By Lemma
3.40
, there exists
\eta_{\mathcal{I}}\in(0,\delta]
such that
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta_{\mathcal{I}}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Let
\eta\in(0,\eta_{\mathcal{I}}]
, since
\eta\leqslant\eta_{\mathcal{I}}\leqslant\delta
we have:

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}=\bigcup_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}}\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\delta}\right\},

by Lemma
2.34
. Thus, it is enough to check that each of the sets appearing on the right-hand side is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

In the case
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}
, by Lemma
2.34
we have
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\subset{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta_{\mathcal{I}}}
, so that

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}\subset\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta_{\mathcal{I}}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}.

The right-hand side is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
by definition of
\eta_{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence, so is the left-hand side.

In the case
\mathcal{J}>\mathcal{I}
, since
\delta\leqslant\eta_{\mathcal{J}}
, there exists a compact set
\Gamma_{1}
such that

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\delta}\right\}\subset\Gamma_{1}\subset\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right),

by the induction hypothesis. Besides, by Corollary
3.36
, there exists a compact set
\Gamma_{2}
such that

\left\{\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\delta}\right\}\subset\Gamma_{2}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

Since
(\Sigma,\Pi)\mapsto\Pi\circ\Sigma\circ\Pi^{*}
is continuous,
\Gamma=\left\{\Pi\circ\Sigma\circ\Pi^{*}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\Sigma\in\Gamma_{1},\Pi\in\Gamma_{2}\right\}
is a compact subset of
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Let us now consider
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\delta}
. Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}
, by Lemma
3.30
.
1
we have
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})=\Pi_{I}^{\{J\}}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{J}),\underline{x}_{J}\right)
, hence
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}=\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{J}),\underline{x}_{J}\right)
by Definition
3.29
. Since
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}
we have
\mathcal{I}=\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{I}_{J}
. Thus, recalling Definition
3.35
, we have:

\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}=\left(\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{J}),\underline{x}_{J}\right)\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}=\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}.

It implies the following for variance operators:

\Sigma_{I}(f,\underline{x})=\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\right)\circ\Sigma_{\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\circ\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Pi_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{J})\right)^{*}\in\Gamma.

Hence
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},\delta}\right\}\subset\Gamma
, which proves its relative compactness.

We have proved that for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{I}]
, the set
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. This concludes the induction step, and the proof.
∎

To conclude this section we prove that, if
f
is the limit of a family of Gaussian fields, then we can extend the result of Proposition
3.38
into a uniform non-degeneracy result for the whole family. Recall that we consider a convex open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a non-empty open
\Omega\subset U
such that
\dist(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus U)>0
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be an unbounded subset of
(0,+\infty)
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
f_{R}:RU\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered Gaussian field. In the following,
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is still a centered stationary Gaussian field. We first prove the uniform convergence of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\cdot)
towards
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\cdot)
under the suitable assumptions.

Proposition 3.41

(Uniform convergence of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
. Let us assume that
f
and
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
satisfy Hypotheses (
Reg
(
q
)
), (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
). Then, for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta>0
, we have:

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta>0
. Recalling Definition
3.27
, we have
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
by blocks, and similarly
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
. The estimate we want to prove does not depend on the norm on
\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. For the sake of the argument, say we use the operator norm associated with the Euclidean structure we used to define
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
. Then, it is enough to prove that, for all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
,

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

Recall that
r
(resp.
r_{R}
) stands for the covariance kernel of
f
(resp.
f_{R}
). Let
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
and
\varepsilon>0
. By Lemma
3.37
, there exists
C>0
such that, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, for all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(RU)^{A}
,

\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert\leqslant C\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \max_{w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(w,z)\right\rVert.

(3.6)

Let
g
be the function appearing in (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
). Since
g
goes to
0
at infinity, there exists
T\geqslant\eta
such that
\sup_{\lVert y\rVert\geqslant T}g(y)\leqslant\varepsilon
. Applying our hypothesis (
ScL
(
q
)
) to the closed ball
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of center
0
and radius
\card(A)T
, there exists
R_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
such that: for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
such that
R\geqslant R_{0}
,

\sup_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \sup_{y\in R\Omega}\ \sup_{w,z\in\mathbb{B}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(y+w,y+z)-\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r(w,z)\right\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon.

(3.7)

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}
be such that
R\geqslant R_{0}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}
. Since
T\geqslant\eta
, by Lemma
2.34
there exists a unique
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{I}
such that
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{J},T}
. Recalling Definition
2.9
, if
[I]_{\mathcal{J}}\neq[J]_{\mathcal{J}}
, then
\dist\left(\conv(\underline{x}_{I}),\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\right)>T
by Lemma
2.37
. Then, using (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
), for all
w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
and
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})
we have

\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(w,z)\right\rVert\leqslant g(z-w)\leqslant\sup_{\lVert y\rVert\geqslant T}g(y)\leqslant\varepsilon.

The same estimates holds for
r
. Indeed, as explained in Section
1.4
, if (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
) are satisfied, then (
DCL
(
q,\infty
)
) holds with the same parameter
\omega
and the same function
g
. Hence,
\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\rVert\leqslant 2C\varepsilon
by (
3.6
). If
[I]_{\mathcal{J}}=[J]_{\mathcal{J}}
, let
i\in[I]_{\mathcal{J}}
. For all
j\in[I]_{\mathcal{J}}
we have
\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert\leqslant\card(A)T
, by Lemma
2.37
once again. Hence
\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\cup\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\subset x_{i}+\mathbb{B}
and

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r_{R}-r)(w,z)\right\rVert\leqslant\max_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant q,\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant q}\ \max_{w,z\in\mathbb{B}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}(r_{R}-r)(x_{i}+w,x_{i}+z)\right\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon,

where we used Equation (
3.7
), the stationarity of
f
and the fact that
x_{i}\in R\Omega
. Finally, by Equation (
3.6
), we obtain that
\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\rVert\leqslant C\varepsilon
in this case.

Thus, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{0},+\infty)
, we have
\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{I}^{J}(f,\underline{x})\right\rVert\leqslant 2C\varepsilon
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.42

(Uniform non-degeneracy for families of fields)
.

Let
A
be a set of cardinality
q+1
. Let us assume that
f
and
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
satisfy Hypotheses (
Reg
(
q
)
), (
ND
(
q
)
), (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
). Then, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, there exists
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
such that for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
the set

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}\cup\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\eta},+\infty)\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
.

Proof.

As explained in Section
1.4
, if (
ScL
(
q
)
) and (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
) are satisfied then the correlation kernel
r
of
f
satisfies (
DC
(
q,\infty
)
). Since we also assume (
Reg
(
q
)
) and (
ND
(
q
)
), the field
f
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
3.38
. Let
\eta_{0}>0
be given by Proposition
3.38
and let
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
. It is enough to prove that, for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, there exists
R_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{R}
such that

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}\cup\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\mathcal{I}},+\infty)\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Then the result follows with
R_{\eta}=\max_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}R_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Since
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, the closure
\Gamma
of
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}
is a compact subset of the open cone
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Hence, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\Gamma_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\Lambda\in\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\exists\Sigma\in\Gamma,\lVert\Lambda-\Sigma\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon\right\}\subset\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

By Proposition
3.41
, there exists
R_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{R}
such that: for all
R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\mathcal{I}},+\infty)
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}
, we have
\lVert\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon
. Since
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\in\Gamma
we have
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
. Hence

\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}\cup\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\mathcal{I}},+\infty)\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{A}\right\}\subset\Gamma_{\varepsilon}.

This proves the claimed relative compactness, since
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
is compact as the continuous image of the product of
\Gamma
with a closed ball.
∎

4
Kac–Rice formulas revisited

As suggested by the title, this section is concerned with Kac–Rice formulas, which are a classical tool to study zero set of Gaussian fields, see for instance
[
1
,
10
,
11
,
56
]
. In Section
4.1
, we recall the standard form of these formulas, that express the so-called factorial moments of the linear statistics associated with the zeros of a nice enough Gaussian field. In Section
4.2
, we introduce various maps on spaces of polynomials. Then, in Section
4.3
, we use these maps and the Kergin interpolant from Section
3
to derive alternative expressions of the Kac–Rice densities, which allow for a better understanding of their singularities along the diagonal. Section
4.4
is dedicated to proving similar formulas adapted to cumulants and studying the associated densities.

4.1
Bulinskaya Lemma and Kac–Rice formulas for factorial moments

In this section, we recall some classical facts about random submanifolds defined as zero sets of Gaussian fields. One of the main tools to study these random submanifolds are the Kac–Rice formulas, see Theorem
4.5
below. Let us first recall the following definition.

Definition 4.1

(Jacobian)
.

Let
V
and
W
be Euclidean spaces such that
\dim(V)\geqslant\dim(W)
. Given
\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}(V,W)
, we denote its
Jacobian
by
\Jac\left(\Lambda\right)=\sqrt{\det(\Lambda\Lambda^{*})}
, where
\Lambda^{*}:W\to V
stands for the adjoint operator of
\Lambda
.

Remark 4.2
.

We have
\Jac\left(\Lambda\right)>0
if and only if
\Lambda
is surjective.

Let us now consider an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a centered Gaussian field
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
. Under mild regularity and non-degeneracy assumptions, its zero set
Z
is almost-surely a nice submanifold of
U
.

Proposition 4.3

(Bulinskaya Lemma)
.

Let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
centered Gaussian field such that, for all
x\in U
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x),D_{x}f\right)
is non-degenerate. Then, almost-surely,

\left\{x\in U\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}f(x)=0\ \text{and}\ \Jac\left(D_{x}f\right)=0\right\}=\emptyset.

In particular,
Z=f^{-1}(0)
is almost-surely a submanifold without boundary of codimension
k
in
U
.

Proof.

This result is a special case of
[
11
, Prop. 6.12]
.
∎

Recalling Section
2.3
, we denote by
\nu
the Riemannian volume measure on
Z
induced by the Euclidean metric on
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is its
(d-k)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. For any Borel-measurable function
\phi:U\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote by

\langle\nu\,,\phi\rangle=\int_{Z}\phi(x)\dmesure\!\nu(x),

(4.1)

the
linear statistics
of
\nu
associated with
\phi
. By Proposition
4.3
, the measure
\nu
is almost-surely a Radon measure on
U
, so that
\langle\nu\,,\phi\rangle
is well-defined if
\phi
is continuous with compact support. Actually, a by-product of the Kac–Rice formula for the expectation is that
\langle\nu\,,\phi\rangle
is an almost-surely well-defined random variable as soon as
\phi
is integrable on
U
, see
[
4
, Rem. 6.18]
. Given a non-empty finite set
A
, recall that the power measure
\nu^{A}
and the factorial power measure
\nu^{[A]}
were defined in Definition
2.21
. These are almost-surely Radon measures on
U^{A}
and their linear statistics are defined similarly to (
4.1
).

Definition 4.4

(Kac–Rice density)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field such that, for all
(x_{a})_{a\in A}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate. We define the
Kac–Rice density

\rho_{A}(f,\cdot):U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to[0,+\infty)
by:

\forall\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}},\hskip 23.49976pt\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{a\in A}\Jac\left(D_{x_{a}}f\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall a\in A,f(x_{a})=0\right]}{\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}},

where the numerator is the conditional expectation of
\prod_{a\in A}\Jac\left(D_{x_{a}}f\right)
given that
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}=0
.

Theorem 4.5

(Kac–Rice formula)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field such that:
\left(f(x),D_{x}f\right)
is non-degenerate for all
x\in U
, and
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate for all
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
. Let
\Phi:U^{A}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Borel-measurable function which is either non-negative or such that
\Phi\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(U^{A})
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu^{[A]}\,,\Phi\rangle\right]=\int_{U^{A}}\Phi(\underline{x})\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})\dmesure\!\underline{x}.

Proof.

The field
f
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
4.3
, so that
\nu^{[A]}
is an almost-surely well-defined Radon measure. Moreover,
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is well-defined outside of
{{\boxslash}}
.

Let us first consider the case
k=d
, so that
Z
is almost-surely discrete. Let
\mathcal{B}\subset U
be a Borel subset and
\Phi=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}^{A}}
be the indicator function of
\mathcal{B}^{A}\subset U^{A}
. Then
\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu^{[A]}\,,\Phi\rangle\right]
is the factorial moment of order
\lvert A\rvert
of the linear statistic
\langle\nu\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle=\card\left(Z\cap\mathcal{B}\right)
, as explained in
[
11
, p. 58]
. In this case, the result is given by
[
11
, Thm. 6.2]
if
\lvert A\rvert=1
and
[
11
, Thm. 6.3]
if
\lvert A\rvert\geqslant 2
. The proofs of these results are still valid if
\Phi
is the indicator function of any Borel subset of
U^{A}
.

If
k<d
, recall that
Z
has almost-surely dimension
d-k>0
, so that
\nu^{[A]}=\nu^{A}
by Lemma
2.25
. If
\Phi
is of the form
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}^{A}}
, the result is given by
[
11
, Thm. 6.8]
if
\lvert A\rvert=1
and
[
11
, Thm. 6.9]
if
\lvert A\rvert\geqslant 2
. Once again, the proofs are still valid if
\Phi
is the indicator function of any Borel subset of
U^{A}
.

Now that the result is proved for indicator functions of Borel sets, we recover simple functions by linearity, then non-negative functions by monotone convergence. When
\Phi\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is integrable, since
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is non-negative, the result follows by applying the previous case to the non-negative and non-positive parts of
\Phi
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

It was proved in
[
4
, Thm. 6.26]
and
[
23
, Thm. 1.13]
that, if we assume in addition that
f
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert}
and satisfies (
ND
(
\lvert A\rvert-1
)
) over
U
, then
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is locally integrable on
U^{A}
. In this case, if
\Phi\in L^{\infty}(U^{A})
has compact support, then
\Phi\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(U^{A})
and we can apply Theorem
4.5
. We will prove in Section
8.1
that, under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.7
, if
(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
are in
L^{1}(U)\cap L^{\infty}(U)
then
\phi_{A}^{\otimes}\,\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(U^{A})
, where
\phi_{A}^{\otimes}
is as in Definition
2.16
.

4.2
Evaluation maps and their kernels

The Kac–Rice densities are infamously singular along the diagonal, and a major difficulty in our work is to understand this singularity. The goal of this section is to introduce some evaluations maps, and their kernels, that we use to derive alternative expressions of the Kac–Rice densities and deal with their singularities.

In the following, we consider a non-empty finite set
A
. Recall that if
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we defined
\flat(\underline{x})
as the barycenter of
\underline{x}
and
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}=\left(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})\right)_{a\in A}
in Definition
3.17
.

Definition 4.7

(Centered evaluation map)
.

For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, let
\ev_{\underline{x}}:\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\to(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{A}
be the linear map defined by
\ev_{\underline{x}}:P\mapsto\left(P(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x}))\right)_{a\in A}
.

Lemma 4.8

(Regularity of the evaluation)
.

The map
\underline{x}\mapsto\ev_{\underline{x}}
is smooth from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k},(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{A}\right)
.

Proof.

If
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, then
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, so that
\ev_{\underline{x}}
is surjective by Corollary
3.8
. Then, we observe that the coefficients of the matrix of
\ev_{\underline{x}}
in the canonical bases of
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{A}
are monomials in
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}
, hence polynomials in
\underline{x}
. Thus
\underline{x}\mapsto\ev_{\underline{x}}
is smooth.
∎

Given a finite-dimensional vector space
V
and
n\in\llbracket 0,\dim(V)\rrbracket
, recall that the
Grassmannian

\Gr\left(n,V\right)
is the set of codimension
n
subspaces of
V
. This set has a canonical manifold structure that we use in the following. For more details on this matter, see
[
46
, Chap. 5]
or
[
37
, Ex. 1.36]
.

Definition 4.9

(Evaluation kernel)
.

For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})=\ker(\ev_{\underline{x}})
.

Lemma 4.10

(Regularity of
\mathcal{G}_{A}
)
.

The map
\mathcal{G}_{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to\Gr\left(k\lvert A\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
is smooth.

Proof.

In this proof, we use the canonical bases of
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{A}
, and the inner products making them orthonormal. For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, the lines of the matrix of
\ev_{\underline{x}}
form a basis of
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})^{\perp}
. This basis depends smoothly on
\underline{x}
by Lemma
4.8
. Locally, the Gram–Schmidt procedure yields an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
depending smoothly on
\underline{x}
. We recover
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
by considering the span of this basis, which is also a smooth operation, cf.
[
46
, Lem. 5.1 and Problem 5.A]
.
∎

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional space and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{P}_{l}(V)
the space of homogeneous polynomial functions of degree
l
on
V
. If
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is a norm on
V
, then we define a subordinated norm on
\mathfrak{P}_{l}(V)
by
\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert=\max\left\{\lvert\mathfrak{p}(v)\rvert\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}v\in V,\lVert v\rVert\leqslant 1\right\}
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}_{l}(V)
. For example, recalling Definition
4.1
, the map
\Jac^{2}:\Lambda\mapsto\det(\Lambda\Lambda^{*})
defines an element of
\mathfrak{P}_{2k}\left(\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})\right)
.

Let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we denote by
\pi_{\underline{x}}
the orthogonal projection from
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
onto
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
. Given
a\in A
, the map
D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}:P\mapsto D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}P
is linear from
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
, so that
\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{2k}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Considering these polynomials is one of the important ideas introduced in
[
23
]
. It allows us to read the conditional Jacobian appearing in Definition
4.4
as the square root of some deterministic polynomial applied to the Kergin interpolant of the field. In this way, we can isolate the contribution of the random field
f
from that of the geometry of the tuple
\underline{x}
, and better understand the behavior of this Jacobian.

Lemma 4.11

(Non-degeneracy of differentials)
.

Let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we have:

\prod_{a\in A}\left(\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}\right)\in\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert A\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\setminus\{0\}.

Proof.

We already explained that, for all
a\in A
, the map
\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
2k
on
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. The product of these maps is therefore homogeneous of degree
2k\lvert A\rvert
, and we need to check that it is non-zero.

Since
d\geqslant k
, there exists a surjective map
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Since
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, we have
\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\notin{{\boxslash}}
. Then, by Corollary
3.8
, there exists
P\in\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
such that, for all
a\in A
, we have
P(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x}))=0
and
D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}P=\Lambda_{0}
. That is,
P\in\ker(\ev_{\underline{x}})=\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
, so that
\pi_{\underline{x}}(P)=P
, and we have:

\prod_{a\in A}\left(\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}\right)(P)=\prod_{a\in A}\Jac\left(D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}P\right)^{2}=\Jac\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)^{2\lvert A\rvert}>0.\qed

We conclude this section by introducing notation for the radial and spherical parts of the map studied in Lemma
4.11
. It will be used in Section
4.3
to define alternative expressions of the Kac–Rice densities.

Definition 4.12

(Polar decomposition of products of Jacobians)
.

For all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we denote by
N_{A}(\underline{x})=\left\lVert\prod_{a\in A}\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}\right\rVert\in(0,+\infty)
and by
\theta_{A}(\underline{x})\in\mathbb{S}\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert A\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
the unit polynomial function defined by:

\theta_{A}(\underline{x}):P\mapsto\frac{1}{N_{A}(\underline{x})}\prod_{a\in A}\left(\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}\right)(P).

For all
P\in\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
we denote by
\theta_{A}(\underline{x},P)=\theta_{A}(\underline{x})(P)
for simplicity, so that if
P\in\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
then
\prod_{a\in A}\Jac\left(D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}P\right)=\sqrt{N_{A}(\underline{x})}\sqrt{\theta_{A}(\underline{x},P)}
.

Lemma 4.13

(Regularity of
N_{A}
and
\theta_{A}
)
.

The maps
\theta_{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to\mathbb{S}\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert A\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
and
N_{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to(0,+\infty)
are smooth.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
\underline{x}\mapsto\prod_{a\in A}\Jac^{2}\circ D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}\circ\pi_{\underline{x}}
is smooth from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
to
\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert A\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
, since we have already shown that it is never zero in Lemma
4.11
.

Given
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})=\ker(\ev_{\underline{x}})
is
\pi_{\underline{x}}=\Id-\ev_{\underline{x}}^{*}(\ev_{\underline{x}}\ev_{\underline{x}}^{*})^{-1}\ev_{\underline{x}}
. Composing continuous linear maps, inverting them or taking their adjoint are smooth operations. Hence
\underline{x}\mapsto\pi_{\underline{x}}
is smooth on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, by Lemma
4.8
. The map
\underline{x}\mapsto\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}=\left(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})\right)_{a\in A}
is also smooth.

Then, we observe that the function
\left(\underline{y},\Lambda,P\right)\mapsto\prod_{a\in A}(\Jac^{2}\circ D_{y_{a}}\circ\Lambda)(P)
is a polynomial on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\times\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\times\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, so that
\left(\underline{y},\Lambda\right)\mapsto\prod_{a\in A}(\Jac^{2}\circ D_{y_{a}}\circ\Lambda)
is smooth as a map with values in
\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert A\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. The conclusion follows by composing
\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}},\pi_{\underline{x}}\right)
with the previous map.
∎

4.3
Kac–Rice densities as functions of variance operators

The goal of this section is to write the Kac–Rice density
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
from Definition
4.4
in a way that allows to deal with its singularity along the diagonal. We write it as a product of two terms: the first one is a universal geometrical factor, depending only on
\underline{x}
, that fully accounts for the singularity of
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
along
{{\boxslash}}
; the second one depends on the field
f
but is not singular along
{{\boxslash}}
.

Recalling Definitions
4.7
,
4.9
and
4.12
, we define the universal and field-dependent parts of the Kac–Rice density as follows.

Definition 4.14

(Universal part of the density)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, we define

\Upsilon_{A}:\underline{x}\longmapsto\frac{\sqrt{N_{A}(\underline{x})}}{\Jac\left(\ev_{\underline{x}}\right)}

from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
to
(0,+\infty)
.

Definition 4.15

(Sets of variance operators and sets of parameters)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a partition of a finite set
A\neq\emptyset
, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
the following compact set:

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Gr\left(k\lvert I\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{S}\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert I\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

We will denote a generic element in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
by
\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)
. We also denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, if for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
we have
\mathcal{G}_{I}\in\Gr\left(k\lvert I\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
, then
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
is a subspace of codimension
k\lvert A\rvert
in
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. In particular, we can apply operators on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
to tuples of the form
(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
.

Definition 4.16

(Field-dependent part of the density)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. We define a map
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\mathcal{I}}\to[0,+\infty)
by:

\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}:\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma\right)\longmapsto\int_{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}\right)\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\right)}{\sqrt{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma\right)}}\dmesure\!\underline{P},

where
\underline{P}=(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
and
\dmesure\!\underline{P}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
induced by the inner product on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
introduced before Definition
3.27
.

An important idea is that, in the expression of
\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})
given by Definition
4.4
, we can replace
f
by its Kergin interpolant, introduced Section
3
. For this, we need to interpolate not only the values of
f
on the
(x_{a})_{a\in A}
but also those of its differential. This suggest to consider the interpolant not on
\underline{x}
but on
\underline{x}_{2A}=(\underline{x},\underline{x})
, where each point appears with double multiplicity, see Section
2.3
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open set and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field. Recalling Definition
3.17
, one can check that
\flat\left(\underline{x}_{2A}\right)=\flat(\underline{x})
and
\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{2A}}}=(\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}},\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})=\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}_{2A}
. In particular,
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})=K\left(\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f,\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}},\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}\right)=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot K(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
for all
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, see Definition
3.19
. Then, for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we have
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})=\left(\Sigma_{2I}^{2J}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}=\Var\left(\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)
, see Definition
3.27
.

Lemma 4.17

(Relation between notions of non-degeneracy)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field, let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
be such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
is positive-definite, then the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate, and
\left(f(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}f\right)
is non-degenerate for all
a\in A
.

Proof.

Our assumption on
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
means that the Gaussian vector
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
is non-degenerate. Since translations act by isomorphisms on each factor
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, this means that
\left(K(f,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
is non-degenerate. Then, applying Corollary
3.8
block-wise shows that the Gaussian vector
\left(\left(f(x_{i}),D_{x_{i}}f\right)_{i\in I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}=\left(f(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}f\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate. Hence the result.
∎

Lemma 4.18

(Kac–Rice formula revisited)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field. Let
\underline{x}\in U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
is positive-definite, then

\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Upsilon_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})\right)\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}),

where we denoted by
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}):=\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I}))_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I}))_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right)
.

Proof.

First,
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate by Lemma
4.17
. Hence
\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})
is well-defined by Definition
4.4
. Starting from this definition, we proceed in two steps.

Step 1: Replacing
f
with its twisted Kergin interpolant.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
and let us denote by
K_{I}=\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I})\in\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. We have:

K_{I}(X-\flat(\underline{x}_{I}))=\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I})(X-\flat(\underline{x}_{I}))=\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\cdot K(f,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)(X-\flat(\underline{x}_{I}))=K(f,\underline{x}_{2I}).

(4.2)

Recalling Definition
4.7
, we have:

\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}(K_{I})=\left(K_{I}(x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I}))\right)_{i\in I}=\left(K(f,\underline{x}_{2I})(x_{i})\right)_{i\in I}=\left(f(x_{i})\right)_{i\in I},

using the interpolation properties of
K(f,\underline{x}_{2I})
. Hence, we have
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}=\left(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}(K_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
, and conditioning on
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}=0
is the same as conditioning on
K_{I}\in\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})=\ker(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}})
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
i\in I
, differentiating Equation (
4.2
) at
x_{i}
and using Theorem
3.4
yields that

D_{x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})}K_{I}=D_{x_{i}}K(f,\underline{x}_{2I})=D_{x_{i}}f.

Recalling Definition
4.12
, given that
K_{I}\in\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})
, we obtain that:

\prod_{i\in I}\Jac\left(D_{x_{i}}f\right)=\prod_{i\in I}\Jac\left(D_{x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})}K_{I}\right)=\sqrt{N_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I})}.

Thus

\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{N_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I})}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall I\in\mathcal{I},K_{I}\in\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})\right]}{\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}(K_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we have an orthogonal direct sum
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}=\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})^{\perp}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})
. Accordingly, we write
K_{I}=K_{I,0}+K_{I,1}
where
K_{I,0}\in\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})^{\perp}
and
K_{I,1}\in\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})
. Let us denote by
\Lambda_{I}
the restriction of
\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}
to
\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})^{\perp}
, so that
\ev_{x_{I}}(K_{I})=\Lambda_{I}(K_{I,0})
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, the linear map
\Lambda_{I}:\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x})^{\perp}\to(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{I}
is an isomorphism. Hence, using the notation introduced in Definition
3.35
, the block-diagonal operator
\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Lambda_{I}
is an isomorphism and

\Var\left(\left(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}(K_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)=\Var\left(\left(\Lambda_{I}(K_{I,0})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)=\left(\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Lambda_{I}\right)\Var\left(\left(K_{I,0}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\left(\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Lambda_{I}\right)^{*}.

Taking the determinant on both sides yields:

\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}(K_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\right)=\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(K_{I,0}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\right)\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\det\left(\Lambda_{I}\Lambda_{I}^{*}\right).

Observing that
\Lambda_{I}\Lambda_{I}^{*}=\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}^{*}
, we get that
\det\left(\Lambda_{I}\Lambda_{I}^{*}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Jac\left(\ev_{\underline{x}_{I}}\right)
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Moreover, because of the orthogonal projection in Definition
4.12
, we have
\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I})=\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I,1})
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Finally, recalling Definition
4.14
, we proved that:

\rho_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Upsilon_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I,1})}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall I\in\mathcal{I},K_{I,0}=0\right]}{\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(K_{I,0}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Step 2: Dealing with the conditional expectation.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})
, with our choice of inner product we have
\mathcal{G}^{\perp}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})^{\perp}
and
\left(K_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}=K_{0}+K_{1}
, where
K_{0}=\left(K_{I,0}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\perp}
and
K_{1}=\left(K_{I,1}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{G}
. We can use the orthogonal splitting
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}=\mathcal{G}^{\perp}\oplus\mathcal{G}
to write
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})=\Var\left(\left(K_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)
as a block-operator
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{0}&\Sigma_{2}^{*}\\
\Sigma_{2}&\Sigma_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where
\Sigma_{0}=\Var\left(K_{0}\right)
,
\Sigma_{1}=\Var\left(K_{1}\right)
and
\Sigma_{2}=\Cov\left(K_{1},K_{0}\right)
.

Since
(K_{0},K_{1})
is a centered Gaussian with positive-definite variance
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
, the conditional distribution of
K_{1}
given that
K_{0}=0
is a centered Gaussian whose variance operator is
\widetilde{\Sigma}=\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\Sigma_{2}^{*}
, see
[
11
, Prop. 1.2]
. The operator
\widetilde{\Sigma}\in\sym^{+}(\mathcal{G})
is the Schur complement of
\Sigma_{0}
in
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})
. It is well-known that
\det\left(\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right)=\det(\Sigma_{0})\det(\widetilde{\Sigma})
and that
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}
is the bottom-right block of
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})^{-1}
. In particular, for all
\underline{P}\in\mathcal{G}
, we have
\langle\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle=\langle\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle
. Thus,

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},K_{I,1})}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall I\in\mathcal{I},K_{I,0}=0\right]}{\det\left(2\pi\Var\left(\left(K_{I,0}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
\begin{aligned} &=\frac{1}{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{\underline{P}\in\mathcal{G}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},P_{I})}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\right)}{\det\left(2\pi\widetilde{\Sigma}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\dmesure\!\underline{P}\\
&=\int_{\underline{P}\in\mathcal{G}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I},P_{I})}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\right)}{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\dmesure\!\underline{P}\\
&=\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\left(\theta_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right),\end{aligned}

which concludes the proof.
∎

Now that we explained how
\Upsilon_{A}
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
are related to
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
, the remainder of this section is dedicated to proving some properties of these maps.

Definition 4.19

(Parametric smooth maps)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a topological space (that we think of as a parameter space) and
\Omega
be an open subset of a finite-dimensional vector space
V
.

•

Let
F:\mathcal{M}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
(w,x)\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
we denote by
D^{q}_{(w,x)}F=D^{q}_{x}\left(F(w,\cdot)\right)
, if
F(w,\cdot):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is
q
-differentiable at
x
. Similarly, assuming we are given a basis of
V
, for any multi-index
\alpha
we denote by
\partial^{\alpha}F(w,x)=\partial^{\alpha}(F(w,\cdot))(x)
, if it is well-defined.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
the space of functions
F:\mathcal{M}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
(w,x)\mapsto D_{(w,x)}^{q}F
is well-defined and continuous on
\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
. Equivalently,
F\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
if and only if
\partial^{\alpha}F
is well-defined and continuous on
\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
for all
\alpha
.

Lemma 4.20

(Regularity of
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
)
.

The map
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
from Definition
4.16
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
.

Proof.

It is enough to check the regularity of
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
locally, which can be done using local charts on the Grassmannians, cf.
[
37
, Ex. 1.36]
. The proof is divided in two steps.

Step 1: Reduction to a computation in local coordinates.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0}\in\Gr\left(k\lvert I\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. The map
\Lambda_{I}\mapsto\left\{P+\Lambda_{I}(P)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}P\in\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0}\right\}
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0},(\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0})^{\perp}\right)
onto
\left\{\mathcal{G}\in\Gr\left(k\lvert I\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{G}\cap(\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0})^{\perp}=\{0\}\right\}
, which defines local coordinates centered at
\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0}
. Reading
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
in these coordinates, we need to prove that it admits continuous partial derivatives at any order with respect to
\Sigma
.

For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\Lambda_{I}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0},(\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0})^{\perp}\right)
and
\mathcal{G}_{I}
denote its graph. Then,
P\mapsto P+\Lambda_{I}(P)
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0}
to
\mathcal{G}_{I}
whose Jacobian equals
\sqrt{\det\left(\Id+\Lambda_{I}^{*}\Lambda_{I}\right)}
. Starting from the integral expression in Definition
4.16
, a change of variables yields that
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma\right)
equals:

\frac{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\det\left(\Id+\Lambda_{I}^{*}\Lambda_{I}\right)}}{\sqrt{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma\right)}}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{0}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I}+\Lambda_{I}(P_{I}))}\exp\left(-\frac{\langle\Sigma^{-1}\left(\underline{P}+\Lambda(\underline{P})\right)\,,\underline{P}+\Lambda(\underline{P})\rangle}{2}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{P},

where
\mathcal{G}^{0}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}^{0}
, we let
\underline{P}=(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{G}^{0}
and
\Lambda(\underline{P})=\left(\Lambda_{I}(P_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
, and
\dmesure\!\underline{P}
stands for the Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{G}^{0}
. What we gained is that the domain of integration no longer depends on the arguments, and this expression depends explicitly on
\Lambda=\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Lambda_{I}
.

Step 2: Regularity of
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
in the previous local coordinates.

Since the maps
\Sigma\mapsto\Sigma^{-1}
and
\left(\Lambda,\Sigma\right)\mapsto\det\left(2\pi\Sigma\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\det\left(\Id+\Lambda_{I}^{*}\Lambda_{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
are smooth, we only need to prove that

\left(\Lambda,\theta,\Sigma\right)\longmapsto\int_{\mathcal{G}^{0}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I}+\Lambda_{I}(P_{I}))}\exp\left(-\frac{\langle\Sigma\left(\underline{P}+\Lambda(\underline{P})\right)\,,\underline{P}+\Lambda(\underline{P})\rangle}{2}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{P}

(4.3)

admits continuous partial derivatives at any order with respect to
\Sigma
, where
\theta=\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
.

The integrand in (
4.3
) is continuous with respect to
\left(\Lambda,\theta,\Sigma,\underline{P}\right)
, as well as its partial derivatives with respect to
\Sigma
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\lVert P_{I}+\Lambda_{I}(P_{I})\rVert\leqslant(1+\lVert\Lambda_{I}\rVert)\lVert P_{I}\rVert
and, since
\theta_{I}
has unit norm,
\lvert\theta_{I}\left(P_{I}+\Lambda_{I}(P_{I})\right)\rvert\leqslant(1+\lVert\Lambda_{I}\rVert)\lVert P_{I}\rVert
. This is enough to obtain dominating functions with Gaussian decay with respect to
\lVert\underline{P}\rVert
, uniformly with respect to
(\Lambda,\theta,\Sigma)
in any compact subset of
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{G}^{0}_{I},(\mathcal{G}^{0}_{I})^{\perp}\right)\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{S}\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert I\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. Hence the result.
∎

Lemma 4.21

(Regularity of
\Upsilon
)
.

Let
A
be non-empty and finite, the function
\Upsilon_{A}
from Definition
4.14
is continuous on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
and locally integrable on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
. Moreover, we have
\tau\cdot\Upsilon_{A}=\Upsilon_{A}
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

The continuity follows from Definition
4.14
and from Lemmas
4.8
and
4.13
. Let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{A})^{d}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
. By Definition
4.7
, we have
\ev_{\tau\cdot\underline{x}}=\ev_{\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}}=\ev_{\underline{x}}
. Then, we get
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})
. And finally
N_{A}(\tau\cdot\underline{x})=N_{A}(\underline{x})
by Definition
4.12
. Thus
\Upsilon_{A}(\tau\cdot\underline{x})=\Upsilon(\underline{x})
for all
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is
\Upsilon_{A}
is invariant under the action of diagonal translations. The difficulty is in proving the local integrability, which is done in two steps.

Step 1: Reduction to lower bounding
\sigma_{\{A\}}
.

Let us consider a standard Gaussian vector
f_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. On the one hand,
f_{0}
defines a smooth centered Gaussian field from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. On the other hand, if
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we have
K(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})=f_{0}
by uniqueness in Theorem
3.4
. Hence the Gaussian vector
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot K(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot f_{0}
is non-degenerate, since
P\mapsto\flat(\underline{x})\cdot P
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
. Thus, we have
\Sigma_{\{2A\}}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})=\Var\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\{A\}}
for all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
. Then, by Lemma
4.18
, we have
\rho_{A}(f_{0},\cdot)=\Upsilon_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\{A\}}(f_{0},\cdot)
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
.

Let
\Gamma\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
be compact. We know from
[
4
, Thm. 6.26]
and
[
23
, Thm. 1.13 and Lem. 2.9]
that
\rho_{A}(f_{0},\cdot)
is locally integrable on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
. Indeed, in this case, the non-degeneracy of
f_{0}
implies that of its multijets up to order
2\lvert A\rvert
. In particular
\rho_{A}(f_{0},\cdot)
is integrable on
\Gamma
. In order to prove that
\Upsilon_{A}
is integrable on
\Gamma
, it is thus enough to show that the non-negative function
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\{A\}}(f_{0},\cdot)
is bounded from below on
\Gamma\setminus{{\boxslash}}
by some constant
C>0
.

Step 2: Existence of a lower bound.

We prove the existence of
C>0
by contradiction. If there exists no such bound, there is a sequence
\left(\underline{x}^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\Gamma\setminus{{\boxslash}}
such that
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\{A\}}\left(f_{0},\underline{x}^{(n)}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
. By compactness of
\Gamma
, up to extracting a subsequence, we can assume that
\underline{x}^{(n)}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\underline{x}\in\Gamma
. By Remark
3.28
, we have
\Sigma_{\{2A\}}\left(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A}^{(n)}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\Sigma_{\{2A\}}\left(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\{A\}}
. By compactness of
\mathcal{M}_{\{A\}}
, see Definition
4.15
, we can also assume that
\left(\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)}),\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})\right)\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\left(\mathcal{G},\theta\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\{A\}}
. Finally by continuity of
\sigma_{\{A\}}
, see Lemma
4.20
, we have:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\{A\}}\left(f_{0},\underline{x}^{(n)}\right)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sigma_{\{A\}}\left(\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)}),\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)}),\Sigma_{\{2A\}}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A}^{(n)})\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\{A\}}\left(\mathcal{G},\theta,\Sigma_{\{2A\}}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})\right)=\int_{\mathcal{G}}\sqrt{\theta(P)}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma_{\{2A\}}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})^{-1}P\,,P\rangle\right)}{\sqrt{\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{\{2A\}}(f_{0},\underline{x}_{2A})\right)}}\dmesure\!P.

Hence
\theta_{|\mathcal{G}}=0
. We will prove that this cannot happen.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\lVert\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})\rVert=1
. Hence, there exists
P_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
such that
\lVert P_{n}\rVert\leqslant 1
and
\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)},P_{n})=1
. Its orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})
satisfies
\lVert\pi_{\underline{x}^{(n)}}(P_{n})\rVert\leqslant\lVert P_{n}\rVert\leqslant 1
and
\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)},\pi_{\underline{x}^{(n)}}(P_{n}))=\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)},P_{n})=1
, see Definition
4.12
. Up to replacing
P_{n}
by
\pi_{\underline{x}^{(n)}}(P_{n})
, we can assume
P_{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})
. By compactness of the unit ball, we can also assume that
P_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}P
.

As above, we work in a chart on
\Gr\left(k\lvert A\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\mathcal{G}
, for
n
large enough there exists
\Lambda_{n}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\perp})
such that
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})
is the graph of
\Lambda_{n}
, and moreover
\Lambda_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
. Let
Q_{n}\in\mathcal{G}
denote the orthogonal projection of
P_{n}
onto
\mathcal{G}
. Since
P_{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})
, we have
P_{n}=Q_{n}+\Lambda_{n}(Q_{n})
. Then,

\lVert P_{n}-Q_{n}\rVert=\lVert\Lambda_{n}(Q_{n})\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert\lVert Q_{n}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert\lVert P_{n}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0,

so that
Q_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}P
, and thus
P\in\mathcal{G}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1-\theta(P)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)},P_{n})-\theta(P)\right\rvert\leqslant\left\lvert\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)},P_{n})-\theta(P_{n})\right\rvert+\lvert\theta(P_{n})-\theta(P)\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\theta_{A}(\underline{x}^{(n)})-\theta\right\rVert+\lvert\theta(P_{n})-\theta(P)\rvert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0,

by continuity of
\theta
. Thus
\theta(P)=1
and
\theta_{|\mathcal{G}}\neq 0
, which is the contradiction we sought.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a Gaussian field, where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open and convex.

•

Let
\underline{y}\in U^{A}
be such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{y}_{2A})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. By continuity of
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\cdot)
, there exists a compact neighborhood
\Gamma
of
\underline{y}
such that
\left\{\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in\Gamma\right\}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. In particular, the expression of
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
derived in Lemma
4.18
is valid for all
\underline{x}\in\Gamma\setminus{{\boxslash}}
. Then, we deduce from Remark
3.28
and Lemmas
4.10
,
4.13
,
4.20
and
4.21
that
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is continuous on
\Gamma\setminus{{\boxslash}}
.

•

By continuity of
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\cdot)
the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}\times\left\{\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in\Gamma\right\}
is compact. Since
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is continuous, it is bounded on this set, hence
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\cdot)
is bounded on
\Gamma\setminus{{\boxslash}}
. This substantiate our claim that the singularity of
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
is fully accounted for by the universal term
\underline{x}\mapsto\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\Upsilon_{I}(\underline{x}_{I})
.

To conclude this section we prove that, if
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
is block-diagonal, then
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(w,\Sigma)
is a product of terms of the same form. Let us introduce some notation before stating this result.

Definition 4.23

(Frobenius inner product)
.

Let
V
and
W
be Euclidean spaces, the
Frobenius inner product
on
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
is defined by
\left\langle\Lambda_{1}\,,\Lambda_{2}\right\rangle=\Tr\left(\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}^{*}\right)
for all
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(V,W)
.

Let
A\neq\emptyset
be finite and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Recall that we equipped
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
with an inner product such that the factors are orthogonal. Self-adjoint operators in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
have a natural block structure adapted to this product, and the Frobenius inner product on this space is such that
\langle\Sigma\,,\Lambda\rangle=\sum_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\langle\Sigma_{I}^{J}\,,\Lambda_{I}^{J}\rangle
for all
\Sigma=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
and
\Lambda=\left(\Lambda_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 4.24

(Block-diagonal variance operators)
.

Let
A
be a finite set and
B\subset A
be a proper subset, that is, such that
\emptyset\neq B\neq A
. Let
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\left\{B,A\setminus B\right\}
, we denote by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}

\displaystyle=\left\{\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall I\in\mathcal{I}_{B},\forall J\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B},\Sigma_{I}^{J}=0\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}.

For the Frobenius inner product, the orthogonal of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
is:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}=\left\{\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}=0\ \text{and}\ (\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}=0\right\}.

If
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
is such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
, then
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{B}\sqcup\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
so that

\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}=\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}

and operators on this space have an associated
2\times 2
block structure. Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
) is the subspace of block-diagonal (resp. block-anti-diagonal) operators with respect to
\{B,A\setminus B\}
.

Lemma 4.25

(Exact clustering)
.

Let
A
be finite, let
B
be a proper subset of
A
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
. For all
\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
, we have:

\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma\right)=\prod_{C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{C}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{C}},(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
. For all
C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}
, let us denote by
\Sigma_{C}=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}
, so that
\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{B}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{A\setminus B}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
