by blocks, with respect to
\{B,A\setminus B\}
. Then
\det\left(2\pi\Sigma\right)=\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{B}\right)\det\left(2\pi\Sigma_{A\setminus B}\right)
and
\Sigma^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{B}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. For all
\underline{P}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, we have:

\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{B}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}}\\
\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}}\\
\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\rangle=\sum_{C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}}\langle\Sigma_{C}^{-1}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\,,\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\rangle,

hence
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}=\prod_{C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma_{C}^{-1}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\,,\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\right\rangle}
. Since
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{B}\sqcup\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
, products indexed by
I\in\mathcal{I}
can be splitted into two products indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{B}
and
\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
respectively. Then, starting from Definition
4.16
and applying Fubini’s Theorem, we get:

\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},\Sigma\right)=\prod_{C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}}\int_{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}\mathcal{G}_{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}\right)\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma_{C}^{-1}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\,,\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}\rangle}}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi\Sigma_{C})}}\dmesure\!\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{C}}.\qed

Lemma 4.26

(Vanishing of the differential)
.

Let
A
be finite, let
B
be a proper subset of
A
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
. For all
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
, for all
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
, we have
D_{(w,\Sigma)}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\Lambda=0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+})
and that by differential we mean differential with respect to the second variable. Let
w=\left((\mathcal{G}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}},(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
, then
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(w,\cdot)=\varphi\psi
, where

\displaystyle\varphi:\Sigma\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi\Sigma)}}

and

\displaystyle\psi:\Sigma\mapsto\int_{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\right)\dmesure\!\underline{P}

are smooth maps from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
to
[0,+\infty)
. Let
\Sigma=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
and
\Lambda=\left(\Lambda_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
. By Leibniz’ Rule, in order to prove that
D_{(w,\Sigma)}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\Lambda=0
it is enough to prove that
D_{\Sigma}\varphi\cdot\Lambda=0
and
D_{\Sigma}\psi\cdot\Lambda=0
. This is what we do in the following two steps.

Step 1: Differential of
\varphi
.

The differential of
\det
at
2\pi\Sigma
is
M\mapsto\det(2\pi\Sigma)\Tr\left(\Sigma^{-1}\frac{M}{2\pi}\right)
. By the Chain Rule, we obtain that
D_{\Sigma}\varphi\cdot\Lambda=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Tr(\Sigma^{-1}\Lambda)}{\sqrt{\det(2\pi\Sigma)}}
.

As above, let
\Sigma_{C}=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{C}}
for
C\in\{B,A\setminus B\}
. We have
\Sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma_{B}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{A\setminus B}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
since
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
. Let us also denote by
\Lambda_{0}=\left(\Lambda_{I}^{J}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B},J\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}
, so that
\Lambda=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\Lambda_{0}^{*}\\
\Lambda_{0}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
since
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
. Then

\Tr(\Sigma^{-1}\Lambda)=\Tr\left(\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{B}^{-1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Lambda_{0}^{*}\\
\Lambda_{0}&0\end{pmatrix}\right)=\Tr\begin{pmatrix}0&\Sigma_{B}^{-1}\Lambda_{0}^{*}\\
\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\Lambda_{0}&0\end{pmatrix}=0,

and finally
D_{\Sigma}\varphi\cdot\Lambda=0
.

Step 2: Differential of
\psi
.

We will prove below that, for all
M\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
,

D_{\Sigma}\psi\cdot M=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}\right)\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\right)\dmesure\!\underline{P}.

(4.4)

With the same notation as above, we have
\Sigma^{-1}\Lambda\Sigma^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\Sigma_{B}^{-1}\Lambda_{0}^{*}\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\\
\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\Lambda_{0}\Sigma_{B}^{-1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Hence, for all
\underline{P}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
, we have
\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\Lambda\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle=2\langle\Sigma_{A\setminus B}^{-1}\Lambda_{0}\Sigma_{B}^{-1}\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}}\,,\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\rangle
. Assuming that (
4.4
) holds for
M=\Lambda
, the integrand is then an odd function of
\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}}=(P_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}
when
\underline{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}
is fixed. Indeed, the
(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}
are homogeneous polynomial functions of even degree. Thus the integral of this function vanishes and
D_{\Sigma}\psi\cdot\Lambda=0
.

Let us now prove that (
4.4
) holds. Let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough that
\Sigma+\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, where
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
denotes the closed ball of center
0
and radius
\varepsilon
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Recall that the differential of the smooth map
M\mapsto M^{-1}
at
\Sigma
is
M\mapsto-\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1}
. By Taylor’s Theorem, there exists
C>0
such that, for all
M\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
\lVert(\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}\rVert\leqslant C\lVert M\rVert
and
\lVert(\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}+\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1}\rVert\leqslant C\lVert M\rVert^{2}
. Since
\Sigma
is positive-definite, up to increasing
C
, we can assume that
\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\rangle\geqslant\frac{1}{C}\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}
for all
\underline{P}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
. Finally, up to decreasing
\varepsilon
, we can assume that
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{2C^{2}}
.

The second order Taylor expansion of
\exp
at
0
yields that, for all
\underline{P}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
and
M\in\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}
:

\left\lvert\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right)-1+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert\\
\begin{aligned} &\leqslant\left\lvert\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert^{2}e^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert}\\
&\leqslant\left\lVert(\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}\right\rVert^{2}\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{4}e^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert(\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}\right\rVert\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}}\leqslant C^{2}\lVert M\rVert^{2}\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{4}e^{\frac{C\varepsilon}{2}\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}}.\end{aligned}

Thus,

\left\lvert e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle(\Sigma+M)^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert\\
\begin{aligned} \leqslant&e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}\left\lvert\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right)-1+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert\\
&+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}\left\lvert\left\langle((\Sigma+M)^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}+\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1})\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle\right\rvert\\
\leqslant&C^{2}\lVert M\rVert^{2}\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{4}e^{-\frac{\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}}{2C}\left(1-C^{2}\varepsilon\right)}+e^{-\frac{\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}}{2C}}C\left\lVert M\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\underline{P}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lVert M\rVert^{2}e^{-\frac{\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}}{4C}}\left(1+C\lVert\underline{P}\rVert^{2}\right)^{2}.\end{aligned}

Since
(\theta_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
are polynomials in
\underline{P}
, multiplying the previous terms by
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}
and integrating over
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}
yields:

\left\lvert\psi(\Sigma+M)-\psi(\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{G}_{I}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{\theta_{I}(P_{I})}\right)\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}M\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\Sigma^{-1}\underline{P}\,,\underline{P}\right\rangle}\dmesure\!\underline{P}\right\rvert=O(\lVert M\rVert^{2})

as
M\to 0
. This proves that
D_{\Sigma}\psi
is indeed given by Equation (
4.4
), and concludes the proof.
∎

4.4
Kac–Rice densities for cumulants

In the previous section, we dealt with Kac–Rice densities for factorial moments. The goal of the present one is to introduce densities that play the same role for cumulants. In all this section, we consider a convex open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
.

Definition 4.27

(Cumulant Kac–Rice density)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field such that, for all
(x_{a})_{a\in A}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate. We define the
cumulant Kac–Rice density

\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\cdot):U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}\to\mathbb{R}
by:

\forall\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}},\hskip 23.49976pt\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\rho_{J}(f,\underline{x}_{J}),

where the
(\rho_{J})
were defined in Definition
4.4
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{J}}=(-1)^{\lvert\mathcal{J}\rvert-1}\left(\lvert\mathcal{J}\rvert-1\right)!
for all
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. If
A=\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, we let
\mathcal{F}_{p}=\mathcal{F}_{A}
.

Like the Kac–Rice density
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
from Definition
4.4
, the function
\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\cdot)
is a priori singular along the diagonal. In order to work with this singularity, we will prove a result analogous to Lemma
4.18
for
\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\cdot)
. Recalling the notation for blocks with respect to a coarser partition introduced in Definition
2.9
, we define the following.

Definition 4.28

(Parameter set of projections)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, we denote by

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}=\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert[J]_{\mathcal{I}}\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right).

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
I=[J]_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{I}
. Recalling Definition
3.29
and Lemma
3.30
.
2
, we have
\Pi_{2J}^{2I}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2I})\in\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
for all
\underline{x}_{I}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{I}
. Typical elements of
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}
we are interested in are those of the form
\left(\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
, where
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
, see Definition
2.6
. For all
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
\left(\Pi_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}
, we denote by
\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\in\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
the map defined by:

\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}:\left(P_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\longmapsto\left(\Pi_{J}(P_{[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}.

(4.5)

Note that there is a slight abuse of notation with respect to Definition
3.35
. Indeed, we have
\mathcal{J}_{K}=\left\{J\in\mathcal{J}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}J\subset K\right\}=\left\{I\cap K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\in\mathcal{I}\right\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
so that, formally, the source space of
\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}
should be a product indexed by
\left\{[J]_{\mathcal{I}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}\right\}=\left\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\cap K\neq\emptyset\right\}
instead of
\mathcal{I}
. Here we first implicitly project onto
\prod_{\left\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\cap K\neq\emptyset\right\}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, then apply the block-diagonal operator with diagonal blocks
\left(\Pi_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}
, which is surjective since its diagonal blocks are.

Definition 4.29

(Field-dependent part of the cumulant density)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, we define a function
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
by

F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}:\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\Pi_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma\right)\longmapsto\\
\sum_{\left\{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}\right\}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\sigma_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},(\textstyle\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J})\Sigma(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J})^{*}\right),

where the maps
(\sigma_{\mathcal{J}_{K}})
were defined in Definition
4.16
.

The cumulant Kac–Rice density can be written in terms of the field-dependent parts we just introduced. This relation uses the functions introduced in Definitions
3.29
,
4.9
and
4.12
.

Lemma 4.30

(Relation between
\mathcal{F}_{A}
and
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field, and
\underline{x}\in U^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
be such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, then

\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J}),

where
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})=F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\right)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.17
, since
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
we have that
(f(x_{a}))_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate, hence
\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\underline{x})
is well-defined. We want to apply Lemma
4.18
to each factor in the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\underline{x})
. For this, we first need to check the non-degeneracy of a family of variance operators.

Let
B\subset A
be non-empty. For all
J\in\mathcal{I}_{B}
, there exists a unique
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
J=I\cap B
, and
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2J})=\Pi_{2J}^{2I}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I}),\underline{x}_{2I}\right)
by Lemma
3.30
.
1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}_{B}}(f,\underline{x}_{2B})

\displaystyle=\Var\left(\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{B}}\right)=\Var\left(\left(\Pi_{2(I\cap B)}^{2I}\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{2I}),\underline{x}_{2I}\right)\right)_{\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mid I\cap B\neq\emptyset\}}\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mid I\cap B\neq\emptyset\}}\Pi_{2(I\cap B)}^{2I}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\left(\bigoplus_{\{I\in\mathcal{I}\mid I\cap B\neq\emptyset\}}\Pi_{2(I\cap B)}^{2I}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2I})\right)^{*},

with the same abuse of notation as in (
4.5
). By Lemma
3.30
.
2
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
I\cap B\neq\emptyset
the map
\Pi_{2(I\cap B)}^{2I}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2I})
is surjective. Hence, the direct sum of these maps is surjective and, since
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, we have
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}_{B}}(f,\underline{x}_{2B})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}}^{+}
for all non-empty
B\subset A
.

Then, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we can use Lemma
4.18
for each block of
\mathcal{K}
. This yields:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\rho_{K}(f,\underline{x}_{K})=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{K})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{K})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{K}),

where the third equality follows from
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\bigsqcup_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{I}_{K}
. Then, if
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{K}
are such that
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
, for all
K\in\mathcal{K}
we have
\mathcal{I}_{K}=\left\{I\cap K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}I\in\mathcal{I}\right\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}=\mathcal{J}_{K}
, and Equation (
4.6
) yields:

\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{2K})=\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)^{*}.

Finally, recalling the definitions of
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})
and
\left(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{K})\right)_{\emptyset\neq K\subset A}
we recognize that

\sum_{\left\{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}\right\}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}(f,\underline{x}_{K})=\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x}).\qed

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open subset and
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered Gaussian field. Let
\Omega\subset U
be open, we let
\nu
denote the Riemannian volume measure of
Z=f^{-1}(0)\cap\Omega
, and define its linear statistics by (
4.1
).

Definition 4.31

(Cumulants of linear statistics)
.

Let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
be a finite family of Borel measurable functions on
\Omega
. We denote by
\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})=\kappa\left((\langle\nu\,,\phi_{a}\rangle)_{a\in A}\right)
whenever this cumulant is well-defined, see Definition
2.12
.

Let us express these cumulants in terms of the cumulant Kac–Rice densities. Recall that we defined
\mathcal{P}_{A,n}
in Definition
2.24
.

Proposition 4.32

(Kac–Rice formula for cumulants)
.

Let
A\neq\emptyset
be finite and
q=\max(2,2\lvert A\rvert-1)
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{q}(U,\mathbb{R}^{k})
be a centered Gaussian field and
\eta>0
be such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2A})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap\Omega^{A}
. Let
\underline{\phi}=\left(\phi_{a}\right)_{a\in A}
be Borel-measurable functions. We assume that for all non-empty
B\subset A
, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
, for all
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, the function

\underline{x}\longmapsto\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})

is integrable over
\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
. Then,

\displaystyle\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\int_{\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}}\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{A}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\\
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\ \int_{\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{A}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}.

Proof.

There are two parts to the proof. The first one is to check that our assumptions ensure that we can use Theorem
4.5
when we need to. The second one is computational.

Step 1: Checking the hypotheses of Theorem
4.5
.

For all
\underline{x}\in\Omega^{A}
, there exists
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, see Definition
2.31
. Then, by Lemma
4.17
, for all
\underline{x}\in\Omega^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
we have that
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate. Moreover,
\left(f(x),D_{x}f\right)
is non-degenerate for all
x\in\Omega
. Thus the non-degeneracy hypotheses in Theorem
4.5
are satisfied. Let us focus on the integrability ones.

Let
B\subset A
be non-empty and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
. Let
\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
. By Definition
4.27
, for all non-empty
I\subset\mathcal{K}
, we have:

\mathcal{F}_{I}(f,\underline{x}_{I})=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{I}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\rho_{J}(f,\underline{x}_{J}).

By Möbius inversion, see Proposition
2.10
, we deduce that
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{F}_{I}(f,\underline{x}_{I})
. Using Lemma
2.8
.
1
, we can re-index this sum by the set of partitions of
B
that are coarser than
\mathcal{K}
. That is, for all
\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, we have
\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{a\in K}\phi_{a}(x_{K})\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}\\
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}\end{subarray}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}\\
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}\end{subarray}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}_{J}}\prod_{a\in K}\phi_{a}(x_{K})\right).

By Fubini’s Theorem, in order to prove that
\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}\right)\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\Omega^{\mathcal{K}})
, it is enough to prove that
\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{J}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\Omega^{\mathcal{K}_{J}})
for all
\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}
in
\mathcal{P}_{B}
and all
J\in\mathcal{J}
. Note that
\emptyset\neq J\subset A
and
\mathcal{K}_{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{J,d-k}
. We want the former integrability condition to be satisfied for all non-empty
B\subset A
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
. By the previous discussion, it is enough to prove that: for all non-empty
B\subset A
, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
, we have
\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\Omega^{\mathcal{K}})
.

Let
B\subset A
be non-empty and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
. Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, by Lemma
4.30
we have :

\forall\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta},\hskip 23.49976pt\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J}).

Our integrability assumptions ensure that each term on the right-hand side is integrable over
U^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, hence so is the left-hand side. This holds for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, hence
\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\cdot)
is integrable over
\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}
for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
, where
\emptyset\neq B\subset A
. Finally, our integrability assumptions imply that
\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{B}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}\right)\rho_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\Omega^{\mathcal{K}})
for all non-empty
B\subset A
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{B,d-k}
.

Step 2: Computation of the cumulants.

By Definitions
2.12
,
2.21
and
4.31
and Lemma
2.25
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in J}\langle\nu\,,\phi_{j}\rangle\right]=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu^{J}\,,\phi_{J}^{\otimes}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\sum_{\mathcal{K}_{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{J,d-k}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu^{[\mathcal{K}_{J}]}\,,\phi_{J}^{\otimes}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}\right\rangle\right].

Let
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\in\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{P}_{J,d-k}
. If
d>k
, then
\mathcal{K}_{J}=\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{J}
for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{K}_{J}=\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{A}
is the only element of
\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}
. Otherwise
\mathcal{K}=\bigsqcup_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{K}_{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}
, and each
\mathcal{K}\leqslant\mathcal{J}
in
\mathcal{P}_{A}
can be uniquely realized in this way, see Lemma
2.8
.
2
. Re-indexing the previous sum, we obtain:

\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\sum_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu^{[\mathcal{K}_{J}]}\,,\phi_{J}^{\otimes}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}\right\rangle\right].

We checked in the first step of the proof that we can apply the Kac–Rice formula, see Theorem
4.5
, to compute each factor on the right-hand side. Hence,

\displaystyle\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\sum_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\int_{\Omega^{\mathcal{K}_{J}}}\left(\phi_{J}^{\otimes}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})\right)\rho_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})\dmesure\!\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\int_{\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}}\left(\phi_{A}^{\otimes}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\sum_{\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}}\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\rho_{\mathcal{K}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{\mathcal{K}_{J}})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}}\int_{\underline{x}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}}\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{A}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{x})\dmesure\!\underline{x},

where we used Lemma
2.8
.
1
to replace the sum over
\{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mid\mathcal{J}\geqslant\mathcal{K}\}
by a sum over
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
and recognized
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\cdot)
, see Definition
4.27
. This establishes the first expression of
\kappa(\nu)(\underline{\phi})
.

In order to establish the second one, for each
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A,d-k}
, we split the integral over
\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}
into a sum, indexed by
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, of integrals over
\Omega^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, see Definition
2.31
. Then, the result follows from Lemma
4.30
.
∎

The results we established for
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
at the end of Section
4.3
have natural analogues for the functions
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
. We state and prove these results in the remainder on the present section.

Lemma 4.33

(Regularity of
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, then
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left((\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger})\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.29
it is enough to prove that, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
and all
K\in\mathcal{K}
, the following map belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left((\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger})\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
:

\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\Pi_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma\right)\mapsto\sigma_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)\Sigma\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)^{*}\right).

(4.7)

Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
and
K\in\mathcal{K}
. We have
\mathcal{J}_{K}=\left\{J\in\mathcal{J}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}J\subset K\right\}\subset\mathcal{J}
since
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{K}
. Recalling Definition
4.15
, the parameter space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}
is then a factor in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}
. The projection
\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\right)\mapsto\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\right)
from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}
onto
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}
is continuous. The map
\left(\left(\Pi_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma\right)\mapsto\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)\Sigma\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)^{*}
is smooth from
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}^{+}
. Then, by Lemma
4.20
, the map (
4.7
) indeed belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left((\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger})\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
.
∎

Recalling the notation introduced in Definition
4.24
, we can now prove that the map
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
vanishes at second order along well-chosen subspaces.

Lemma 4.34

(Vanishing of
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
A
be a finite set and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Let
B
be a proper subset of
A
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\left\{B,A\setminus B\right\}
. Then, for all
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}
, for all
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
we have
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)=0
and
D_{(w,\Sigma)}F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\cdot\Lambda=0
.

Proof.

The key idea is that projection operators of the form (
4.5
) preserve the block structure of the operators
\Sigma
and
\Lambda
, in a sense to be made precise below. Thus, we can deduce the result from Lemmas
4.25
and
4.26
. We deal first with the vanishing of the function, then with that of its transverse derivatives.

Step 1: Vanishing of
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
.

For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
\emptyset\neq K\subset I
, let
\mathcal{G}_{K}\in\Gr\left(k\lvert K\rvert,\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert K\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
,
\theta_{K}\in\mathbb{S}\mathfrak{P}_{2k\lvert K\rvert}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert K\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
and
\Pi_{K}\in\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert K\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. For all
\mathcal{K}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, we denote by
w_{\mathcal{K}}=\left((\mathcal{G}_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}},(\theta_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}},(\Pi_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{K}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},\mathcal{I}}
. In particular,
w_{\mathcal{J}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}
, and every element of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}
can be realized in this way.

For all
K\subset A
, let us denote by
\varepsilon_{K}=1
if
K\in\mathcal{J}
and
\varepsilon_{K}=0
otherwise. Then, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we have
\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\varepsilon_{K}=1
if
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
and
\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\varepsilon_{K}=0
otherwise. Let
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
, for every non-empty
K\subset A
, we denote by

m_{K}=\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)\Sigma\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)^{*}\right)\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\varepsilon_{I}.

Then, recalling that
\bigsqcup_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{I}_{K}=\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}
for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we compute:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}m_{K}=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}\right)_{\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)\Sigma\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)^{*}\right)\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\varepsilon_{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{J}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}\right)_{\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{\mathcal{I}_{K}},\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)\Sigma\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{I}\right)^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\\
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{J}\right)F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(w_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}},\Sigma)=F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w_{\mathcal{J}},\Sigma).

Indeed, if
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{I}_{K}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{K}
for all
K\in\mathcal{K}
. We will prove below that
m_{K}=m_{K\cap B}m_{K\setminus B}
for all
K\subset A
, with the convention that
m_{\emptyset}=1
. Then, it will follow from Lemma
2.11
that
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w_{\mathcal{J}},\Sigma)=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}m_{K}=0
.

Let
K\subset A
. If
K\cap B=\emptyset
or
K\setminus B=\emptyset
, then
m_{K}=m_{K\cap B}m_{K\setminus B}
because
m_{\emptyset}=1
. In the following, we denote by
L=K\cap B
and assume that
\emptyset\neq L\neq K
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{K}
, we denote by
\widetilde{I}
the unique block of
\mathcal{I}
such that
I=\widetilde{I}\cap K
. Since
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\left\{B,A\setminus B\right\}
, we have either
\widetilde{I}\subset B
or
\widetilde{I}\subset(A\setminus B)
. In the first case
I=\widetilde{I}\cap K\subset L
, and in the second
I\subset K\setminus L
. Thus
\mathcal{I}_{K}\leqslant\left\{L,K\setminus L\right\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{K}=\mathcal{I}_{L}\sqcup\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{I}_{L}=(\mathcal{I}_{K})_{L}=\left\{\widetilde{I}\cap K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\widetilde{I}\in\mathcal{I}_{B}\right\}\setminus\left\{\emptyset\right\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}=(\mathcal{I}_{K})_{K\setminus L}=\left\{\widetilde{I}\cap K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\widetilde{I}\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}\right\}\setminus\left\{\emptyset\right\}
.

Let us denote by
\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
the blocks of
\Sigma
. Then, recalling Equation (
4.5
), we have:

\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)\Sigma\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)^{*}=\left(\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}.

(4.8)

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
and
J\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
, then
\widetilde{I}\in\mathcal{I}_{B}
and
\widetilde{J}\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
, and we have
\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}=0
since
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
, see Definition
4.24
. Hence,
\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)\Sigma\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)^{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}_{K},L}^{+}
. By Lemma
4.25
,

m_{K}=\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{L}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{L}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{L}},\left(\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{L}}\right)\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{L}}\varepsilon_{I}\\
\quad\times\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}}\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}},\left(\theta_{I}\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}},\left(\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}}\right)\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}}\varepsilon_{I}.

Applying (
4.8
) with
\mathcal{I}_{K}
replaced by
\mathcal{I}_{L}
and
\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
respectively, we recognize this product to be
m_{L}m_{K\setminus L}=m_{K\cap B}m_{K\setminus B}
. Thus,
m_{K}=m_{K\cap B}m_{K\setminus B}
for all
K\subset A
and, by Lemma
2.11
, we have
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w_{\mathcal{J}},\Sigma)=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}m_{K}=0
.

Step 2: Vanishing of
DF_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
.

Let
w=\left((\mathcal{G}_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}},(\theta_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}},(\Pi_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}
and
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{K}\geqslant\mathcal{J}
and
K\in\mathcal{K}
, we denote by
w_{K}^{\prime}=\left((\mathcal{G}_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}},(\theta_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\right)
and
\Sigma_{K}=\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)\Sigma\left(\bigoplus_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{K}}\Pi_{J}\right)^{*}
. With this notation, Definition
4.29
becomes:

F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)=\sum_{\left\{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}\right\}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\sigma_{\mathcal{J}_{K}}(w^{\prime}_{K},\Sigma_{K}).

Since the
\Sigma\mapsto\Sigma_{K}
is linear for all
K\in\mathcal{K}\geqslant\mathcal{J}
, differentiating the previous expression yields that, for all
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}
, all
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
and all
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we have:

D_{(w,\Sigma)}F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\cdot\Lambda=\sum_{\left\{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}\right\}}\mu_{\mathcal{K}}\sum_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\left(\prod_{L\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{K\}}\sigma_{\mathcal{J}_{L}}\left(w^{\prime}_{L},\Sigma_{L}\right)\right)D_{(w^{\prime}_{K},\Sigma_{K})}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}\cdot\Lambda_{K}.

(4.9)

Let us fix
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
such that
\mathcal{I}\wedge\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
and
K\in\mathcal{K}
. For all
I\in\mathcal{J}_{K}=\mathcal{I}_{K}
, let us denote by
\widetilde{I}
the only element of
\mathcal{I}
such that
I=\widetilde{I}\cap K
. Writing
\Sigma=\left(\Sigma_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
and
\Lambda=\left(\Lambda_{I}^{J}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
as block-operators we obtain:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{K}

\displaystyle=\left(\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}}

and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}

\displaystyle=\left(\Pi_{I}\circ\Lambda_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}\right)_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{K}},

as in Equation (
4.8
). Let us assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\mathcal{I},B}
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
. Denoting by
L=K\cap B
, we have as above
\mathcal{I}_{K}=\mathcal{I}_{L}\sqcup\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
and
J\in\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
, have
\widetilde{I}\in\mathcal{I}_{B}
and
\widetilde{J}\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
, and
\Pi_{I}\circ\Sigma_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}=0
. Thus
\Sigma_{K}\in\mathcal{S}^{+}_{\mathcal{I}_{K},L}
. For all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}_{L}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{K\setminus L}
), we have
\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J}\in\mathcal{I}_{B}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
), and
\Pi_{I}\circ\Lambda_{\widetilde{I}}^{\widetilde{J}}\circ\Pi_{J}^{*}=0
. Thus
\Lambda_{K}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}_{K},L}^{\perp}
. Then, by Lemma
4.26
, we have
D_{(w^{\prime}_{K},\Sigma_{K})}\sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{K}}\cdot\Lambda_{K}=0
.

Finally, if
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{+}
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
, then each term on the right-hand side of (
4.9
) vanishes, and
D_{(w,\Sigma)}F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\cdot\Lambda=0
.
∎

Lemma
4.34
shows that
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
vanishes at second order along the subspace
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
, which is defined by the vanishing of the off-diagonal blocks
(\Sigma_{I,J})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B},J\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}
of
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. Using Taylor formulas, one can hope to deduce estimates of the type
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)=O\left(\max_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B},J\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{2}\right)
from this result. Actually, we can derive a better estimate by patching together estimates of the previous type for all
B\subset A
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
. This is the object of the next section.

5
Refined Hadamard lemma

The purpose of this section to derive a refinement of the Hadamard lemma for smooth functions defined on an open subset of a Euclidean space and vanishing at second order along a well-chosen family of subspaces. The upshot is to deduce from this result an estimate for the functions
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
, see Definition
4.29
and Lemma
4.34
.

In section
5.1
, we introduce a notation of minimality for multi-indices indexed by a finite set. We use this notion of minimal multi-indices to state and prove a refined version of the Hadamard lemma for smooth function on a Euclidean space in Section
5.2
. Finally, in Section
5.3
, we deduce a key estimate for
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
from this refined Hadamard lemma.

5.1
Minimal multi-indices

Recall that we introduced multi-indices indexed by
\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
in Definition
3.11
. In the following, we need to consider multi-indices indexed by a more general finite set. In this section, we first introduce the associated notation. Then, we define a set of minimal multi-indices and prove some of its properties. In all this section,
A
is a non-empty finite set.

Definition 5.1

(Multi-indices indexed by
A
)
.

The set of
multi-indices
indexed by
A
is
\mathbb{N}^{A}
. For all
\underline{n}=(n_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
, we denote by
\underline{n}!=\prod_{a\in A}n_{a}!
and by
\lvert\underline{n}\rvert=\sum_{a\in A}n_{a}
its
length
. If
B\subset A
, we let
\underline{n}_{B}=(n_{b})_{b\in B}\in\mathbb{N}^{B}
. Finally, for all
a\in A
, we denote by
\mathbf{1}_{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
the multi-index having a
1
in position
a
and zeros elsewhere.

Remark 5.2
.

If
B\subset A
, there is a canonical injection mapping
\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{B}
to the only
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
such that
\underline{n}_{B}=\underline{m}
and
\underline{n}_{A\setminus B}=0
. Using this injection, we consider
\mathbb{N}^{B}
as a subset of
\mathbb{N}^{A}
in the following.

Recall that the canonical order on
\mathbb{N}
induces a partial order on
\mathbb{N}^{A}
, such that
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
if and only if
m_{a}\leqslant n_{a}
for all
a\in A
. Accordingly, we write
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
if
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
and
\underline{m}\neq\underline{n}
.

Definition 5.3

(Minimal multi-indices)
.

Let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathbb{N}^{A}
, we say
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}
is
minimal
in
\mathcal{N}
if for all
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
we have
\underline{m}=\underline{n}
. We denote by
\min(\mathcal{N})
the set of minimal elements of
\mathcal{N}
.

Lemma 5.4

(Existence of minimal elements)
.

Let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathbb{N}^{A}
and
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, there exists
\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N})
such that
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
.

Proof.

The set
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\left\{\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}\right\}
is non-empty and finite. Hence it admits a minimal element, say
\underline{m}_{0}
. If
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}
is such that
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{m}_{0}
, then
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, and hence
\underline{m}=\underline{m}_{0}
. Thus
\underline{m}_{0}\in\min(\mathcal{N})
.
∎

In the following, we consider a family
\mathcal{B}
of non-empty subsets of
A
. That is
\mathcal{B}\subset 2^{A}
and
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{B}
. Note that we do not assume the elements of
\mathcal{B}
to be disjoint as subsets of
A
. We can associate with
\mathcal{B}
the following set of minimal multi-indices.

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}=\left\{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\forall B\in\mathcal{B},\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 2\right\}.

(5.1)

Lemma 5.5

(Description of
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
)
.

If
\mathcal{B}\subset 2^{A}
is such that
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{B}
, then
\emptyset\neq\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})\subset\llbracket 0,2\rrbracket^{A}
. In particular
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
is finite. Moreover, for all
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
and
a\notin\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}B
we have
n_{a}=0
.

Proof.

Since
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{B}
, we have
(2,\dots,2)\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
. By Lemma
5.4
, there exists
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\underline{n}\leqslant(2,\dots,2)
. In particular
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})\neq\emptyset
.

Let
\underline{n}=(n_{a})_{a\in A}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
. We define
\underline{m}=(m_{a})_{a\in A}\in\llbracket 0,2\rrbracket^{A}
by
m_{a}=\min(n_{a},2)
if
a\in\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}B
and
m_{a}=0
otherwise. We have
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
. For all
B\in\mathcal{B}
, if there exists
b\in B
such that
n_{b}\geqslant 2
, then
\lvert\underline{m}_{B}\rvert\geqslant m_{b}=2
; otherwise
\underline{m}_{B}=\underline{n}_{B}
, and
\lvert\underline{m}_{B}\rvert=\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 2
. This proves that
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
, and hence
\underline{m}=\underline{n}
by minimality of
\underline{n}
. The conclusion follows.
∎

Example 5.6
.

Let us give some examples.

1.

If
\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbb{N}^{A}
and
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})=\{0\}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{B}=\{B\}
, then
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})=\left\{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{B}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lvert\underline{n}\rvert=2\right\}=\left\{\mathbf{1}_{b}+\mathbf{1}_{c}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}b,c\in B\right\}\subset\mathbb{N}^{A}
.

3.

If
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{2,3\}\right\}\subset 2^{\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket}
then
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})=\left\{(1,1,1),(2,2,0),(2,0,2),(0,2,2)\right\}
.

5.2
Refined Hadamard lemma in
\mathbb{R}^{A}

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{A}
be an open subset. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a locally compact topological space. The goal of the present section is to prove a refined version of the Hadamard lemma which is adapted to functions in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
vanishing at second order along several subspaces in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{A}
, see Definition
4.19
. This is done in Proposition
5.8
below.

Consistently with the notation introduced in Definitions
3.11
and
5.1
, we let
\partial_{a}
denote the partial derivative with respect to the coordinate indexed by
a\in A
, which acts on
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
. For all
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
and
\underline{n}=(n_{a})_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
, we denote by
\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}=\prod_{a\in A}x_{a}^{n_{a}}
. Given
B\subset A
and
\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{B}
, we have
\underline{x}^{\underline{m}}=(\underline{x}_{B})^{\underline{m}}
thanks to the inclusion
\mathbb{N}^{B}\subset\mathbb{N}^{A}
, see Remark
5.2
. Similarly,
\partial^{\underline{m}}
acts naturally on
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
.

We start by proving a parametric version of the Hadamard lemma for functions of
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
vanishing at order
2
along
\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{A}
, in the case where
\Omega
is the product of two balls. In the following, we use the convention that
\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}=\{0\}
.

Lemma 5.7

(Parametric Hadamard Lemma)
.

Let
B\subset A
be non-empty. Let
\Omega=U\times U^{\prime}
, where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}
and
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{B}
are open balls, and
0\in U^{\prime}
. Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
be such that: for all
(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
such that
\underline{x}_{B}=0
and
\underline{h}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{B}
, we have
F(w,\underline{x})=0
and
D_{(w,\underline{x})}F\cdot\underline{h}=0
. Then, there exist functions
(F_{\underline{m}})_{\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{B},\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=2}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
such that

F:(w,\underline{x})\longmapsto\sum_{\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{B},\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=2}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}}F_{\underline{m}}(w,\underline{x}).

Proof.

Let
w\in\mathcal{M}
and
\underline{x}\in\Omega=U\times U^{\prime}
. Since
U^{\prime}
is convex, we can write the first-order Taylor expansion with integral remainder of the function
\underline{y}\mapsto F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},\underline{y})
between
0
and
\underline{x}_{B}
. Our hypotheses imply that its first-order Taylor polynomial at
0
vanishes, hence

F(w,\underline{x})=\sum_{\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{B},\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=2}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}}\underbrace{\frac{2}{\underline{m}!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\partial^{\underline{m}}F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},t\underline{x}_{B})\dmesure\!t}_{=F_{\underline{m}}(w,\underline{x})}.

Let
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{A}
, the function
(w,\underline{x},t)\mapsto(1-t)t^{\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert}\partial^{\underline{m}+\underline{n}}F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},t\underline{x}_{B})
is continuous. Thus, for any compact subset
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
it can be dominated by a constant on
\Gamma\times[0,1]
. This is enough to prove that the parametric integral
F_{\underline{m}}
admits continuous partial derivatives at any order on the interior of
\Gamma
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is locally compact, so is
\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
. Hence
F_{\underline{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
.
∎

As in Section
5.1
, let
\mathcal{B}\subset 2^{A}
be a family of non-empty subsets of
A
. Recall that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}\subset\mathbb{N}^{A}
is defined by Equation (
5.1
) and that
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
is finite by Lemma
5.5
.

Proposition 5.8

(Refined Hadamard lemma)
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
be such that, for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
: for all
(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
such that
\underline{x}_{B}=0
and
\underline{h}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{B}
, we have
F(w,\underline{x})=0
and
D_{(w,\underline{x})}F\cdot\underline{h}=0
. Then there exist functions
\left(F_{\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
such that

F:(w,\underline{x})\longmapsto\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}F_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\card(\mathcal{B})
. If
\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
then
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})=\{0\}
, see Example
5.6
.
1
, and we get the result by letting
F_{0}=F
. Let us assume that
\card(\mathcal{B})\geqslant 1
and let
\emptyset\neq B\in\mathcal{B}
. The induction hypothesis applied to
\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}
yields functions
\left(G_{\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
such that
F:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})
. In order to establish the induction step, we consider three cases.

Case 1:
\Omega
is a product of balls intersecting
\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}\times\{0\}
.

We assume that
\Omega=U\times U^{\prime}
, where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}
and
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{B}
are open balls, and that
0\in U^{\prime}
.

For all
(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
, we have
(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
, hence

0=F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert=0\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0).

(5.2)

Moreover, for all
b\in B
, we have
\partial_{b}F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)=D_{(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)}F\cdot\mathbf{1}_{b}=0
, so that:

0=x_{b}\partial_{b}F(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\underline{n}_{B}=\mathbf{1}_{b}\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\underline{n}_{B}=0\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}x_{b}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0).

Summing over
b\in B
, we obtain:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert=1\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{b\in B}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}x_{b}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)=0.

(5.3)

Let
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
, the map
(w,\underline{x})\mapsto G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})-G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)-\sum_{b\in B}x_{b}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.7
. Thus, recalling Example
5.6
.
2
, there exist functions
\left(H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
such that

G_{\underline{n}}:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{b\in B}x_{b}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}}H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

Then, using Equations (
5.2
) and (
5.3
), for all
(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
we have

F(w,\underline{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 2\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{b\in B}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}x_{b}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)\\
+\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}\sum_{\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}+\underline{n}}H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

Let
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
be such that
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 2
. We have
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
, hence there exists
\underline{p}_{\underline{n}}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\underline{p}_{\underline{n}}\leqslant\underline{n}
, see Lemma
5.4
. Similarly, let
b\in B
and
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
be such that
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 1
. We have
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}}
, hence
\underline{n}+\mathbf{1}_{b}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}}
. Besides
\lvert(\underline{n}+\mathbf{1}_{b})_{B}\rvert=\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert+1\geqslant 2
, so that
\underline{n}+\mathbf{1}_{b}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
. By Lemma
5.4
, there exists
\underline{q}_{\underline{n},b}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\underline{q}_{\underline{n},b}\leqslant\underline{n}+\mathbf{1}_{b}
. Finally, given
\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})
and
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
, we have
\underline{m}+\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Hence, there exists
\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\leqslant\underline{m}+\underline{n}
. For all
\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
, we define
F_{\underline{p}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
by:

F_{\underline{p}}:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 2;\ \underline{p}_{\underline{n}}=\underline{p}\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}-\underline{p}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})\\
\lvert\underline{n}_{B}\rvert\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in B\\
\underline{q}_{\underline{n},b}=\underline{p}\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}+\mathbf{1}_{b}-\underline{p}}\partial_{b}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}_{A\setminus B},0)\\
+\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})\\
\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}=\underline{p}\end{subarray}}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}+\underline{n}-\underline{p}}H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

Then,
F:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\sum_{\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{p}}F_{\underline{p}}(w,\underline{x})
is the expansion we are looking for in this case.

Case 2:
\Omega
is a product of balls disjoint from
\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}\times\{0\}
.

Let us deal with a second special case. We assume that
\Omega=U\times U^{\prime}
, where
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{A\setminus B}
and
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{B}
are open balls, and
0\notin U^{\prime}
.

In this case,
\underline{x}\mapsto\lVert\underline{x}_{B}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{b\in B}x_{b}^{2}
is a positive smooth function on
\Omega
. Recall that we described
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})
in Example
5.6
.
2
. Let
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
and
\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})
. If there exists
b\in B
such that
m_{b}=2
, we define
H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\frac{1}{\lVert\underline{x}_{B}\rVert_{2}^{2}}G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})
. Otherwise we let
H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}=0
. We have
H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
for all
(\underline{m},\underline{n})\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})\times\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
and, for all
(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega
,

F(w,\underline{x})=\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}\left(\sum_{b\in B}x_{b}^{2}\frac{G_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})}{\lVert\underline{x}_{B}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)=\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}});\,\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}+\underline{n}}H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

As above, for all
\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}})
and
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})
, there exists
\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}\leqslant\underline{m}+\underline{n}
. Then,
F:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\sum_{\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{p}}F_{\underline{p}}(w,\underline{x})
where, for all
\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
, we defined:

F_{\underline{p}}:(w,\underline{x})\longmapsto\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B\}})}\ \sum_{\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\{B\}});\,\underline{r}_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}=\underline{p}}\underline{x}^{\underline{m}+\underline{n}-\underline{p}}H_{\underline{m},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

This is the expansion of
F
we are looking for in this second case.

Case 3: General case.

Let us now consider the case where
\Omega
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{A}
. In this case,
\Omega
admits an open cover
\left(\Omega_{i}\right)_{i\in\aleph}
, where each
\Omega_{i}
is of a type considered in one of the previous two special cases. Let
\left(\chi_{i}\right)_{i\in\aleph}
be a smooth partition of unity subordinate to
\left(\Omega_{i}\right)_{i\in\aleph}
, cf.
[
37
, Thm. 2.23]
. Given
i\in\aleph
, we can apply one the previous two cases to the restriction of
F
to
\mathcal{M}\times\Omega_{i}
. This yields functions
\left(F_{i,\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega_{i}\right)
such that

\forall(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega_{i},\qquad F(w,\underline{x})=\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}F_{i,\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

Let
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})
, for all
i\in\aleph
the function
(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\chi_{i}(\underline{x})F_{i,\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})
extends into a function of
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
vanishing outside
\mathcal{M}\times\Omega_{i}
. Since each compact of
\Omega
intersects the support of finitely many of the
(\chi_{i})_{i\in\aleph}
, the map
F_{\underline{n}}:(w,\underline{x})\mapsto\sum_{i\in\aleph}\chi_{i}(\underline{x})F_{i,\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}(\mathcal{M}\times\Omega)
. Then,

\forall(w,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{M}\times\Omega,\qquad F(w,\underline{x})=\sum_{i\in\aleph}\chi_{i}(\underline{x})\left(\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}F_{i,\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x})\right)=\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}})}\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}F_{\underline{n}}(w,\underline{x}).

This establishes the induction step in the general case, and concludes the proof.
∎

5.3
Refined Hadamard lemma for
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}

In this section, we state a version of Proposition
5.8
adapted to the maps
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
from Definition
4.29
. As a consequence, we derive an estimate for these maps, which will be fundamental in the proof of Theorem
1.7
. This is done in Proposition
5.10
below, which is a generalization of
[
25
, Lem. 3.22]
.

In all this section, let
A
be a non-empty finite set, and let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Recall that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}
were defined in Definitions
4.15
and
4.28
respectively. Recall also that
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left((\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger})\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
, see Lemma
4.33
.

For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we defined the inner product on
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
so that
\left(X^{\alpha}\otimes e_{l}\right)_{\lvert\alpha\rvert<2\lvert I\rvert,1\leqslant l\leqslant k}
is an orthonormal basis, where
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This basis is indexed by:

\mathcal{A}_{I}=\left\{(I,\alpha,l)\in\{I\}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lvert\alpha\rvert<2\lvert I\rvert\right\}.

(5.4)

Since we use the product Euclidean structure on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, we obtain an orthonormal basis of this space by juxtaposing the previous bases of each factor. The resulting basis is indexed by:

\mathcal{A}=\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{A}_{I}.

(5.5)

In the following, we identify a self-adjoint operator
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
with its matrix
\left(\lambda_{ab}\right)_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}
in the previous basis. The map
\Lambda\mapsto\left((\lambda_{aa})_{a\in\mathcal{A}},(\sqrt{2}\lambda_{ab})_{\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}}\right)
is well-defined since
\left(\lambda_{ab}\right)_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}
is symmetric. In particular,
\widetilde{A}=\mathcal{A}\sqcup\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}
naturally indexes the coefficients on or above the diagonal in the matrix of
\Lambda
, the subset
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}
) indexing to diagonal (resp. off-diagonal) coefficients. One can check that the previous map is an isometric isomorphism from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
equipped with the Frobenius inner product (see Definition
4.23
) to
\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
with its standard Euclidean structure. Using these Euclidean coordinates, one can see
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
as an open subset in
\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
.

Let
B
be a proper subset of
A
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
. Recall that
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{B}\sqcup\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}
. We denote by
\widetilde{B}\subset\widetilde{A}
the set of indices associated with coefficients in the off-diagonal block above the diagonal in the
2\times 2
block-decomposition induced by the partition
\{B,A\setminus B\}
. That is,

\widetilde{B}=\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{B},J\in\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}\subset\widetilde{A},

(5.6)

where we used the canonical inclusion from Remark
2.2
. Note that
\widetilde{B}\neq\emptyset
. We also denote by

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}=\left\{\widetilde{B}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\varsubsetneq B\varsubsetneq A\ \text{such that}\ \mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}\right\}.

(5.7)

Using this notation, a special case of Proposition
5.8
is the following. Recall that
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}})
is a finite set of multi-indices indexed by
\widetilde{A}
, see Equation (
5.1
) and Lemma
5.5
.

Corollary 5.9

(Refined Hadamard lemma for
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Then there exists a family
\left(F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}})}
of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{0,\infty}\left((\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger})\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\right)
such that, for all
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}
and
\underline{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
we have:

F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\underline{\Sigma})=\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}})}\underline{\Sigma}^{\underline{n}}F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\underline{n}}(w,\underline{\Sigma}).

Proof.

As explained previously, we identify any self-adjoint operator
\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
with the vector of its Euclidean coordinates
\underline{\Lambda}=\left((\lambda_{aa})_{a\in\mathcal{A}},(\sqrt{2}\lambda_{ab})_{\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
, where
(\lambda_{ab})_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}
is the matrix of
\Lambda
in the orthonormal basis discussed above. Under this identification, for any proper
B\subset A
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
, the subspace
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}
from Definition
4.24
corresponds to
\{\underline{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}\mid\underline{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{B}}=0\}
, see Equation (
5.6
). Similarly,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I},B}^{\perp}
corresponds to
\{\underline{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}\mid\underline{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{A}\setminus\widetilde{B}}=0\}
.

Thus, Lemma
4.34
translates into the fact that for all
\widetilde{B}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}
the following holds: for all
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}
, all
\underline{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
such that
\underline{\Sigma}_{\widetilde{B}}=0
and all
\underline{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
such that
\underline{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{A}\setminus\widetilde{B}}=0
we have
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\underline{\Sigma})=0
and
D_{(w,\underline{\Sigma})}F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\cdot\underline{\Lambda}=0
. The conclusion follows by Proposition
5.8
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a non-empty finite set. For any proper
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, recall that
\mathcal{W}\times(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})
can be seen as a subset of
\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
thanks to the inclusion from Remark
2.2
. We define:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}=\left\{\mathcal{W}\times(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\varsubsetneq\mathcal{W}\varsubsetneq\mathcal{V}\right\}\subset 2^{(\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2})}.

(5.8)

Note that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}=\emptyset
if and only if
\card(\mathcal{V})=1
, and that
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Let us now consider
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. If
B\subset A
is a proper subset such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
, then
\mathcal{I}_{B}
is a proper subset of
\mathcal{I}
. Conversely, any proper subset of
\mathcal{I}
can be obtained in this way, compare Lemma
2.8
.
1
in the case of partitions with exactly two blocks. Thus, in this case, the previous definition can be rewritten as follows:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}=\left\{\mathcal{I}_{B}\times\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\varsubsetneq B\varsubsetneq A\ \text{such that}\ \mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}\right\}\subset 2^{(\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2})}.

(5.9)

A consequence of Corollary
5.9
is the following key estimate.

Proposition 5.10

(Key estimate for
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\neq\{A\}
. For any compact
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, there exists
C_{\Gamma}\geqslant 0
such that:

\forall(w,\Sigma)\in\Gamma,\qquad\left\lvert F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)\right\rvert\leqslant C_{\Gamma}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})}\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{n_{\{I,J\}}},

where
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
by blocks, and
\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert=\lVert\Sigma_{J}^{I}\rVert
stands for the common operator norm of these linear maps, subordinated to the Euclidean ones.

Proof.

We start from the expansion we established in Corollary
5.9
. By continuity of the functions
\left(F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\underline{n}}\right)_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})}
on the compact
\Gamma
, there exists
C\geqslant 0
such that

\forall(w,\Sigma)\in\Gamma,\qquad\left\lvert F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)\right\rvert\leqslant C\sum_{\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}})}\lvert\underline{\Sigma}^{\underline{n}}\rvert,

where
(\Sigma_{ab})_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}
is the matrix of
\Sigma
in the orthonormal basis discussed at the beginning of this section, and
\underline{\Sigma}=\left((\Sigma_{aa})_{a\in\mathcal{A}},(\sqrt{2}\Sigma_{ab})_{\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{A}}
.

Let
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}})
. It is a multi-index indexed by

\widetilde{A}=\mathcal{A}\sqcup\mathcal{A}^{\curlywedge 2}=\mathcal{A}\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{I})^{\curlywedge 2}\right)\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}\right),

(5.10)

see (
5.4
) and (
5.5
). By Equations (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), we have
\left(\mathcal{A}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{I})^{\curlywedge 2}\right)\cap\left(\bigcup_{\widetilde{B}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\widetilde{B}\right)=\emptyset
. Hence, by Lemma
5.5
, we have
\underline{n}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
, and
\underline{n}_{(\mathcal{A}_{I})^{\curlywedge 2}}=0
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Note that
\mathcal{A}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{A}_{I})^{\curlywedge 2}
is the set indexing the coefficients in the diagonal blocks of the matrix of
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
. We just proved that these coefficients do not appear in the product
\underline{\Sigma}^{\underline{n}}
. Thus, given
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, we have:

\lvert\underline{\Sigma}^{\underline{n}}\rvert=\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\prod_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}\left\lvert\sqrt{2}\Sigma_{ab}\right\rvert^{n_{\{a,b\}}}\leqslant\sqrt{2}^{\lvert\underline{n}\rvert}\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}\rvert}.

Indeed, if
I\neq J
and
(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}
, then
\Sigma_{ab}
is a coefficient in the matrix of the block
\Sigma_{I}^{J}
. With our choice of norms, we have
\lvert\Sigma_{ab}\rvert\leqslant\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert=\lVert\Sigma_{J}^{I}\rVert
. In particular, there is no ambiguity coming from the fact that we consider unordered pairs.

Since
\mathcal{I}\neq\{A\}
, we have
\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}\neq\emptyset
. Let us define
\underline{m}=(m_{\{I,J\}})\in\mathbb{N}^{(\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2})}
by
m_{\{I,J\}}=\left\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}\right\rvert
for all
\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
. Let
B
be a proper subset of
A
such that
\mathcal{I}\leqslant\{B,A\setminus B\}
. We have:

\lvert\underline{m}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}\times\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\rvert=\sum_{(I,J)\in\mathcal{I}_{B}\times\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}m_{\{I,J\}}=\sum_{(I,J)\in\mathcal{I}_{B}\times\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\sum_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}n_{\{a,b\}}=\lvert\underline{n}_{\widetilde{B}}\rvert\geqslant 2,

(5.11)

since
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
, see Equations (
5.1
), (
5.6
) and (
5.7
). Hence
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}}
, see Equation (
5.9
).

Let us prove that
\underline{m}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
. By Lemma
5.4
, there exists
\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
such that
\underline{p}\leqslant\underline{m}
. We have
p_{\{I,J\}}\leqslant\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}\rvert
for all
\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
. Thanks to the splitting of
\widetilde{A}
given by (
5.10
), there exists
\underline{q}\in\mathbb{N}^{\widetilde{A}}
such that
\underline{q}\leqslant\underline{n}
and, for all
