\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
,
\left\lvert\underline{q}_{\mathcal{A}_{I}\times\mathcal{A}_{J}}\right\rvert=p_{\{I,J\}}
. The same computation as (
5.11
) shows that, for all
\widetilde{B}\in\widetilde{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we have
\lvert\underline{q}_{\widetilde{B}}\rvert=\lvert\underline{p}_{\mathcal{I}_{B}\times\mathcal{I}_{A\setminus B}}\rvert\geqslant 2
. Hence,
\underline{q}\in\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
. By minimality of
\underline{n}
we have
\underline{q}=\underline{n}
, which implies
\underline{m}=\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
. Hence, for all
(w,\Sigma)\in\Gamma
, we have:

\lvert\underline{\Sigma}^{\underline{n}}\rvert\leqslant\sqrt{2}^{\lvert\underline{n}\rvert}\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{m_{\{I,J\}}}\leqslant\sqrt{2}^{\lvert\underline{n}\rvert}\sum_{\underline{p}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})}\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{p_{\{I,J\}}}.

By Lemma
5.5
, the cardinality of
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
is at most
3\lvert\widetilde{A}\rvert
, and moreover
\lvert\underline{n}\rvert\leqslant 2\lvert\widetilde{A}\rvert
for all
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{I}})
. Letting
C_{\Gamma}=3C\lvert\widetilde{A}\rvert 2^{\lvert\widetilde{A}\rvert}
, we conclude the proof by summing the previous inequalities over
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
.
∎

6
Edge-connected graphs

The goal of this section is to recall some standard facts about graphs and to relate indexing sets of the form
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
to graphs. This is done in Section
6.1
. The graphs we consider satisfy a property reinforcing connectedness, called
2
-edge-connectedness. We prove in Section
6.2
that
2
-edge-connectedness implies the existence of a nice family of spanning trees.

6.1
Graphs and edge-connectedness

We consider non-empty finite graphs that have no loops but may have multiple edges with the same endpoints. We are interested in keeping track of the labels of the vertices, but we do not care about the labels of the edges. This leads to the following definition, compare
[
57
, Def. 1.1.2]
.

Definition 6.1

(Graph)
.

A
graph

G
is a couple
\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{n}\right)
where
\mathcal{V}
is a non-empty finite set and
\underline{n}=(n_{\{u,v\}})_{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}}
is a multi-index in
\mathbb{N}^{(\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2})}
. Elements of
\mathcal{V}
are called the
vertices
of
G
. For all
e=\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
, the integer
n_{e}
is the number of
edges
between
u
and
v
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}_{G}=\left\{e\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}n_{e}\geqslant 1\right\}
the set of pairs of vertices that are the
endpoints
of at least one edge.

Since Definition
6.1
is but one among many possible variations, we recall the following standard definitions for the reader’s convenience.

Definition 6.2

(Path, cycle and spanning tree)
.

Let
G=\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{n}\right)
be a graph.

1.

We say that a graph
H=(\mathcal{W},\underline{m})
is a
sub-graph
of
G
, and we write
H\subset G
, if
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
and
\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
. Note that
\underline{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{(\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2})}
since the first condition implies
\mathcal{W}^{\curlywedge 2}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
, see Remark
5.2
.

2.

Let
l\geqslant 2
. A
cycle
of length
l
in
G
is a sub graph of the type
\left(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\},\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i-1},v_{i}\}}\right)
, where
v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\in\mathcal{V}
are pairwise distinct and we denoted
v_{0}=v_{l}
.

3.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
u,v\in\mathcal{V}
. A
path
of length
l
between
u
and
v
in
G
is a sub graph of the type
\left(\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\},\sum_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i-1},v_{i}\}}\right)
; where
v_{0}=u
,
v_{l}=v
and
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{l}\in\mathcal{V}
are pairwise distinct. The vertices
(v_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant l-1}
are called the
internal vertices
of the path.

4.

A connected graph without cycle is called a
tree
. In particular, a tree has no multiple edge.

5.

We say that a sub graph
T=(\mathcal{W},\underline{m})\subset G
is a
spanning tree
of
G
if
T
is a tree and
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{V}
.

We are interested in a class of graphs satisfying a property reinforcing connectedness, that we now introduce. This is a special case of the notion of
k
-edge-connectedness, see
[
57
, Def. 4.1.7]
.

Definition 6.3

(
2
-edge-connectedness)
.

We say that a graph
G=\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{n}\right)
is
2
-edge-connected
if it is connected and remains so after removing any of its edges. That is, for all
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
such that
\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=\lvert\underline{n}\rvert-1
, the graph
\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{m}\right)
is connected. We say that
G
is
minimally
2
-edge-connected
if it is
2
-edge-connected and, for all
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
, the graph
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
is not
2
-edge-connected.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a non-empty finite set. Recall that we defined
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
in Equation (
5.8
) and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
in Equation (
5.1
). With this in mind, we can characterize
2
-edge-connected graphs as follows.

Lemma 6.4

(Characterization of
2
-edge-connectedness)
.

A non-trivial graph
G=\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{n}\right)
is
2
-edge-connected (resp. minimally
2
-edge-connected) if and only if
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
(resp.
\underline{n}\in\min\left(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}\right)
).

Proof.

Since we assume
G
to be non-trivial, we have
\card(\mathcal{V})\geqslant 2
and
\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\neq\emptyset
. Let us first prove that
G=(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})
is connected if and only if, for all proper
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, we have
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant 1
. Recall that
\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)\subset\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
by Remark
2.2
.

Step 1: Characterization of connectedness.

Let us assume that
G
is connected. Let
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
be a proper subset. Let
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
. By connectedness, there exists a path from
w
to
v
in
G
. Let
w=v_{0},\dots,v_{l}=v
denote the distinct vertices of this path, ordered as in Definition
6.2
.
3
. There exists a smallest
j\in\llbracket 1,l\rrbracket
such that
v_{j}\notin\mathcal{W}
. Then, there is at least one edge of
G
between
v_{j-1}\in\mathcal{W}
and
v_{j}\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
, and
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant n_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}\geqslant 1
. Thus, for all proper
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, we have
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant 1
.

Conversely, let us assume that
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant 1
for all
\emptyset\varsubsetneq\mathcal{W}\varsubsetneq\mathcal{V}
. Let
u\in\mathcal{V}
, we denote by

\mathcal{W}=\left\{w\in\mathcal{V}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{there exists a path between}\ u\ \text{and}\ w\ \text{in}\ G\right\},

the connected component of
u
. For all
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
v\notin\mathcal{W}
we have
n_{\{v,w\}}=0
. Since
u\in\mathcal{W}
, if we had
\mathcal{W}\neq\mathcal{V}
, then
\mathcal{W}
would be a proper subset of
\mathcal{V}
such that
\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}=0
, which would contradict our hypothesis. Hence
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{V}
, and
G
is connected.

Step 2: Characterization of (minimal)
2
-edge-connectedness.

Assuming that
G
is
2
-edge-connected, let
\mathcal{W}
be a proper subset of
\mathcal{V}
. Since
G
is connected,
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant 1
. Hence there exists
e\in\mathcal{W}\times(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W})
such that
n_{e}\geqslant 1
. Letting
\underline{m}=\underline{n}-\mathbf{1}_{e}
, our hypothesis ensures that
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
is also connected, hence
\lvert\underline{m}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant 1
and
\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert=\lvert\underline{m}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert+1\geqslant 2
. This holds for all proper
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, hence
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
.

Conversely, let us assume that
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
. Let
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
be such that
\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=\lvert\underline{n}\rvert-1
. Then, there exists a pair
e\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
such that
\underline{m}=\underline{n}-\mathbf{1}_{e}
. For all proper subset
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{V}
, we have
\lvert\underline{m}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert\geqslant\lvert\underline{n}_{\mathcal{W}\times\left(\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right)}\rvert-1\geqslant 1
. Hence
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
is connected. This establishes the
2
-edge-connectedness of
G
, and concludes the proof of the first equivalence.

Let us now assume that
G
is
2
-edge-connected graph, that is,
\underline{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
. If
G
is minimally
2
-edge-connected, for all
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
, the graph
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
is not
2
-edge-connected, hence
\underline{m}\notin\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
by the characterization we just proved. Thus
\underline{n}\in\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}})
. Conversely, if
\underline{n}
is minimal in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
, then for all
\underline{m}<\underline{n}
we have
\underline{m}\notin\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}}
, and hence
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
is not
2
-edge-connected. Thus,
G
is minimally
2
-edge-connected. This concludes the proof the second equivalence.
∎

Example 6.5
.

Here are some useful examples.

•

A tree is not
2
-edge-connected unless it is trivial.

•

Cycles are minimally
2
-edge-connected.

Definition 6.6

(Set of minimally
2
-edge-connected graphs)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a non-empty finite set, we denote by
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
the finite set of minimally
2
-edge-connected graphs with vertex set
\mathcal{V}
. Since the set of vertices is explicit in this notation, we often denote
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
instead of
(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

If
\card(\mathcal{V})=1
, then
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
contains only the trivial graph. Otherwise,
(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})\mapsto\underline{n}
is a bijection from
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
to
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{V}}})
by Lemma
6.4
. By Lemma
5.5
, this shows that
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{V}}
is indeed finite. This also allows us to restate Proposition
5.10
as follows.

Corollary 6.7

(Key estimate with graphs)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. For any compact
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}^{\dagger}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, there exists
C_{\Gamma}\geqslant 0
such that:

\forall(w,\Sigma)\in\Gamma,\qquad\left\lvert F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(w,\Sigma)\right\rvert\leqslant C_{\Gamma}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}}\prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert^{n_{\{I,J\}}},

where
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{I}^{J})_{I,J\in\mathcal{I}}
, and
\lVert\Sigma_{I}^{J}\rVert=\lVert\Sigma_{J}^{I}\rVert
are operator norms subordinated to the Euclidean ones.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{I}\neq\{A\}
we have
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 2
, and the result follows from Proposition
5.10
by re-indexing the sum, using the bijection between
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\min(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}})
we just described. If
\mathcal{I}=\{A\}
, then
\lvert\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}\rvert=1
and
\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}=\emptyset
. In this case, we just need to prove that
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
is bounded on the compact
\Gamma
, which follows from its continuity, see Lemma
4.33
.
∎

6.2
Ear decompositions and spanning trees

An interesting feature of
2
-edge-connected graphs is that they admit a
closed-ear decomposition
, cf.
[
57
, Thm. 4.2.10]
. Such a decomposition allows us to prove that a
2
-edge-connected graph admits a nice family of spanning trees, which is the main result of this section, see Lemma
6.9
.

Lemma 6.8

(Closed-ear decomposition)
.

Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})
be a non-trivial
2
-edge-connected graph. There exist
p\in\mathbb{N}
and non-trivial sub-graphs
G_{0}=(\mathcal{V}_{0},\underline{n}^{(0)})
, …,
G_{p}=(\mathcal{V}_{p},\underline{n}^{(p)})
of
G
satisfying the following properties.

1.

The graph
G_{0}
is a cycle whose length, denoted by
l_{0}
, is maximal among cycles in
G
.

2.

For all
q\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
,
G_{q}
is either a cycle of length at most
l_{0}
with exactly one vertex in
\bigcup_{i=0}^{q-1}\mathcal{V}_{i}
or a path of length less than
l_{0}
between vertices of
\bigcup_{i=0}^{q-1}\mathcal{V}_{i}
with no internal vertex in
\bigcup_{i=0}^{q-1}\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

3.

We have
\mathcal{V}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{p}\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\underline{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{p}\underline{n}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

We build
(G_{q})_{0\leqslant q\leqslant p}
by induction. Since
G
is
2
-edge-connected and non-trivial, it is connected but it is not a tree, hence
G
admits cycles. The length of these cycles is bounded by
\lvert\underline{n}\rvert
, hence there exists one whose length
l_{0}
is maximal. We denote by
G_{0}=(\mathcal{V}_{0},\underline{n}^{(0)})
any such cycle, so that Condition
1
holds. In particular
\underline{n}^{(0)}\leqslant\underline{n}
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
. We assume that we have built sub-graphs
G_{0}=(\mathcal{V}_{0},\underline{n}^{(0)})
, …,
G_{q}=(\mathcal{V}_{q},\underline{n}^{(q)})
satisfying Conditions
1
and
2
, and such that
\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}\leqslant\underline{n}
.

We first consider the case where
\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}=\underline{n}
. Let
v\in\mathcal{V}
, since
G
is connected and non-trivial, there exists
u\neq v
such that
n_{\{u,v\}}\geqslant 1
. Hence, there exists
i\in\llbracket 0,q\rrbracket
such that
n_{\{u,v\}}^{(i)}\geqslant 1
, which implies that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Thus, for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
we have
v\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{V}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. In this case, we let
p=q
and we are done.

In the case where
\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}<\underline{n}
, there exists
e=\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{q}n_{e}^{(i)}<n_{e}
. If
v\notin\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, by connectedness of
G
, there exists a path from
v
to some
w\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Denoting by
v=v_{0},\dots,v_{l}=w
the distinct vertices of this path, ordered as in Definition
6.2
.
3
, there is a smallest
j\in\llbracket 1,l\rrbracket
such that
v_{j}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Since
v_{j-1}\notin\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, we have
\sum_{i=0}^{q}n_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}^{(i)}=0<n_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}
. Thus, up to replacing
e=\{u,v\}
by
\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}
, we can assume that
v\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Let
\underline{m}=\underline{n}-\mathbf{1}_{e}\geqslant\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}
. By
2
-edge-connectedness of
G
, there is a path from
u
to
v
in the sub-graph
(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
. Denoting by
u=v_{0},\dots,v_{l}=v
the distinct vertices of this path, ordered as in Definition
6.2
.
3
, we have
\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{l},v_{0}\}}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}\leqslant\mathbf{1}_{\{u,v\}}+\underline{m}\leqslant\underline{n}
. Hence, by adding an edge between
u
and
v
, we obtain a cycle in
G
of length
l+1
. By maximality of
l_{0}
, we have
l+1\leqslant l_{0}
.

Since
v=v_{l}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, there exists a smallest
j_{0}\in\llbracket 0,l\rrbracket
such that
v_{j_{0}}\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Recalling that
u=v_{0}
, we define
\mathcal{V}_{q+1}=\left\{v,u,v_{1},\dots,v_{j_{0}}\right\}
and
\underline{n}^{(q+1)}=\mathbf{1}_{\{u,v\}}+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}
. If
j_{0}=l
, then
v_{j_{0}}=v
and
G_{q+1}=\left(\mathcal{V}_{q+1},\underline{n}^{(q+1)}\right)
is a cycle of length
l+1\leqslant l_{0}
such that
\mathcal{V}_{q+1}\cap\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)=\{v\}
. If
j_{0}<l
then
G_{q+1}
is a non-trivial path of length
j_{0}+1\leqslant l<l_{0}
from
v
to
v_{j_{0}}
such that
\mathcal{V}_{q+1}\cap\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)=\{v,v_{j_{0}}\}
. Thus,
G_{q+1}
is of one of the two types described in Condition
2
.

For all
j\in\llbracket 1,j_{0}\rrbracket
, since
v_{j-1}\notin\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i}
, we have
\sum_{i=0}^{q}n^{(i)}_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}=0<m_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}
. Hence,

\sum_{i=0}^{q+1}\underline{n}^{(i)}=\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}+\sum_{j=1}^{j_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{u,v\}}\leqslant\underline{m}+\mathbf{1}_{\{u,v\}}\leqslant\underline{n},

which concludes the induction step.

Each
G_{q}
being non-trivial and connected,
\sum_{i=0}^{q}\lvert\underline{n}^{(i)}\rvert
increases with
q
. Since this quantity is bounded by
\lvert\underline{n}\rvert
, the previous inductive construction process finishes after finitely many steps.
∎

Lemma 6.9

(Existence of spanning trees, cf.
[
25
, Lem. 3.17]
)
.

Let
G=\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{n}\right)
be a
2
-edge-connected graph. It admits a family
(T_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}
of spanning trees with the following property: for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, there exists
v\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\underline{m}^{(v)}\leqslant\underline{n}-\mathbf{1}_{e}
, where we denoted
T_{v}=\left(\mathcal{V},\underline{m}^{(v)}\right)
.

Proof.

If
G
is trivial then it has no edge and
\mathcal{V}=\{v\}
. Since
G
is a tree, it is enough to let
T_{v}=G
. In the following, we assume that
G
is non-trivial, in particular
\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
G_{0}=(\mathcal{V}_{0},\underline{n}^{(0)})
, …,
G_{p}=(\mathcal{V}_{p},\underline{n}^{(p)})
be a decomposition of
G
given by Lemma
6.8
. For all
i\in\llbracket 0,p\rrbracket
, we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\{e\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\mid n^{(i)}_{e}\geqslant 1\}
. We have
\card(\mathcal{E}_{i})\leqslant\lvert\underline{n}^{(i)}\rvert\leqslant l_{0}=\card(\mathcal{V}_{0})
, by Conditions
1
and
2
in Lemma
6.8
. Hence there exists a surjection
\zeta_{i}:\mathcal{V}_{0}\to\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Note that
\underline{n}^{(i)}-\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{i}(v)}\in\mathbb{N}^{(\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2})}
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}_{0}
, since
\zeta_{i}(v)\in\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{V}_{0}
, for all
q\in\llbracket 0,p\rrbracket
we denote by
T_{q,v}=\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{q}\mathcal{V}_{i},\sum_{i=0}^{q}\underline{n}^{(i)}-\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{i}(v)}\right)
and let
T_{v}=T_{p,v}
. Since
T_{0,v}
is obtained by removing exactly one edge from the cycle
G_{0}
, it is a path and in particular a tree. Let
q\in\llbracket 0,p-1\rrbracket
, we assume that
T_{q,v}
is a tree. If
G_{q+1}
is a cycle, we obtain
T_{q+1,v}
by adding to
T_{q,v}
a path sharing exactly one vertex with it. If
G_{q+1}
is a path, we obtain
T_{q+1,v}
by adding to
T_{q,v}
two paths of the previous kind, that have no common vertex. These operations preserve connectedness and do not create cycles, hence
T_{q+1,v}
is again a tree. By induction,
T_{v}=T_{p,v}
is a tree. It is actually a spanning tree since
\bigcup_{i=0}^{p}\mathcal{V}_{i}=\mathcal{V}
, by Condition
3
in Lemma
6.8
.

The previous construction shows that
G
admits spanning trees. For all
v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{0}
, we denote by
T_{v}
any spanning tree of
G
. For all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we let
T_{v}=(\mathcal{V},\underline{m}^{(v)})
.

Let
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we have
n_{e}>0
. By Conditions
3
in Lemma
6.8
, there exists
i\in\llbracket 0,p\rrbracket
such that
\underline{n}^{(i)}_{e}>0
, that is,
e\in\mathcal{E}_{i}
. By surjectivity of
\zeta_{i}
, there exists
v\in\mathcal{V}_{0}
such that
\zeta_{i}(v)=e
. Then
\underline{m}^{(v)}=\sum_{i=0}^{p}\left(\underline{n}^{(i)}-\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{i}(v)}\right)\leqslant\left(\sum_{i=0}^{p}\underline{n}^{(i)}\right)-\mathbf{1}_{e}=\underline{n}-\mathbf{1}_{e}
. This proves that
(T_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}
satisfies the claimed property.
∎

7
Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities

This section is concerned with Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities, which are a family of inequalities generalizing those of Hölder and Young. In Section
7.1
, we recall what these inequalities are and how to interpolate between them. In Section
7.2
, we prove that specific Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities hold in the case of a product space, where the product is indexed by the vertices and edges of a
2
-edge-connected graph. Finally, in Section
7.3
, we use these inequalities to establish integral estimates for the universal part
\Upsilon_{A}
of the Kac–Rice density, see Definition
4.14
.

7.1
Interpolation of Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities

In this section, we recall the definition of Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities and a theorem of Bennett, Carbery, Christ and Tao
[
14
]
that allows us to interpolate between these inequalities.

Let
V
be a Euclidean space, endowed with its Lebesgue measure
\dmesure\!x
, and
\varphi:V\to[-\infty,+\infty]
be a Borel-measurable map. For all
s\geqslant 1
, we denote by
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{s}=\left(\int_{V}\lvert\varphi(x)\rvert^{s}\dmesure\!x\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}
the
L^{s}
-norm of
\varphi
, which can be either a non-negative number or
+\infty
. Similarly, we denote by
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty}\in[0,+\infty]
the essential supremum of
\lvert\varphi\rvert
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, let
V
and
(V_{a})_{a\in A}
be Euclidean spaces. For all
a\in A
, let
\ell_{a}:V\to V_{a}
be a linear surjection. We denote by
\underline{\ell}=\left(\ell_{a}\right)_{a\in A}
the collection of these surjections.

Definition 7.1

(Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequality)
.

Let
\underline{\ell}=(\ell_{a})_{a\in A}
be a family of surjections as above and
\underline{p}=(p_{a})_{a\in A}\in[1,+\infty]^{A}
. We say that a
Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb
(HBL)
inequality
associated with
\left(\underline{\ell},\underline{p}\right)
holds if there exists
C\in(0,+\infty)
such that

\int_{x\in V}\prod_{a\in A}\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(x))\dmesure\!x\leqslant C\prod_{a\in A}\lVert\varphi_{a}\rVert_{p_{a}},

(7.1)

for any family
(\varphi_{a})_{a\in A}
such that
\varphi_{a}:V_{a}\to[0,+\infty]
is Borel-measurable for all
a\in A
. Note that both sides of Equation (
7.1
) can take the value
+\infty
.

Given
\underline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in A}\in[0,1]^{A}
, we denote by
\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}=(\frac{1}{\alpha_{a}})_{a\in A}\in[1,+\infty]^{A}
, with the usual convention that
\frac{1}{0}=+\infty
. A necessary and sufficient condition for a HBL inequality associated with
\left(\underline{\ell},\underline{p}\right)
to hold was established in
[
14
]
. It can be stated using the set defined by:

\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}=\left\{\underline{\alpha}\in[0,1]^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{a HBL inequality associated with}\ \left(\underline{\ell},\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}\right)\ \text{holds}\right\}.

(7.2)

Theorem 7.2

(Bennett–Carbery–Christ–Tao)
.

Let
\underline{\ell}=(\ell_{a})_{a\in A}
be a family of surjections as above and
\underline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in A}\in[0,1]^{A}
. Then,
\underline{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
if and only if, for every subspace
W\subset V
, we have
\dim(W)\leqslant\sum_{a\in A}\alpha_{a}\dim(\ell_{a}(W))
, and equality holds when
W=V
.

Proof.

This is
[
14
, Thm. 2.1]
for
(\ell_{a})_{a\in A}
and
(p_{a})_{a\in A}=\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}
.
∎

In general, it is difficult to use Theorem
7.2
to check that a given
\underline{\alpha}\in[0,1]^{A}
belongs to
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
. However, it implies the following qualitative result.

Corollary 7.3

(Interpolation of Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities)
.

The set
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
is convex.

Proof.

Theorem
7.2
shows that
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
is defined by a family of linear inequalities, hence it is convex.
∎

7.2
Hölder–Brascamp–Lieb inequalities and edge-connected graphs

The goal of this section is to prove that HBL inequalities hold for some specific parameters, in the case where the family of surjections
\underline{\ell}
is related with a
2
-edge-connected graph.

Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})
be a graph. For all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we denote by
\ell_{v}:\underline{x}=(x_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\mapsto x_{v}
from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given
e=\{v,w\}\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we define
\ell_{e}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
either as
\underline{x}\mapsto x_{v}-x_{w}
or as
\underline{x}\mapsto x_{w}-x_{v}
arbitrarily. This defines a family
\underline{\ell}=(\ell_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
of linear surjections from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
\xi=(\xi_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{V}}
, we denote by
V_{\xi}=\left\{\underline{x}=(x_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\xi_{u}x_{u}=0\right\}
. Given
e=\{v,w\}\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we let
\ell_{e}^{(\xi)}
denote the restriction of
\ell_{e}
to
V_{\xi}
. We let
\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}=(\ell_{e}^{(\xi)})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, which defines a family of linear surjections from
V_{\xi}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Indeed, given
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
z=\ell_{e}^{(\xi)}(\underline{y})
, where
\underline{y}=(y_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}
is defined by
y_{v}=\frac{\xi_{w}}{\xi_{v}+\xi_{w}}z
,
y_{w}=-\frac{\xi_{v}}{\xi_{v}+\xi_{w}}z
and
y_{u}=0
for all
u\in\mathcal{V}\setminus e
.

The purpose of the following is to find some relevant points in the convex sets
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
, see (
7.2
). We first establish HBL inequalities as a consequence of the existence of a spanning tree of
G
, see Definition
6.2
.
5
. A similar result appears in the proof of
[
25
, Lem. 4.6]
. Note that, since
\underline{n}_{\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{G}}=0
, for any sub-graph
\left(\mathcal{W},\underline{m}\right)\subset G
we can consider
\underline{m}
as an element of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
.

Lemma 7.4

(Spanning trees and HBL inequalities)
.

Let
T=(\mathcal{V},\underline{m})
be a spanning tree of
G
and
v\in\mathcal{V}
. Then, we have
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
. Moreover, for all
\xi\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{E}_{T}=\{e\in\mathcal{V}^{\curlywedge 2}\mid m_{e}\geqslant 1\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{G}
. Since
T
is a tree, it has no multiple edge. Hence,
\underline{m}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{e}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}=\sum_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{a}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
. We denote by
\underline{p}=(p_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}=\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}}
, that is,
p_{a}=1
if
a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}
and
p_{a}=+\infty
otherwise.

Proof that
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
.

We have to prove that a HBL inequality associated with
(\underline{\ell},\underline{p})
holds, see (
7.1
). For all
a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}
, let
\varphi_{a}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
be a Borel-measurable function. We have:

\int_{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant\left(\prod_{a\notin\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\lVert\varphi_{a}\rVert_{\infty}\right)\int_{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}.

We claim that the linear map
\Lambda:\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\ell_{a}(\underline{x})\right)_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}
is an isomorphism from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}
to
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}
. Assuming this fact for now, we perform the linear change of variable
\underline{y}=\Lambda(\underline{x})
, which yields:

\int_{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}}\prod_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{a}(y_{a})\frac{\dmesure\!\underline{y}}{\Jac(\Lambda)}=\frac{1}{\Jac(\Lambda)}\prod_{a\in\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}\lVert\varphi_{a}\rVert_{1}.

Thus, a HBL inequality associated with
(\underline{\ell},\underline{p})
holds with constant
\frac{1}{\Jac\left(\Lambda\right)}
, and hence
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
.

Let us now prove that
\Lambda:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\{v\}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{T}}
is an isomorphism. Since
T
is a spanning tree, we have
1+\card(\mathcal{E}_{T})=1+\lvert\underline{m}\rvert=\card(\mathcal{V})
, see
[
57
, Thm. 2.1.4.B]
. Hence, the source and target spaces have the same dimension, and it is enough to prove that
\Lambda
is injective.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\in\ker(\Lambda)
. We prove that
x_{u}=0
for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
, by induction on the graph distance
\dist(u,v)
from
u
to
v
in
T
. If
d(u,v)=0
, then
u=v
and
x_{u}=x_{v}=\ell_{v}(\underline{x})=0
. Let
u\in\mathcal{V}
be such that
\dist(u,v)=l\geqslant 1
. There exists a path from
u
to
v
of length
l
in
T
. In particular, there exists
w\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\dist(u,w)=1
and
\dist(w,v)=l-1
. Letting
e=\{u,w\}
, the first condition proves that
e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}
, and hence
\lVert x_{u}-x_{w}\rVert=\lVert\ell_{e}(\underline{x})\rVert=0
. By the induction hypothesis, we have
x_{w}=0
, and hence
x_{u}=0
. This concludes the induction step. Since
T
is a spanning tree of
G
, this proves that
\underline{x}=0
. Thus,
\Lambda
is injective, which concludes the proof that
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
.

Proof that
\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
.

Let
\xi\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{V}}
. Considering
\underline{m}
as an element of
\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, we observe that
\underline{p}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}=\frac{1}{\underline{m}}
. Thus, we have to prove that a HBL inequality associated with
(\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)},\underline{p}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
holds. For any family
(\varphi_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
of non-negative Borel-measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\int_{\underline{x}\in V_{\xi}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\varphi_{e}(\ell_{e}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant\prod_{e\notin\mathcal{E}_{T}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\infty}\int_{\underline{x}\in V_{\xi}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{e}(\ell_{e}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x},

where
\dmesure\!\underline{x}
stands for the Lebesgue measure on the subspace
V_{\xi}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}
. Let
\Lambda_{\xi}:V_{\xi}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{E}_{T}}
be the linear map
\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\ell_{e}(\underline{x})\right)_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}
. As above, we have
\dim(V_{\xi})=d(\card(\mathcal{V})-1)=d\card(\mathcal{E}_{T})
, because
T
is a spanning tree. Assuming for now that
\Lambda_{\xi}
is injective, it is an isomorphism. The change of variable
\underline{y}=\Lambda_{\xi}(\underline{x})
then yields:

\int_{\underline{x}\in V_{\xi}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{e}(\ell_{e}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}=\frac{1}{\Jac\left(\Lambda_{\xi}\right)}\int_{\underline{y}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{E}_{T}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\varphi_{e}(y_{e})\dmesure\!\underline{y}=\frac{1}{\Jac\left(\Lambda_{\xi}\right)}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{1}.

Thus, a HBL inequality associated with
(\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)},\underline{p}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
holds, and
\underline{m}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
.

We still need to prove that
\Lambda_{\xi}
is injective. Let
\underline{x}=(x_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\in V_{\xi}
be such that
\Lambda_{\xi}(\underline{x})=0
. Let
v\in\mathcal{V}
. As above, we prove by induction on
\dist(u,v)
that
x_{u}=x_{v}
for all
u\in\mathcal{V}
. Then, since
\underline{x}\in V_{\xi}
, we have
0=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\xi_{u}x_{u}=x_{v}\left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{V}}\xi_{u}\right)
. Since the
(\xi_{u})_{u\in\mathcal{V}}
are positive, this implies that
x_{v}=0
, hence
\underline{x}=0
. Finally,
\Lambda_{\xi}
is injective, as claimed. Hence the result.
∎

We can now prove the main result of this section, which states that HBL inequalities of the form (
7.1
) hold for some particular exponents if
G
is
2
-edge-connected.

Proposition 7.5

(HBL inequalities for
2
-edge-connected graphs)
.

Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})
be a
2
-edge-connected graph such that
\card(\mathcal{V})\geqslant 2
. Let
\underline{\ell}=(\ell_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
be the family of surjections defined above. There exist exponents
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in[2,+\infty]^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and positive constants
C
and
C^{\prime}
such that, for any Borel-measurable functions
\left(\varphi_{a}\right)_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\int_{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant C\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\lVert\varphi_{v}\rVert_{2}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}\right),

(7.3)

and, letting
q_{e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}\frac{p_{e}}{2}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
,

\int_{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant C^{\prime}\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\lVert\varphi_{v}\rVert_{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\frac{q_{e}}{n_{e}}}\right).

(7.4)

Moreover, for all
\xi\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{V}}
, there exists
C_{\xi}>0
such that for any Borel-measurable functions
\left(\varphi_{e}\right)_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\int_{\underline{x}\in V_{\xi}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{e}(\ell_{e}(\underline{x}))\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant C_{\xi}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\frac{q_{e}}{n_{e}}}.

(7.5)

Proof.

The strategy is to find relevant points in the set
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
) defined by (
7.2
), which satisfy some additional constraints. For any non-negative Borel-measurable functions
(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, we have:

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}\varphi_{a}(\ell_{a}(\underline{x}))\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\infty}\right)\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{V}}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\varphi_{v}(x_{v})\dmesure\!\underline{x}=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\lVert\varphi_{v}\rVert_{1}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\infty}\right).

This shows that
\underline{\gamma}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbf{1}_{v}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
. Besides, since
G=(\mathcal{V},\underline{n})
is
2
-edge-connected, it admits a family
(T_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}
of spanning trees given by Lemma
6.9
. For all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, letting
T_{v}=(\mathcal{V},\underline{m}^{(v)})
, we have
\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}^{(v)}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
by Lemma
7.4
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
is convex, see Corollary
7.3
. We define
\underline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and
\underline{\beta}=(\beta_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
by
\underline{\beta}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{1}_{v}+\underline{m}^{(v)})
and
\underline{\alpha}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\underline{\beta}+\left(1-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\right)\underline{\gamma}
. Indeed, since we assumed
\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert\geqslant 2
, we have
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\in[0,1]
.

For all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we have
\underline{m}^{(v)}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\subset\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and
\underline{m}^{(v)}_{\mathcal{V}}=0
. Hence
\underline{\beta}_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbf{1}_{v}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\underline{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Then,

\underline{\alpha}_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\underline{\beta}_{\mathcal{V}}+\left(1-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\right)\underline{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}=\underline{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(1+\frac{1}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\underline{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}.

That is, for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we have
\alpha_{v}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta_{v}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}
.

Let
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we have
n_{e}\geqslant 1
by definition of
\mathcal{E}_{G}
. For all
v\in\mathcal{V}
, since
T_{v}
is a tree it cannot have multiple edges, and hence
m^{(v)}_{e}\leqslant 1
. Moreover, since we obtained
(T_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}
from Lemma
6.9
, there exists
u\in\mathcal{V}
such that
m_{e}^{(u)}<n_{e}
. If
n_{e}=1
, then
m_{e}^{(u)}=0
, and

\beta_{e}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}m_{e}^{(v)}\leqslant\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\card(\mathcal{V}\setminus\{u\})=n_{e}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}.

If
n_{e}\geqslant 2
, we have
\beta_{e}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}m_{e}^{(v)}\leqslant 1\leqslant 2\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\leqslant n_{e}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}
, since we assumed that
\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert\geqslant 2
. Thus, for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we have
\beta_{e}\leqslant n_{e}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}
and
\alpha_{e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{2(\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1)}\beta_{e}\leqslant\frac{n_{e}}{2}
.

For all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we define
p_{e}=\frac{n_{e}}{\alpha_{e}}\in[2,+\infty]
and
q_{e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert-1}\frac{p_{e}}{2}=\frac{n_{e}}{\beta_{e}}
. Recalling Definition
7.1
and Equation (
7.2
), since
\alpha\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
, a HBL inequality associated with
(\underline{\ell},\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}})
holds for some constant
C>0
. Since
\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}2\mathbf{1}_{v}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}\mathbf{1}_{e}
, this concludes the proof of (
7.3
). Similarly, we have
\frac{1}{\underline{\beta}}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert\mathbf{1}_{v}+\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\frac{q_{e}}{n_{e}}\mathbf{1}_{e}
, and
\underline{\beta}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}}
, which proves (
7.4
).

Let
\xi\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{V}}
, by Lemma
7.4
we have
\underline{m}^{(v)}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
. Considering the projections of the previous multi-indices on
\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, we have:
\underline{\beta}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\underline{m}^{(v)}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}}
by convexity. Thus a HBL inequality associated with
\underline{\ell}^{(\xi)}
and
\frac{1}{\underline{\beta}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\frac{q_{e}}{n_{e}}\mathbf{1}_{e}
holds, which proves the existence of
C_{\xi}>0
such that (
7.5
) holds.
∎

7.3
Integral estimates for the universal part of the Kac–Rice density

This section is concerned with proving upper bounds for some integrals where the universal part of the Kac–Rice density appears. Recall that the thick diagonals
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
were defined in Definition
2.31
.

Lemma 7.6

(Local mass of the singularity)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set,
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta>0
. Then, the following positive constant is finite:

C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}=\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\ \prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}<+\infty,

where
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}=\left\{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\flat(\underline{x})=0\right\}
, the functions
\Upsilon_{J}
were introduced in Definition
4.14
, and
\dmesure\!\underline{x}
is the Lebesgue measure on the subspace
\flat^{-1}(0)=\left\{\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{a\in A}x_{a}=0\right\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.21
, for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
, the function
\Upsilon_{J}
is translation-invariant. Hence, letting
\mathbb{B}
denote the closed ball of center
0
and volume
1
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}=\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{B}}\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\tau\cdot\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}\dmesure\!\tau=\lvert A\rvert^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\underline{y}\in\Gamma}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{y}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{y},

where
\Gamma=\{\tau\cdot\underline{x}\mid\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}\ \text{and}\ \tau\in\mathbb{B}\}=\{\underline{y}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{A\},\eta}\mid\flat(\underline{y})\in\mathbb{B}\}
and we performed the linear change of variable
\underline{y}=\tau\cdot\underline{x}
, whose Jacobian is
\lvert A\rvert^{-\frac{d}{2}}
. Denoting by
\Gamma_{J}
the projection of
\Gamma
onto
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{J}
for all
J\in\mathcal{J}
, we have
\Gamma\subset\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Gamma_{J}
, and hence:

C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\leqslant\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\int_{\underline{y}_{J}\in\Gamma_{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{y}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{y}_{J}.

By Corollary
3.33
, the set
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}
is compact. Therefore,
\Gamma
is compact, and so are its projections
(\Gamma_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
. By Lemma
4.21
, the functions
(\Upsilon_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
are locally integrable. Hence,
C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}<+\infty
.
∎

Definition 7.7

(An integral transform)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set,
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta>0
. Let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
be a family of Borel-measurable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
(-\infty,+\infty]
. We define a function
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by the following formula, whenever it makes sense:

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi}):\tau\longmapsto\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\left(\prod_{a\in A}\phi_{a}(\tau+x_{a})\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x},

where
\dmesure\!\underline{x}
is the Lebesgue measure on the Euclidean subspace
\flat^{-1}(0)\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
.

For reasons that will be apparent later on, it is convenient for us to state the following estimates as the continuity of some multilinear maps between Lebesgue spaces.

Lemma 7.8

(Regularity of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}
)
.

Let
A\neq\emptyset
be finite,
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\eta>0
. For all
s\in[1,+\infty]
, the map
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}:\left(L^{s\lvert A\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{A}\to L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is multilinear continuous of norm at most
C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}
is multilinear, the proof of its continuity boils down to proving an integral estimate. Let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
be Borel-measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\left(\prod_{a\in A}\left\lvert\phi_{a}(\tau+x_{a})\right\rvert\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\prod_{a\in A}\lVert\phi_{a}\rVert_{\infty}.

In particular, if
\phi_{a}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
a\in A
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is well-defined and bounded by
C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\prod_{a\in A}\lVert\phi_{a}\rVert_{\infty}
. This proves the result in the case
s=+\infty
.

Let us now assume that
s\in[1,+\infty)
. In the following, we denote by
\lvert\underline{\phi}\rvert=(\lvert\phi_{a}\rvert)_{a\in A}
. Applying Jensen’s inequality to the probability measure with density
\underline{x}\mapsto\frac{1}{C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})
, we get:

\left(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\phi}\rvert)(\tau)\right)^{s}\leqslant(C_{\mathcal{J},\eta})^{s-1}\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\prod_{a\in A}\left\lvert\phi_{a}(\tau+x_{a})\right\rvert^{s}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Integrating with respect to
\tau
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\phi}\rvert)(\tau)\right)^{s}\dmesure\!\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant(C_{\mathcal{J},\eta})^{s-1}\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\left\lVert\prod_{a\in A}\phi_{a}(\cdot+x_{a})\right\rVert_{s}^{s}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

\displaystyle\leqslant(C_{\mathcal{J},\eta})^{s-1}\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{A\},\eta}}\left(\prod_{a\in A}\left\lVert\phi_{a}(\cdot+x_{a})\right\rVert_{s\lvert A\rvert}^{s}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

\displaystyle\leqslant(C_{\mathcal{J},\eta})^{s}\prod_{a\in A}\left\lVert\phi_{a}\right\rVert_{s\lvert A\rvert}^{s}.

Thus, if
\phi_{a}\in L^{s\lvert A\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
a\in A
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\phi}\rvert)(\tau)<+\infty
for almost every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In this case,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi})
is well-defined almost everywhere, and
\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi})\rVert_{s}\leqslant\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\phi}\rvert)\rVert_{s}\leqslant C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\prod_{a\in A}\lVert\phi_{a}\rVert_{s\lvert A\rvert}
. This proves the result if
s\in[1,+\infty)
.
∎

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. As in Section
7.2
, we define
\ell_{I}:(y_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{I}}\mapsto y_{I}
from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Similarly, for all
e=\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
we define
\ell_{e}
either as
\ell_{I}-\ell_{J}
or as
\ell_{J}-\ell_{I}
arbitrarily. Recalling Definition
3.17
, we let
\flat_{\mathcal{I}}:\underline{x}\mapsto(\flat(\underline{x}_{I}))_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
to
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 7.9

(Integration functionals)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set,
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
and
\eta>0
. Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})
be a graph. For every families
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{a})_{a\in A}
and
\underline{g}=\left(g_{e}\right)_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
of Borel-measurable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
(-\infty,+\infty]
, we denote by

\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi},\underline{g})=\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\prod_{a\in A}\phi_{a}(x_{a})\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x},

if this integral is well-defined.

Recalling Proposition
4.32
, Corollary
6.7
and Lemma
3.37
, quantities of the form
\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\phi},\underline{g})
are typically what we need to control in order to prove Theorem
1.7
. The estimates we need are summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.10

(Integral estimates for
\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}
)
.

If
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 2
and the graph
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})
is
2
-edge-connected, then the map
\underline{g}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})
is well-defined and continuous in the following three cases, where the exponents
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and
(q_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
are given by Proposition
7.5
:

1.

from
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the space of the continuous multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
;

2.

from
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the space of the continuous multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{2\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
;

3.

from
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the space of the continuous multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
.

Proof.

For any family
\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in A\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
of Borel-measurables functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define:

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})=\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\prod_{a\in A}\varphi_{a}(x_{a})\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\varphi_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x},

if this integral makes sense, so that
\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left((\phi_{a})_{a\in A},(g_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left((\phi_{a})_{a\in A},(g_{e}^{n_{e}})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\right)
. The first step of the proof is to bound
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})
by an integral quantity where the functions
(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
from Definition
7.7
appear. Then, the claimed continuities follow from Proposition
7.5
and Lemma
7.8
.

Step 1: Bounding
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})
.

Let
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\underline{x}_{I}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{I\},\eta}
by Lemma
2.35
. Hence, we have
\underline{x}_{I}=\flat(\underline{x}_{I})+\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}}
, where
\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}
were defined in Definition
3.17
. Thus
\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}),(\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)
is an isomorphism from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
to
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}\times\flat_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}(0)
that maps
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
to a subset of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}
. One can check that the inverse of its Jacobian is
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\lvert I\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\leqslant\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}
.

Let us consider a family
\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in A\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{G}}
of Borel-measurables functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
(-\infty,+\infty]
, and denote
\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert=(\lvert\varphi_{a}\rvert)_{a\in A}
. Starting from the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert)
, we perform the linear change of variable
(\tau_{I},\underline{z}_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}=(\flat(\underline{x}_{I}),\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
. Recalling that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
and that the functions
(\Upsilon_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
are non-negative and translation-invariant (see Lemma
4.21
), we obtain:

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert\right)=\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\lvert\varphi_{i}(x_{i})\rvert\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{I}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{x}\\
\leqslant\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}\int_{\underline{\tau}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}}\int_{\underline{z}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\lvert\varphi_{i}(\tau_{I}+z_{i})\rvert\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{I}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\right)\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{e}\left(\ell_{e}(\underline{\tau})\right)\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{z}\dmesure\!\underline{\tau}\\
\leqslant\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}\int_{\underline{\tau}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\varphi}_{I}\rvert)(\tau_{I})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\left\lvert\varphi_{e}\left(\ell_{e}(\underline{\tau})\right)\right\rvert\dmesure\!\underline{\tau}.

(7.6)

Step 2: Deducing the continuity from HBL inequalities.

Let us first prove Item
2
. By Proposition
7.5
and Lemma
7.8
with
s=2
, we deduce from (
7.6
) that:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\varphi}_{I}\rvert)\right\rVert_{2}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}C_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}\right)\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\lVert\varphi_{i}\rVert_{2\lvert I\rvert}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}\right).

If
\underline{\varphi}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{2\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}\times\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert\right)<+\infty
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\underline{\varphi}\right)
is well-defined. Thus,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}
is a well-defined multilinear form on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{2\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}\times\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For all
\underline{\varphi}
in this space, we have
\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\underline{\varphi}\right)\right\rvert\leqslant\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert\right)
, and the previous inequality shows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}
is continuous. Hence,
\underline{\varphi}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{\varphi}_{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is multilinear continuous from
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the space of continuous multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{2\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
p\in[1,+\infty]
, the map
(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
is multilinear continuous from
\left(L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{n}
to
L^{\frac{p}{n}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by Hölder’s inequality. Hence, for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, the map
g_{e}\mapsto g_{e}^{n_{e}}
is continuous from
L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{\frac{p_{e}}{n_{e}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as the restriction of one the previous to the deepest diagonal. Thus, the map
\underline{g}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})
is well-defined and continuous between the space appearing in Item
2
, as the composition of
(g_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\mapsto(g_{e}^{n_{e}})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and
(\varphi_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\cdot,(\varphi_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\right)
.

The proof of Item
3
is similar. We just replace
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
by
(q_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
in the previous argument, use Equation (
7.4
) instead of (
7.3
) and use, this time, the continuity of
T_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}
from
\left(L^{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
to
L^{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Concerning Item
1
, we start from Equation (
7.6
) and bound the functions
(\varphi_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
by their sup-norm. Using Lemma
7.8
with
s=1
to bound
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}(\lvert\underline{\varphi}_{I}\rvert)\right\rVert_{1}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we obtain:

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\lvert\underline{\varphi}\rvert\right)\leqslant\lvert A\rvert^{\frac{d\lvert A\rvert}{2}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}C_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}\right)\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\lVert\varphi_{i}\rVert_{\lvert I\rvert}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert\varphi_{e}\rVert_{\infty}\right).

The conclusion follows by the same argument as in the other two cases.
∎

We need a similar estimate for an integral over
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}:={{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap\flat^{-1}(0)
, with respect to the Lebesgue measure on Euclidean the subspace
\flat^{-1}(0)
. The integrand in Lemma
7.11
will naturally appear as the dominating function in a dominated convergence later on.

Lemma 7.11

(Integrable dominating function)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
and
\eta>0
. Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})
be a
2
-edge-connected graph. For every
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, let
g_{e}\in L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be non-negative, where
(q_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
is given by Proposition
7.5
. Then,

\int_{\underline{x}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}<+\infty,

where
\dmesure\!\underline{x}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\flat^{-1}(0)=\left\{(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{a\in A}x_{a}=0\right\}
.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\xi=\left(\lvert I\rvert\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in(0,+\infty)^{\mathcal{I}}
and
V_{\xi}=\left\{(\tau_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\lvert I\rvert\tau_{I}=0\right\}
. We have checked, in the first step of the proof of Lemma
7.10
, that
\underline{x}\mapsto\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}),(\accentset{\bullet}{\overbracket{\underline{x}_{I}}})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\right)
is an isomorphism from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
to
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}\times\flat_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}(0)
that maps
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
onto a subset of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{I}}\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}
. For all
\underline{x}=(x_{a})_{a\in A}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we have
\lvert A\rvert\flat(\underline{x})=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\lvert I\rvert\flat(\underline{x}_{I})
. Hence, the previous isomorphism restricts into an isomorphism from
\flat^{-1}(0)
to
V_{\xi}\times\flat_{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}(0)
, that maps
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
onto a subset of
V_{\xi}\times\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}
. Letting
C>0
denote the inverse of its Jacobian, we perform the corresponding linear change of variable. Then, the integral we are interested in is bounded by:

C\int_{\underline{\tau}\in V_{\xi}}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\int_{\underline{z}_{I}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}_{I}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z}_{I}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{e}\left(\ell_{e}(\tau_{I})\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{\tau}\\
=C\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}C_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}\right)\int_{\underline{\tau}\in V_{\xi}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{e}\left(\ell_{e}(\tau_{I})\right)^{n_{e}}\dmesure\!\underline{\tau}\leqslant C\left(\prod_{I\in`\mathcal{I}}C_{\mathcal{J}_{I},\eta}\right)C_{\xi}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\lVert g_{e}^{n_{e}}\rVert_{\frac{q_{e}}{n_{e}}},

by Proposition
7.5
. Since
g_{e}\in L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, the right-hand side is finite.
∎

8
Proof of Theorem
1.7
: cumulants asymptotics for zero sets

This section is concerned with the proof of the cumulants asymptotics of Theorem
1.7
. Let us recall the setting. The following notation will remain fixed in this whole section.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
, we denote by
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
a centered stationary Gaussian field and by
r
its covariance kernel. Let
\Omega\subset U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open subsets such that
U
is convex and
\dist(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus U)>0
. Let
\mathcal{R}\subset(0,+\infty)
be unbounded. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, we consider a centered Gaussian field
f_{R}:RU\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
and denote by
r_{R}
its covariance kernel. In order to simplify some statements, we let
f_{\infty}=f
and use the convention that
\infty\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}=\infty U
. In the following,
R
is always implicitly assumed to be an element of
\mathcal{R}\sqcup\{\infty\}
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume in all this section that the following hold: (
Reg
(
\max(2,2p-1)
)
), (
ScL
(
2p-1
)
), (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) and (
DC
(
2p-1,s
)
), for some
s\in[1,+\infty]
. Let
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
and
\omega>0
be those appearing in Hypothesis (
DC
(
2p-1,s
)
), recall that the function
g_{\omega}
is defined as
g_{\omega}:x\mapsto\sup_{\lVert y\rVert\leqslant\omega}g(x+y)
. Recall also that, under these assumptions, (
DCL
(
2p-1,s
)
) also holds for the same
\omega
and
g
, see Section
1.4
.

8.1
Validity of the Kac–Rice formula under (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
)

In this section, we work under the assumption that (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) holds, that is
g
is bounded and goes to
0
at infinity. The parameter
\omega
might seem irrelevant in this case, yet it will be useful later on to have it appear in the statements of this section. We will prove that the fields
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}\sqcup\{\infty\}}
are uniformly non-degenerate in the sense of Corollary
3.42
and that the hypotheses of the Kac–Rice formula for cumulants are satisfied, see Proposition
4.32
. As a consequence, we prove Theorem
1.7
.
1
.

Lemma 8.1

(Existence of a good scale parameter)
.

There exists
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
such that the following holds. For all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, for all
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, there exists a compact
\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
such that, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
,

\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})\right)\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}.

Proof.

For all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we have
\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\leqslant p
. Under the hypotheses of this section, we can apply Corollary
3.42
to the set
2\mathcal{K}
. This yields the existence of
\eta_{\mathcal{K}}>0
such that, for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{\mathcal{K}}]
, there exists
R_{\eta,\mathcal{K}}\in\mathcal{R}
such that, for all
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2\mathcal{K}}
, the set

\left\{\Sigma_{\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\geqslant R_{\eta,\mathcal{K}}\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{I}},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{2\mathcal{K}}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2\mathcal{K}}
is of the form
2\mathcal{I}
with
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, then
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
, see Definition
4.15
. Moreover, for all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, we have
\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{I}},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{2\mathcal{K}}
, see Remark
2.33
. Hence,
\left\{\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\geqslant R_{\eta,\mathcal{K}}\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
for all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
. We define

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\eta_{\mathcal{K}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\neq A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{\omega}{2p}\right\}>0

and

\displaystyle R_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{R_{\eta,\mathcal{K}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\neq A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}\right\}.

Then, for all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and all
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, there exists a compact
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
such that
\left\{\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}R\geqslant R_{\eta}\ \text{and}\ \underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\right\}\subset\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
.

Let
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
be non-empty,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
. Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
I=[J]_{\mathcal{I}}
. For all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, we have
\underline{x}_{I}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{I\},\eta}
, by Lemma
2.35
. Then, by Lemma
3.30
and Corollary
3.33
,

\left\{\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\right\}\subset\left\{\Pi_{2J}^{2I}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2I})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}_{I}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\{I\},\eta}\right\}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{J,\eta},

and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{J,\eta}
is compact in
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert[J]_{\mathcal{I}}\rvert-1}[X]^{k},\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert J\rvert-1}[X]^{k}\right)
as the continuous image of a compact. We define
\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{J,\eta}\times\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, which is a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\times\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
(see Definitions
4.15
and
4.28
) with the claimed property.
∎

A first consequence of Lemma
8.1
is the following.

Lemma 8.2

(Uniform domination of
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. There exists
C_{\eta}>0
such that, for all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, all
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and all
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
we have:

\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\right\rvert\leqslant C_{\eta}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}))\right)^{n_{e}},

where
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
is the map appearing in Lemma
4.30
, we defined
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
in Definition
6.6
and, for all
e=\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
, we have
g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}))\right)=g_{\omega}\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{I})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J})\right)=g_{\omega}\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{J})-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\right)
since
g
is even.

Proof.

For all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and all
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, let
\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}
be a compact set given by Lemma
8.1
. Let
C_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}\geqslant 0
be the constant associated with
\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}
by Corollary
6.7
. We define
C_{1}=\max\left\{C_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\emptyset\neq A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket;\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A};\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\ \text{in}\ \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\right\}
. Recalling the relation between
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
and
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
stated in Lemma
4.30
, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, we have:

\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f_{R},\underline{x}\right)\right\rvert\leqslant C_{1}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}}\ \prod_{\{I,J\}\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{2I}^{2J}\left(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}}\right)\right\rVert^{n_{\{I,J\}}}.

By Lemma
3.37
, there exists
C_{2}\geqslant 1
, depending only on
p
and
\eta
, such that for all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, we have:

\left\lVert\Sigma_{2I}^{2J}\left(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}}\right)\right\rVert\leqslant C_{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert\alpha\rvert<2p\\
\lvert\beta\rvert<2p\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\end{subarray}}\ \left\lVert\partial^{\alpha,\beta}r_{R}(w,z)\right\rVert\leqslant C_{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\\
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})\end{subarray}}g(z-w),

where the second inequality is given by (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) and (
DCL
(
2p-1,\infty
)
). If
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
then, by Lemma
3.32
, for all
i\in I
we have
\lVert x_{i}-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\rVert\leqslant p\eta
. Hence,
\left\lVert w-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\right\rVert\leqslant p\eta
for all
w\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
, and similarly
\lVert z-\flat(\underline{x}_{J})\rVert\leqslant p\eta
for all
z\in\conv(\underline{x}_{J})
. Thus,

\left\lVert(z-w)-(\flat(\underline{x}_{J})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J}))\right\rVert\leqslant\lVert w-\flat(\underline{x}_{I})\rVert+\lVert z-\flat(\underline{x}_{J})\rVert\leqslant 2p\eta\leqslant\omega.

Finally, for all
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\max_{(w,z)\in\conv(\underline{x}_{I})\times\conv(\underline{x}_{J})}g(z-w)\leqslant g_{\omega}\left(\flat(\underline{x}_{I})-\flat(\underline{x}_{J})\right)
. Recalling Lemmas
5.5
and
6.4
, we have
\lvert\underline{n}\rvert\leqslant 2p
for all
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
. The result follows, with
C_{\eta}=C_{1}C_{2}^{2p}
.
∎

Under the hypotheses of this section, Lemma
8.2
implies that we can indeed use the Kac–Rice formula for cumulants, see Proposition
4.32
.

Lemma 8.3

(Validity of the Kac–Rice formula for cumulants)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. Let
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(R\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(R\Omega)
. For all non-empty
A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and all
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
, the following function is integrable over
(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
:

\underline{x}\longmapsto\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{A}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J}).

Proof.

Let
\emptyset\neq A\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. For all
K\in\mathcal{K}
, the function
\varphi_{K}=\prod_{i\in K}\lvert\phi_{i}\rvert
is non-negative, and
\varphi_{K}\in L^{1}(R\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(R\Omega)
by Hölder’s inequality. Extending this function by
0
outside of
R\Omega
, we obtain
\varphi_{K}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, for all
\underline{x}=(x_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, we have
\left\lvert\phi_{A}^{\otimes}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right\rvert=\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\lvert\phi_{i}(x_{K})\rvert=\varphi^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})
.

Let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. By Lemma
8.2
, for all
\underline{x}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
we have:

\left\lvert\left(\phi^{\otimes}_{A}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right\rvert\leqslant C_{\eta}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}}\varphi^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}))\right)^{n_{e}}\right).

(8.1)

We need to check that each term on the right-hand side is integrable over
(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, or equivalently over
{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
.

Let us first assume that
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 2
. Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Recalling Definition
7.9
, we deduce from Lemma
7.10
.
1
that

\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\varphi^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{K}}(\underline{x})\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}))\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}=\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi},\underline{g})\\
\leqslant\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})\right\rVert\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{K\in I}\lVert\varphi_{K}\rVert_{\lvert I\rvert}<+\infty,

where
\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}}
,
\underline{g}=(g_{\omega},\dots,g_{\omega})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and
\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})\right\rVert
is the operator norm of
\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})
as a multilinear map on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}L^{\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{I}
. Note that
n_{e}=0
for all
e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{G}
. If
\card(\mathcal{I})=1
, then
\mathcal{I}=\{\mathcal{K}\}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}=\emptyset
. In this case, we have

\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\{\mathcal{K}\},\eta}}\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\varphi_{K}(x_{K})\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}\leqslant p^{\frac{dp}{2}}\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})(y)\dmesure\!y\leqslant p^{\frac{dp}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})\right\rVert_{1}

by the same kind of computation as (
7.6
). The right-hand side is finite by Lemma
7.8
. Thus, in both cases, each term on the right-hand side of (
8.1
) is integrable over
(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
. Hence the result.
∎

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. By Lemma
4.17
, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
x\in R\Omega
, the centered Gaussian vector
\left(f_{R}(x),D_{x}f_{R}\right)
is non-degenerate. Then, by Proposition
4.3
, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
, the set
Z_{R}=f_{R}^{-1}(0)\cap(R\Omega)
is almost-surely a codimension-
k
submanifold. In particular, the random measure
\nu_{R}
on
\Omega
is almost-surely well-defined by Equation (
1.3
). Thanks to Lemma
8.3
, we can express the cumulants
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})
by the Kac–Rice formula.

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. Let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
where
\phi_{i}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\eta},+\infty]
, we have:

\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})=\frac{1}{R^{pd}}\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p,d-k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\\
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}\ \int_{(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\!\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}.

In particular, this proves Theorem
1.7
.
1
.

Proof.

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}\cap[R_{\eta},+\infty]
. As explained above, the definition on
\eta
and
R_{\eta}
ensure that
\nu_{R}
and
Z_{R}
are well-defined Radon measures on
\Omega
and
R\Omega
respectively. Since these measures are related by (
1.3
), we have:

\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})=\kappa\left(\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}\right)=\kappa\left(\left(\frac{\left\langle Z_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rangle}{R^{d}}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}\right)=\frac{\kappa(Z_{R})\left(\phi_{1}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right),\dots,\phi_{p}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right)}{R^{pd}},

By Lemma
8.1
, for all
