\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{p}
, we have
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x},\underline{x})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. For all
i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, we have
\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\in L^{1}(R\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(R\Omega)
, so that the integrability hypotheses in Proposition
4.32
are satisfied, by Lemma
8.3
.

We obtain the claimed expression of
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})
by applying Proposition
4.32
to
f_{R}
over
R\Omega
with the test-functions
\left(\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
. By Lemma
8.3
, each integral on the right-hand side is well-defined and finite, which proves Theorem
1.7
.
1
.
∎

8.2
Cumulants asymptotics under (
DC
(
2p-1,\frac{p}{p-1}
)
)

In this section, we work under the assumption that
p\geqslant 2
and (
DC
(
2p-1,\frac{p}{p-1}
)
) holds. In particular, everything we proved in Section
8.1
is still valid. Our goal is to prove Theorem
1.7
.
3
, i.e., derive the exact asymptotics of the cumulants of order
p
for the linear statistics of
\nu_{R}
, or equivalently
Z_{R}
. It is enough to derive the asymptotics of each term in the expression of the cumulants given by Corollary
8.4
.

In the following, given
\varphi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we consider
\varphi
as a function in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by extending it by
0
outside of
\Omega
. We will use repeatedly the fact that, for all
R>0
and
s\in[1,+\infty)
, we have:

\left\lVert\varphi\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rVert_{s}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\varphi\left(\frac{x}{R}\right)\right\rvert^{s}\dmesure\!x\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}=\left(R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\varphi\left(y\right)\right\rvert^{s}\dmesure\!y\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}=R^{\frac{d}{s}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{s}.

(8.2)

Lemma 8.5

(Exact asymptotics)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{\omega}{2p}]
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}
and
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
be functions in
L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have:

\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}}\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}\\
\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}(y)\dmesure\!y\right)\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{z}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z},

(8.3)

where
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}=\left\{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\flat(\underline{x})=0\right\}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathcal{K}}
and
\dmesure\!\underline{z}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\flat^{-1}(0)
.

Proof.

There are two steps to the proof. The first one is to replace
f_{R}
by the limit field
f
in the integral on the left-hand side of (
8.3
) and prove that the error goes to
0
as
R\to+\infty
. The second one is to prove the claimed convergence once we replaced
f_{R}
by
f
.

Step 1: Replacing
f_{R}
by
f
.

By definition of
\eta
and
R_{\eta}
, see Lemma
8.1
, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, we have

\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\theta_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\left(\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}(\cdot,\underline{x}_{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}})\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}},\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})\right)\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}.

Recall that, if
\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}
, then
\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}}\in{{\boxslash}}_{2\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{2\mathcal{K}}
, see Remark
2.33
. Applying Proposition
3.41
with
A=2\mathcal{K}
yields that

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}}\left\lVert\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})-\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x}_{2\mathcal{K}})\right\rVert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

By Lemma
4.33
, the function
F_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
is continuous, and hence uniformly continuous on the compact
\Gamma_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\eta}
. Thus, recalling the definition of
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
in Lemma
4.30
, we have:

\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}}\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\right\rvert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

For all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
we let
\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})=0
if
\left\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau\cdot\underline{z}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\right\}=\emptyset
and

\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})=\sup\left\{\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\tau\cdot\underline{z})-\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\tau\cdot\underline{z})\right\rvert\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{such that}\ \tau\cdot\underline{z}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\right\}

otherwise. Then,
\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})\leqslant\sup_{\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}}\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})-\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{x})\right\rvert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0
for all
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
.

Recalling that we extended each
\phi_{i}
by
0
outside of
\Omega
, the change of variable
(\tau,\underline{z})=(\flat(\underline{x}),\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})
and the translation-invariance of the
(\Upsilon_{J})_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
, see Lemma
4.21
, yield:

\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\underline{x}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f_{R},\underline{x}\right)-\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{x}\right)\right\rvert\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}\\
\begin{aligned} &\leqslant\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\varepsilon_{R}(\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\accentset{\bullet}{\underline{x}}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}\\
&\leqslant\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\left\lvert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\tau+z_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\dmesure\!\tau\right)\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z}.\end{aligned}

(8.4)

For all
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
, by Hölder’s inequality and Equation (
8.2
), we have:

\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\left\lvert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\tau+z_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\dmesure\!\tau\leqslant\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\frac{1}{R^{\frac{d}{p}}}\left\lVert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot+z_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rVert_{p}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lVert\phi_{i}\rVert_{p}<+\infty,

recalling that
\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket=\bigsqcup_{K\in\mathcal{K}}K
. Thus, considering the integral over
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
on the last line of (
8.4
), the integrand is bounded by
\left(\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lVert\phi_{i}\rVert_{p}\right)\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0
.

We need a dominating function in order to apply Lebesgue’s dominated convergence Theorem. By Lemma
8.2
, for all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
we have:

\varepsilon_{R}(\underline{z})\leqslant\sup_{\left\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau\cdot\underline{z}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\right\}}\left(\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\tau\cdot\underline{z})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\tau\cdot\underline{z})\right\rvert\right)\leqslant 2C_{\eta}\sum_{\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{z}))\right)^{n_{e}}.

(8.5)

Recall that
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
is finite by Lemmas
5.5
and
6.4
. Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
, and let
(q_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
be given by Proposition
7.5
. For all
e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}
, we have either
n_{e}=0
, or
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
and

q_{e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert-1}\frac{p_{e}}{2}\geqslant 1+\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert-1}\geqslant 1+\frac{1}{p-1}=\frac{p}{p-1},

so that
g_{\omega}\in L^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus, multiplying the right-hand side of (
8.5
) by
\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})
, we obtain a function which is independent of
R
, and integrable over
\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
by Lemma
7.11
. Then, by Lebesgue’s Theorem, the last term in (
8.4
) goes to
0
as
R\to+\infty
, and hence so does the first. This proves that, in order to prove (
8.3
), it is enough to prove that this asymptotics holds when
f_{R}
is replaced by
f
on the left-hand side.

Step 2: Convergence for the limit field
f
.

Since the limit field
f
is stationary, the covariance operator
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\cdot)
is invariant by diagonal translations, see Remark
3.28
. By Lemma
3.30
.
4
and Section
4.2
, the maps
\left(\mathcal{G}_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
,
\left(\theta_{J}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
, and
\left(\Pi_{2J}^{2[J]_{\mathcal{I}}}\right)_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
all are translation-invariant. Hence
\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\cdot)
is also translation-invariant. By the same change of variable as in the first step, we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\underline{x}\in(R\Omega)^{\mathcal{K}}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{x}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}

(8.6)

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\tau+z_{K}}{R}\right)\dmesure\!\tau\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{z}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z}

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left(\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(y+\frac{z_{K}}{R}\right)\dmesure\!y\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{z}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z}.

Let us fix
\underline{z}=(z_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
. Let
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
i\in K
. By continuity of the translations we have
\phi_{i}\left(\cdot+\frac{z_{K}}{R}\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\phi_{i}
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By continuity of the product from
\left(L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{p}
to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we obtain
\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\cdot+\frac{z_{K}}{R}\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus, in the last line of (
8.6
), the integrand converges pointwise to
\left(\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}(y)\dmesure\!y\right)\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f,\underline{z})\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})
. Recalling that the integral over
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is bounded by
\prod_{i=1}^{p}\lVert\phi_{i}\rVert_{p}
, uniformly in
\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}
and
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
, we can use the same dominating function as in the first step, see Lemmas
8.2
and
7.11
, and the conclusion follows by another dominated convergence.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.
3
.

We start by dealing with the case
p\geqslant 2
under the hypothesis that (
DC
(
2p-1,\frac{p}{p-1}
)
) holds. Then, we deal with the case
p=1
, for the sake of completeness.

Case
p\geqslant 2
.

Let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Let
\eta>0
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. For all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
, we use the expression of
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})
derived in Corollary
8.4
. Multiplying by
R^{d(p-1)}
and using Lemma
8.5
to compute the asymptotics of each term in this expression, we obtain:

R^{d(p-1)}\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{p}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!x,

where

\gamma_{p}(f)=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p,d-k}}\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}}\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\sum_{\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}}\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}\left(f,\underline{z}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{z}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{z}.

Recalling Definition
4.27
and Lemma
4.30
, we can simplify the expression of this constant:

\gamma_{p}(f)=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p,d-k}}\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}}\int_{\underline{z}\in\accentset{\bullet}{{{\boxslash}}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{z})\dmesure\!\underline{z}=\sum_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p,d-k}}\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\underline{z}\in\flat^{-1}(0)}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(f,\underline{z})\dmesure\!\underline{z}.

Observing that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}}
actually only depends on
\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert
and recalling Definition
2.24
, we finally obtain:

\gamma_{p}(f)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle p^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\left\{\underline{z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{p}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\flat(\underline{z})=0\right\}}\mathcal{F}_{p}(f,\underline{z})\dmesure\!\underline{z},&\text{if}\ k<d,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{p}\card\left(\left\{\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert=q\right\}\right)\,q^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\left\{\underline{z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\flat(\underline{z})=0\right\}}\mathcal{F}_{q}(f,\underline{z})\dmesure\!\underline{z},&\text{if}\ k=d.\end{cases}

(8.7)

Case
p=1
.

In this case, we work under the only assumption that (
Reg
(
2
)
), (
ND
(
1
)
) and (
ScL
(
1
)
) hold. In particular, we cannot use the results from Section
8.1
. We denote by
\mathcal{I}=\{\{1\}\}
the only partition of
\{1\}
for simplicity.

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, for all
x\in RU
, we have
\accentset{\bullet}{(x,x)}=0
and, by Definitions
3.1
,
3.22
and
3.27
and Lemma
3.23
we have:

\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},x,x)=\Var\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f_{R},x,x)\right)=\Var\left(\Psi_{0}\circ\Delta_{(x,x)}(f_{R})\right)=\Psi_{0}\Var\left(f_{R}(x),D_{x}f_{R}\right)\Psi_{0}^{*}.

(8.8)

Since
f_{\infty}=f
is stationary, the function
x\mapsto\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,x,x)
is constant. By (
ND
(
1
)
), the Gaussian vector
(f(0),D_{0}f)
is non-degenerate. Since
\Psi_{0}
is an isomorphism, we have
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,0,0)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
. Then, we deduce from (
ScL
(
1
)
) that
\sup_{x\in R\Omega}\left\lVert\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},x,x)-\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,0,0)\right\rVert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0
. Hence, there exists
R_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
and a compact
\Gamma_{0}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}^{+}
such that
\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f_{R},x,x)\in\Gamma_{0}
for all
R\geqslant R_{0}
and
x\in R\Omega
.

The maps
\mathcal{G}_{\{1\}}
,
\theta_{\{1\}}
and
\Upsilon_{\{1\}}
are translation-invariant, see Section
4.2
and Lemma
4.21
, hence constant. The map
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is uniformly continuous on
\left\{\left(\mathcal{G}_{\{1\}}(0),\theta_{\{1\}}(0)\right)\right\}\times\Gamma_{0}
by Lemma
4.20
. Hence, letting
\gamma_{1}(f)=\rho_{\{1\}}(f,0)
, the previous uniform convergence and Lemma
4.18
imply that

\sup_{x\in R\Omega}\left\lvert\rho_{\{1\}}(f_{R},x)-\gamma_{1}(f)\right\rvert\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

(8.9)

Let
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)
and
R\geqslant R_{0}
. By Equation (
8.8
), for all
x\in R\Omega
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f_{R}(x),D_{x}f_{R}\right)
is non-degenerate. Moreover,
\rho_{\{1\}}(f_{R},\cdot)
is bounded over
R\Omega
by (
8.9
), hence
\phi\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\rho_{\{1\}}(f_{R},\cdot)\in L^{1}(R\Omega)
. Using the Kac–Rice formula for the expectation, see Theorem
4.5
, and the uniform convergence derived in Equation (
8.9
), we have:

\displaystyle\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\right\rangle\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\frac{Z_{R}}{R^{d}}\,,\phi\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rangle\right]=\frac{1}{R^{d}}\int_{R\Omega}\phi\left(\frac{x}{R}\right)\rho_{\{1\}}(f_{R},x)\dmesure\!x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\rho_{\{1\}}(f_{R},Rx)\dmesure\!x\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x.

Finally, since
\left(f(0),D_{0}f\right)
is non-degenerate, we have
\gamma_{1}(f)=\rho_{\{1\}}(f,0)>0
, see Definition
4.4
.
∎

8.3
Asymptotic upper bound for cumulants under (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
)

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.7
.
2
. We work under the assumption that only (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
) holds. In particular, the results from Section
8.1
are still valid, but not those of Section
8.2
.

Let
\eta>0
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}
and
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
be such that
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
. Finally, let
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
be functions in
L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, that we extend by
0
outside of
\Omega
.

Lemma 8.6

(Continuity with respect to the decay function)
.

Let us assume that
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 2
. Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
and let
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
be the associated exponents given by Lemma
7.10
. For all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
, for all
\underline{g}=(g_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and
\widetilde{\underline{g}}=(\widetilde{g}_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have:

\left\lvert\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}-\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\widetilde{g}_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x}\right\rvert\\
\leqslant R^{\frac{d\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},2}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{K\in I}\left\lVert\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\right\rVert_{2\lvert I\rvert},

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{op},2}
is the operator norm for multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(L^{2\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{I}
.

Proof.

Let
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
and
\underline{g},\widetilde{\underline{g}}\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We let
\varphi_{K}=\prod_{i\in K}\lvert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\rvert\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{K})_{K\in\mathcal{K}}
. Recalling Definition
7.9
, and using Lemma
7.10
.
2
, the integral we want to control is

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\underline{\varphi},\underline{g}\right)-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\underline{\varphi},\widetilde{\underline{g}}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\left(\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\cdot,\underline{g}\right)-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}}\right)\right)(\underline{\varphi})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\cdot,\underline{g}\right)-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}\left(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}}\right)\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},2}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{K\in I}\lVert\varphi_{K}\rVert_{2\lvert I\rvert}.

For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
K\in I
, we have
\lVert\varphi_{K}\rVert_{2\lvert I\rvert}=R^{\frac{d}{2\lvert I\rvert}}\left\lVert\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\right\rVert_{2\lvert I\rvert}
, see Equation (
8.2
). Then, the conclusion follows from
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{K\in I}\frac{1}{2\lvert I\rvert}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{1}{2}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}
.
∎

Corollary 8.7

(Asymptotic upper bound)
.

If
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 3
then, for all
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we have:

\frac{1}{R^{\frac{d\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}}}\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{w}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0.

Proof.

Let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
and let
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
be the exponents from Proposition
7.5
. For all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, we have
p_{e}\geqslant 2
, hence
g_{\omega}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\underline{g}=(g_{\omega},\dots,g_{\omega})\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\widetilde{\underline{g}}=(\widetilde{g}_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
q_{e}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert-1}\frac{p_{e}}{2}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
. For all
K\in\mathcal{K}
let
\varphi_{K}=\prod_{i\in K}\left\lvert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rvert
. Applying Lemma
7.10
.
3
to
\widetilde{\underline{g}}
and proceeding as above, we obtain:

\left\lvert\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}\widetilde{g}_{e}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}\right\rvert=\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi},\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert\\
\leqslant\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},\mathcal{I}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{K\in I}\left\lVert\varphi_{K}\right\rVert_{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}\leqslant R^{d}\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},\mathcal{I}}\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{K\in I}\left\lVert\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\right\rVert_{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}

by (
8.2
), since
\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{i\in I}\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}=1
. Here
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},\mathcal{I}}
is the operator norm for multilinear forms on
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}L^{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\lvert I\rvert}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{I}
. Then, by Lemma
8.6
, we have:

\frac{1}{R^{\frac{d\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}}}\left\lvert\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{a\in K}\phi_{a}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}g_{w}\left(\ell_{e}\left(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x})\right)\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}\right\rvert\\
\leqslant C\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},2}+R^{d(1-\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2})}\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op},\mathcal{I}}\right),

(8.10)

where
C\geqslant 0
is some constant depending on
\underline{\phi}
.

For all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
, the subspace
L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, if
p_{e}=+\infty
then
q_{e}=+\infty
; and if
p_{e}<+\infty
then
q_{e}<+\infty
, and
L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
contains the space of continuous functions with compact support, which is dense in
L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in this case. Hence,
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. The density we just established and Lemma
7.10
.
2
prove that there exists a function
\widetilde{\underline{g}}\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{q_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\underline{g})-\mathcal{H}_{G,\mathcal{J},\eta}(\cdot,\widetilde{\underline{g}})\right\rVert_{\textrm{op},2}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Then, since
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 3
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough the right-hand side of (
8.10
) is less than
2C\varepsilon
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.
2
.

Let
p\geqslant 3
. Let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we extend them by
0
outside of
\Omega
. Let
\eta>0
and
R_{\eta}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Lemma
8.1
. For all
R\geqslant R_{\eta}
, we can express
\kappa(\nu_{R})(\underline{\phi})
by Corollary
8.4
, where
\underline{\phi}=(\phi_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant p}
. Then, we are reduced to upper bounding a finite sum of terms of the form:

\frac{1}{R^{pd}}\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left\lvert\widetilde{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}(f_{R},\underline{x})\right\rvert\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\dmesure\!\underline{x},

where
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}
and
\mathcal{J}\leqslant\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
. By Lemma
8.2
, it is actually enough to upper bound a finite number of terms of the following form, where
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
:

\frac{1}{R^{pd}}\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}}\left\lvert\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{x_{K}}{R}\right)\right\rvert\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}}g_{\omega}\left(\ell_{e}(\flat_{\mathcal{I}}(\underline{x}))\right)^{n_{e}}\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}.

(8.11)

If
\card(\mathcal{I})\geqslant 3
, for all
\underline{n}\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
, the term in (
8.11
) is
o(R^{d(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}-p)})
, by Corollary
8.7
. Since
\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert\leqslant\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert\leqslant p
, these terms are indeed
o(R^{-\frac{pd}{2}})
.

If
\card(\mathcal{I})=2
, let
G=(\mathcal{I},\underline{n})\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{I}}
and let
(p_{e})_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}
be the associated exponents given by Proposition
7.5
. As in the proof of Corollary
8.7
, we have
\underline{g}=(g_{\omega},\dots,g_{\omega})\in\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{G}}L^{p_{e}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
since we assumed that (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
) holds. Applying Lemma
8.6
with
\widetilde{\underline{g}}=0
, we obtain that the term in (
8.11
) is
O(R^{d(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}-p)})
. Since
\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert=2
and
p\geqslant 3
, we have
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{I}\rvert}{2}-p<-\frac{p}{2}
. Thus, terms of the form (
8.11
) with
\card(\mathcal{I})=2
are also
o(R^{-\frac{pd}{2}})
.

If
\card(\mathcal{I})=1
, then
\mathcal{I}=\{\mathcal{K}\}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\curlywedge 2}=\emptyset
. In this case, the integral in (
8.11
) can be dealt with by the same change of variable as in (
7.6
), recall Definition
7.7
. Letting
\varphi_{K}=\prod_{i\in K}\left\lvert\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)\right\rvert
for all
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
\underline{\varphi}=\left(\varphi_{K}\right)_{K\in\mathcal{K}}
, we obtain:

\int_{{{\boxslash}}_{\{\mathcal{K}\},\eta}}\left(\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\varphi_{K}(x_{K})\right)\left(\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\Upsilon_{J}(\underline{x}_{J})\right)\dmesure\!\underline{x}=\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})(\tau)\dmesure\!\tau=\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert^{\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})\right\rVert_{1}.

By Lemma
7.8
and Equation (
8.2
), we have

\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{J},\eta}(\underline{\varphi})\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\left\lVert\varphi_{K}\right\rVert_{\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert}\leqslant C_{\mathcal{J},\eta}\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}}R^{\frac{d}{\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert}}\left\lVert\prod_{i\in K}\phi_{i}\right\rVert_{\lvert\mathcal{K}\rvert}=O(R^{d}).

Then, terms of the form (
8.11
) with
\card(\mathcal{I})=1
are
O(R^{d(1-p)})
. Since
p\geqslant 3
, these terms are
o(R^{-\frac{pd}{2}})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

9
The case of critical points

As explained in the introduction, Theorem
1.8
is not a consequence of Theorem
1.7
because gradient fields never satisfy Hypothesis (
ND
(
q
)
) for
q\geqslant 1
. The purpose of this section is to explain how to adapt the contents of Sections
2
to
8
in order to prove Theorem
1.8
. We deal with modifications concerning Kergin interpolation in Section
9.1
and with those concerning Kac–Rice formulas in Section
9.2
. The few other adaptations we need are dealt with in Section
9.3
.

9.1
Kergin interpolation for gradient fields

The goal of this section is to explain what should be adapted in Section
3
in order to deal with the case of gradient fields. The key point is to remark that the Kergin interpolant of a gradient is itself the gradient of some polynomial, see
[
23
]
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
, recall that
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]
is the space of polynomials of degree at most
q
in
d
variables
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{d})
. If
Q\in\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
, then
\nabla Q\in\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{d}
is a polynomial map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to itself.

Definition 9.1

(Polynomial gradients)
.

We denote by
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]=\left\{\nabla Q\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}Q\in\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]\right\}
.

Lemma 9.2

(Translation-invariance of
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
)
.

For all
q\in\mathbb{N}
, the set
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
is a vector subspace of
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{d}
, which is invariant under the action of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by translation.

Proof.

Let
P\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
Q\in\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
be such that
\nabla Q=P
, we have:

\tau\cdot P=\nabla Q(X+\tau)=\nabla(\tau\cdot Q)\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X].\qed

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex open subset. The Poincaré Lemma implies the following result.

Lemma 9.3

(Kergin interpolant of gradients)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert}(U,\mathbb{R})
. For all
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, we have
K(\nabla h,\underline{x})\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
and
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\nabla h,\underline{x})\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, the fact that
K(\nabla h,\underline{x})\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
is proved in
[
23
, Lem. 2.5]
. Then, we deduce that
\accentset{\bullet}{K}(\nabla h,\underline{x})=\flat(\underline{x})\cdot K(\nabla h,\underline{x})\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
by translation-invariance, see Lemma
9.2
.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
9.3
, if
k=d
and
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the gradient of
h:U\to\mathbb{R}
, we can replace spaces of the form
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
by the subspaces
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
in Sections
3
to
8
. Regularity results and estimates remain true by restriction. We need to check that surjectivity and non-degeneracy results also remain true after restricting to these subspaces of polynomial gradients. The following two results are the counterparts for gradients of Lemma
3.7
and Corollary
3.8
respectively.

Lemma 9.4

(Surjectivity of block-wise interpolation for gradients)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set, let
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\underline{x})
. The linear map
P\mapsto\left(K(P,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
is surjective from
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
to
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
.

Proof.

We use the same partition of unity
(\chi_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.7
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
P_{I}\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
and
Q_{I}\in\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
be such that
P_{I}=\nabla Q_{I}
. Let
h=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\chi_{I}Q_{I}
and
P=K(\nabla h,\underline{x})
. By Lemma
9.3
, we have
P\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert A\rvert}[X]
. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we have:

K(P,\underline{x}_{I})=K(K(\nabla h,\underline{x}),\underline{x}_{I})=K(\nabla h,\underline{x}_{I})=K(\nabla Q_{I},\underline{x}_{I})=K(P_{I},\underline{x}_{I})=P_{I},

since
h=Q_{I}
on a neighborhood of
\conv(\underline{x}_{I})
and
K(\cdot,\underline{x}_{I})
is the identity on
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]\subset\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{d}
, see Theorem
3.4
. Hence the result.
∎

Let
h:U\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
function. By definition, we have
Dh:x\mapsto\langle\nabla h(x)\,,\cdot\rangle
, where
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
stands for the canonical inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for all
x\in U
and all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

D^{2}_{x}h(u,v)=\langle D_{x}(\nabla h)\cdot u\,,v\rangle.

(9.1)

That is,
D_{x}(\nabla h)\in\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the self-adjoint operator corresponding to the symmetric bilinear form
D_{x}^{2}h
through the Euclidean structure.

Corollary 9.5

(
1
-jets surjectivity for gradients)
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\setminus{{\boxslash}}
, the linear map
P\mapsto\left(\left(P(x_{a}),D_{x_{a}}P\right)\right)_{a\in A}
is surjective from
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
to
\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)^{A}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Corollary
3.8
. Since
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
, Lemma
9.4
shows that the map
P\mapsto\left(K(P,x_{a},x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is surjective from
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
to
(\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X])^{A}
. For all
a\in A
, the polynomials
P
and
K(P,x_{a},x_{a})
share the same value and the same differential at
x_{a}
. Thus, to conclude it is enough to prove that, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the map
\vartheta_{y}:P\mapsto(P(y),D_{y}P)
is an isomorphism from
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Q\in\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]
, we have
\vartheta_{y}(\nabla Q)=\left(\nabla Q(y),D_{y}(\nabla Q)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see (
9.1
). Hence
\vartheta_{y}\left(\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For all
P\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]
, we have
P=P(y)+D_{y}P(X-y)
, since
P
is a polynomial map of degree
1
. Hence
\vartheta_{y}
is injective. The kernel of
\nabla:\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]\to\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X]
is
\mathbb{R}_{0}[X]\simeq\mathbb{R}
, so that
\dim(\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2}[X])=\dim(\mathbb{R}_{2}[X])-1=d+\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
. Hence
\vartheta_{y}
is actually an isomorphism by a dimension argument.
∎

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Let
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert}(U,\mathbb{R})
be a centered Gaussian field and let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\lvert A\rvert-1}(U,\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the centered Gaussian field
f=\nabla h
. For all
\underline{x}\in U^{A}
, the random vector
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\in\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{d}
belongs deterministically to the subspace
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
, see Lemma
9.3
. Thus, in the case where
f
is a gradient field, we can consider
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}}
as a centered Gaussian vector in
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
, and its variance operator
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})=\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(\nabla h,\underline{x})
as an element of
\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]\right)
, compare Definition
3.27
. With this in mind, we can state the following counterpart of Lemma
3.40
for gradient fields.

Lemma 9.6

(Uniform non-degeneracy for gradient fields)
.

Let
A
be a finite set of cardinality
q+1
. Let
h\in\mathcal{C}^{q+1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
be a stationary centered Gaussian field and let
f=\nabla h
. We assume that
f
satisfies (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) and (
DCL
(
q,\infty
)
). Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\delta>0
, there exists
\eta\in(0,\delta]
such that
\left\{\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\underline{x}\in{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\eta}\cap{{\boxslash}}_{\mathcal{I},\delta}\right\}
is relatively compact in
\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]\right)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.40
, except for Step 3 which needs to be adapted in order to use (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) instead of (
ND
(
q
)
).

First, we observe that, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the map
\nabla:\mathbb{R}_{p}[X]\to\nabla\mathbb{R}_{p}[X]
is surjective and its kernel is
\mathbb{R}_{0}[X]
. Hence, it restricts into an isomorphism from
\Span\left\{X^{\beta}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant p\right\}
to
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{p}[X]
. In particular,
\left(\nabla(X^{\beta})\right)_{0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant p}
is a basis of
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{p}[X]
.

Then, let
(e_{1},\dots,e_{d})
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With the same notation as in the proof of Lemma
3.40
, we modify Step 3 as follows. For all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}
, we have
\underline{x}_{I}=(y_{I},\dots,y_{I})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{I}
. Hence, recalling that
f=\nabla h
, we have:

\displaystyle\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert<\lvert I\rvert}\frac{\partial^{\alpha}f(y_{I})}{\alpha!}X^{\alpha}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant d\\
\lvert\alpha\rvert<\lvert I\rvert\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{i}h(y_{I})}{\alpha!}X^{\alpha}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant d\\
\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant\lvert I\rvert;\beta_{i}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial^{\beta}h(y_{I})}{\beta!}\beta_{i}X^{\beta-\mathbf{1}_{i}}\otimes e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant d\\
0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant\lvert I\rvert\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial^{\beta}h(y_{I})}{\beta!}\beta_{i}X^{\beta-\mathbf{1}_{i}}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant\lvert I\rvert}\frac{\partial^{\beta}h(y_{I})}{\beta!}\nabla(X^{\beta}).

Since
\left(\nabla(X^{\beta})\right)_{0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant\lvert I\rvert}
is a basis of
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}
, we have that
\left(\accentset{\bullet}{K}(f,\underline{x}_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}
is non-degenerate in
\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]
if and only if the centered Gaussian vector
\left(\partial^{\beta}h(y_{I})\right)_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J};0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant\lvert I\rvert}
is non-degenerate. Since
J\subset A
, we have
\lvert J\rvert\leqslant q+1
, and the last condition is implied by (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
), because
\underline{y}=(y_{I})_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\notin{{\boxslash}}
.

Finally, we obtain that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{I}_{J}}(f,\underline{x}_{J})\in\sym^{+}\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{J}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{\lvert I\rvert}[X]\right)
, and we can conclude by contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
3.40
.
∎

Except for the three results we just discussed, the content of Section
3
adapts directly to the case of gradient fields by replacing spaces of the form
\mathbb{R}_{q}[X]^{k}
by the corresponding
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{q+1}[X]
; replacing the use of Hypothesis (
ND
(
q
)
) by that of Hypothesis (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
); and bearing in mind that the field
f
(resp.
f_{R}
) is the gradient of a field
h
(resp.
h_{R}
).

9.2
Kac–Rice formulas for gradient fields

In this section, we discuss the changes we need to make in order to adapt the content of Section
4
to the case of gradient fields.

The first point is inherited from the changes discussed in Section
9.1
: we replace spaces of the form
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
, where
A
is a non-empty finite set, by
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
. For example, for all
\underline{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}
, we now have
\ev_{\underline{x}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\left(\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X],(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{A}\right)
and
\mathcal{G}_{A}(\underline{x})\in\Gr\left(d\lvert A\rvert,\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]\right)
, cf. Lemmas
4.8
and
4.10
. These changes affect similarly Definitions
4.12
,
4.14
,
4.15
,
4.16
,
4.28
and
4.29
. In particular, if
f
is the gradient of some field
h
and
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
, we now have:

\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(f,\underline{x})=\Sigma_{2\mathcal{I}}(\nabla h,\underline{x})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\sym\left(\prod_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]\right).

Then, we need to relax the hypotheses of the Bulinskaya Lemma (Proposition
4.3
) and the Kac–Rice formula (Theorem
4.5
). Indeed, if the centered Gaussian field
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the gradient of some field
h:U\to\mathbb{R}
then, for all
x\in U
, the Gaussian vector
\left(f(x),D_{x}f\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is degenerate as a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It turns out that, for fields from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to itself, and in particular for gradient fields, we can replace Proposition
4.3
by the following.

Proposition 9.7

(Bulinskaya Lemma, maximal codimension case)
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(U,\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a centered Gaussian field such that, for all
x\in U
, the Gaussian vector
f(x)
is non-degenerate. Then, almost-surely,

\left\{x\in U\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}f(x)=0\ \text{and}\ \Jac\left(D_{x}f\right)=0\right\}=\emptyset.

In particular,
Z=f^{-1}(0)
is almost-surely a closed discrete subset of
U
.

Proof.

This is a particular case of
[
11
, Prop. 6.5]
.
∎

The gradient fields we consider satisfy these relaxed hypotheses, so we can replace the use of Proposition
4.3
by that of Proposition
9.7
in their case. Similarly, we can relax the hypotheses of Theorem
4.5
in the case where
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by removing the assumption that
\left(f(x),D_{x}f\right)
be non-degenerate for all
x\in U
. This is because this non-degeneracy hypothesis is only there to ensure that we can use the Bulinskaya Lemma (see
[
11
, Thm. 6.2 and 6.3]
), which is already ensured in this case by the assumption that
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
be non-degenerate for all
\underline{x}\notin{{\boxslash}}
.

Remark 9.8
.

Let
A
be a non-empty finite set and
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a centered Gaussian field. The expressions of the Kac–Rice densities
\rho_{A}(f,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{F}_{A}(f,\cdot)
, see Definitions
4.4
and
4.27
, do not depend on whether
f
is a gradient field or not. As a consequence, the same is true of the constants
\gamma_{p}(f)
in Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
, see (
8.7
). On the contrary, the expressions of
\Upsilon_{A}
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
, see Definitions
4.14
,
4.16
and
4.29
, depend on whether we consider gradient fields or not.

We conclude this section by pointing out three other points where the content of Section
4
needs to be mildly modified in order to deal with gradient fields. Everything else adapts seamlessly.

•

In the proof of Lemma
4.11
, we need to choose
\Lambda_{0}\in\sym^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for example
\Lambda_{0}=\Id
. Then, using Corollary
9.5
instead of Corollary
3.8
, we obtain the existence of
P\in\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
such that
P(x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x}))=0
and
D_{x_{a}-\flat(\underline{x})}P=\Id
for all
a\in A
.

•

The conclusion of Lemma
4.17
needs to be weakened: for gradient fields we can only conclude that
\left(f(x_{a})\right)_{a\in A}
is non-degenerate. This is not an issue, since Lemma
4.17
is only used to check the non-degeneracy hypotheses in the Bulinskaya Lemma and the Kac–Rice formula and we just relaxed these hypotheses for gradient fields. The proof of this weaker version is similar to that of Lemma
4.17
, using Corollary
9.5
instead of Corollary
3.8
.

•

In Step 1 of the proof of Lemma
4.21
, we have to change the definition of
f_{0}
. Let
h_{0}
be a standard Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
. We let
f_{0}=\nabla h_{0}
, so that
f_{0}
is both a gradient field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a centered Gaussian vector in
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
, which is non-degenerate since
\nabla
is surjective and
h_{0}
is non-degenerate. Then, we deduce the local integrability of
\rho_{A}(f_{0},\cdot)
from
[
23
, Thm. 1.13 and Lem. 2.10]
, compare also
[
4
, Thm. 7.7]
. The remainder of the proof is similar to that of Lemma
4.21
.

9.3
Proof of Theorem
1.8
: cumulants asymptotics for critical points

In this section, we discuss the changes to implement in order to turn the proof of Theorem
1.7
into a proof of Theorem
1.8
. We already discussed above the changes to implement in Section
3
and
4
. The contents of Sections
2
,
6
and
7
are completely unaffected by whether the Gaussian fields we consider are gradient fields or not. However, we need to modify slightly Sections
5
and
8
.

At the beginning of Section
5.3
, due to the changes already discussed in Section
9.1
, for all
I\in\mathcal{I}
the space
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
is replaced by
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]
in the case of gradient fields. Since the linear map
\nabla:\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]\to\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]
is surjective with kernel
\mathbb{R}_{0}[X]
, a basis of
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]
is
\left(\nabla(X^{\beta})\right)_{0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant 2\lvert I\rvert}
. We choose to work with the inner product on
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert I\rvert}[X]
making the previous basis orthonormal, this choice being of no consequence to the rest of the argument. We also change the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{I}
, see Equation (
5.4
), into

\mathcal{A}_{I}=\left\{(I,\beta)\in\{I\}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}0<\lvert\beta\rvert\leqslant 2\lvert I\rvert\right\}.

Then, proceeding as in Section
5.3
, we obtain the exact analogue of Proposition
5.10
in the case of gradient fields. Recall that the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}}
depends on whether we consider gradient fields or not, see Remark
9.8
. No other modification is needed in Section
5
.

In Section
8
, we mostly have to implement changes inherited from those of the previous sections: replace
\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert-1}[X]^{k}
by
\nabla\mathbb{R}_{2\lvert A\rvert}[X]
where needed, replace the use of Proposition
4.3
by that of Proposition
9.7
, replace Hypothesis (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) by (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
), etc. Apart from these, we only need to take care of the Case
p=1
in the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
3
. The proof is similar to the same case in the proof of Theorem
1.7
.
3
, except for the fact that
\left(f(0),D_{0}f\right)
is non-degenerate as a Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\sym(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as a consequence of Hypothesis (
\nabla
-
ND
(
1
)
), but not as a Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In the case where
f
and
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
are gradient fields, this is enough for the argument to go through. Finally, we have
\gamma_{2}(f)>0
by
[
26
, thm. 1.7]
.

10
Proofs of limit theorems and other corollaries

In this section, we deduce Corollaries
1.10
,
1.11
and
1.12
and Theorems
1.13
and
1.14
from our cumulants asymptotics.

Let us briefly recall our framework. We consider an unbounded set
\mathcal{R}\subset(0,+\infty)
, as well as a convex open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a non-empty open subset
\Omega\subset U
at positive distance from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus U
. Let
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
and
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered stationary Gaussian field. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
f_{R}:RU\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a centered Gaussian field. We denote by
Z_{R}
the random measure of integration over
f_{R}^{-1}(0)
with respect to its
(d-k)
-dimensional volume measure, which is almost-surely well-defined under our assumptions. Finally, we let
\nu_{R}
be the random measure on
U
defined by (
1.3
).

In the setting of Theorem
1.7
, the limit field
f
satisfies a non-degeneracy hypothesis of the type (
ND
(
q
)
), among other assumptions. In the setting of Theorem
1.8
, we assume that
f
and the
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
are gradient fields. That is,
k=d
and there exist centered Gaussian fields
h
and
(h_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
such that
f=\nabla h
and
f_{R}=\nabla h_{R}
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
. In this case, the limit field
f
satisfies, among other things, a non-degeneracy hypothesis of the type (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
). Since both settings are formally identical, we can deal with both of them at once in the following.

10.1
Moment asymptotics, concentration and hole probability

This section deals with the proofs of Corollaries
1.10
,
1.11
and
1.12
. We start by deducing the asymptotics of the joint moments of the linear statistics of
\nu_{R}
from that of their joint cumulants.

Proof of Corollary
1.10
.
1
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume that (
Reg
(
\max(2,2p-1)
)
), (
ScL
(
2p-1
)
) and (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) hold. Moreover, we assume that either (
\nabla
-
ND
(
2p-1
)
) or (
ND
(
2p-1
)
) holds, depending on whether we consider gradient fields or not.

Let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Let
R\in\mathcal{R}
, for all
i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
we have
\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right\rvert\leqslant\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rvert\right\rangle
. Thus, it is enough to prove that
\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rvert\rangle\right]<+\infty
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough. By Definition
2.12
and Corollary
2.14
, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\left\lvert\phi_{i}\right\rvert\rangle\right]=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa\left(\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rvert\rangle\right)_{j\in J}\right)=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa(\nu_{R})\left(\left(\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rvert\right)_{j\in J}\right)

Under our assumptions, either by Theorem
1.7
.
1
or Theorem
1.8
.
1
, for all non-empty
J\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
we have that
\kappa(\nu_{R})\left(\left(\left\lvert\phi_{j}\right\rvert\right)_{j\in J}\right)
is well-defined and finite for
R
large enough, not depending on
J
nor on
(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})
. Hence the result.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.10
.
2
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we work under the same assumptions as above, except that Hypothesis (
DC
(
2p-1,\infty
)
) is replaced by the stronger Hypothesis (
DC
(
2p-1,2
)
).

Let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. By Corollary
2.14
, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right]=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\kappa(\nu_{R})\left(\left(\phi_{j}\right)_{j\in J}\right).

(10.1)

Under our hypotheses, by Theorems
1.7
.
2
and
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorems
1.8
.
2
and
1.8
.
3
in the case of gradient fields), for all
J\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
such that
\lvert J\rvert\geqslant 2
, we have
\kappa(\nu_{R})\left(\left(\phi_{j}\right)_{j\in J}\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}0
. The terms associated with singletons being convergent as
R\to+\infty
, all the products on the right-hand side of (
10.1
) vanish in the limit, except maybe for the one indexed by
\left\{\{i\}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}1\leqslant i\leqslant p\right\}=\bigwedge\mathcal{P}_{p}
. Thus, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi_{i})+o(1)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)^{p}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi_{i}(x)\dmesure\!x,

which proves (
1.5
).

Let us now deal with central moments. Let
J\subset\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, developing the joint cumulant by multi-linearity and using Lemma
2.11
to cancel the terms where an expectation appears, we have:

\displaystyle R^{\lvert J\rvert\frac{d}{2}}\kappa\left(\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{j}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{j}\rangle\right]\right)_{j\in J}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R^{\lvert J\rvert\frac{d}{2}}\kappa(\nu_{R})\left(\left(\phi_{j}\right)_{j\in J}\right),&\text{if}\ \lvert J\rvert\geqslant 2;\\
0,&\text{if}\ \lvert J\rvert=1;\end{cases}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\begin{cases}\gamma_{2}(f)\displaystyle\int_{x\in\Omega}\prod_{j\in J}\phi_{j}(x)\dmesure\!x,&\text{if}\ \lvert J\rvert=2;\\
0,&\text{otherwise};\end{cases}

by Theorems
1.7
.
2
and
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorems
1.8
.
2
and
1.8
.
3
in the case of gradient fields). Then, proceeding as in the case of non-central moments, by Corollary
2.14
we have:

\displaystyle R^{\frac{pd}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}R^{\lvert J\rvert\frac{d}{2}}\kappa\left(\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{j}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{j}\rangle\right]\right)_{j\in J}\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}\\
\forall J\in\mathcal{J},\lvert J\rvert=2\end{subarray}}\prod_{J\in\mathcal{J}}\left(\gamma_{2}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\prod_{j\in J}\phi_{j}(x)\dmesure\!x\right),

which is a reformulation of (
1.6
).
∎

Classically, the asymptotics we just obtained for central moments imply that the linear statistics of
\nu_{R}
concentrate around their mean as
R\to+\infty
. We now prove that they also concentrate around the asymptotics of their mean.

Proof of Corollary
1.11
.

Let
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\varepsilon>0
. We let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and assume that (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
), (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) and (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
) in the case of gradient fields) hold.

Either by Theorem
1.7
.
3
or Theorem
1.8
.
3
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, we have:

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert=\left\lvert\kappa(\nu_{R})(\phi)-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Thus,
\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon
implies that
\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Using Markov’s inequality and the estimates for the central moment of order
2p
given by Corollary
1.10
.
2
, we obtain:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\\
\leqslant\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)^{2p}\right]=O\left(\frac{1}{R^{pd}}\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

The concentration result of Corollary
1.11
translates into an estimate for the hole probability of
\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}
, where
Z_{R}
stands for the vanishing locus of
f_{R}
.

Proof of Corollary
1.12
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we assume that (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
), (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) and (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
) in the case of gradient fields) hold.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\Omega
be a non-empty open set. Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{O}
be a closed ball of positive radius, so that its indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
belongs to
L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and is compactly supported in
\mathcal{O}
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
, if
\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}\cap\mathcal{O}=\emptyset
, then
Z_{R}\cap R\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
, and
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle=0
by Equation (
1.4
). In this case,
\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert=\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}}\dmesure\!x
. Hence, applying Corollary
1.11
with
\varepsilon=\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\mathcal{B}}\dmesure\!x>0
, we obtain:

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{R}Z_{R}\cap\mathcal{O}=\emptyset\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R}\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right)=O\left(R^{-pd}\right).\qed

10.2
Proof of Theorems
1.6
and
1.13
: Law of Large Numbers

We start by proving Theorem
1.13
. Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, in the following, we assume that (
Reg
(
4p-1
)
), (
ScL
(
4p-1
)
), (
DC
(
4p-1,2
)
) and (
ND
(
4p-1
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
4p-1
)
) in the case of gradient fields) hold. We also fix a sequence
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
of elements of
\mathcal{R}
such that
\sum_{n\geqslant 1}R_{n}^{-pd}<+\infty
. Let us first prove the Law of Large Numbers for the linear statistics of
\nu_{R}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.13
: the case of linear statistics.

Let
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.13
, we can apply Corollary
1.10
to derive the asymptotics of the central moment of order
2p
of
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle
.

Since
\sum_{n\geqslant 1}R_{n}^{-pd}<+\infty
, we have
R_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
. Hence, by Corollary
1.10
.
1
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that, for all
n\geqslant n_{0}
, the random variable
\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle
has a finite moment of order
2p
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n\geqslant n_{0}}\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)^{2p}\right]=\sum_{n\geqslant n_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)^{2p}\right]<+\infty,

since
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)^{2p}\right]=O(R_{n}^{-pd})
by Corollary
1.10
.
2
. Thus, almost-surely, we have

\sum_{n\geqslant n_{0}}\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)^{2p}<+\infty,

which implies
\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right\rvert\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
. We have
\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x
by (
1.5
). Hence,
\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\text{a.s.}}\gamma_{1}(f)\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\dmesure\!x
.
∎

We can now prove a functional Law of Large Numbers, that is, for the random measures
\nu_{R}
themselves.

Proof of Theorem
1.13
: the case of random measures.

We want to show that
\nu_{R_{n}}
almost-surely converges to
\gamma_{1}(f)\dmesure\!x
in the sense of the weak-
*
convergence for Radon measures on
\Omega
; i.e., that almost-surely, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\Omega)
, we have
\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x
. This follows from the first part of Theorem
1.13
and an approximation argument.

Recall that the usual topology of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\Omega)
is characterized by the fact that
\phi_{j}\xrightarrow[j\to+\infty]{}\phi
if and only if there exists a compact
K\subset\Omega
containing the supports of all the
(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\lVert\phi_{j}-\phi\rVert_{\infty}\xrightarrow[j\to+\infty]{}0
. Recall also that
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
endowed with this topology is separable. Let
(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
be a dense sequence in this space. Let
\phi_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}
denote the indicator function of
\Omega
.

Let
(K_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence of compact subsets of
\Omega
such that
\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}K_{i}=\Omega
. For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a non-negative
\chi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\Omega)
such that
\chi_{i}
is constant at
1
on
K_{i}
. Then, for all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have
\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{0}(\Omega)\subset L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. By the first part of Theorem
1.13
and countability of
\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have almost-surely:

\forall(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\qquad\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x.

(10.2)

Let us fix a realization of the sequence
(\nu_{R_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
such that (
10.2
) holds, and work with this deterministic sequence of measures. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(\Omega)
, for all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have:

\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\\
\leqslant\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\left\lvert\phi-\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\right\rvert\rangle+\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\right\rvert+\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\left\lvert\phi-\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\right\rvert\dmesure\!x.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be such that the support of
\phi
is contained in
K_{i}
. Then
\phi=\chi_{i}\phi
, so that we have
\lvert\phi-\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\rvert=\chi_{i}\lvert\phi-\phi_{j}\rvert\leqslant\chi_{i}\phi_{0}\lVert\phi-\phi_{j}\rVert_{\infty}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\\
\leqslant\lVert\phi-\phi_{j}\rVert_{\infty}\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{0}\rangle+\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}\phi_{0}\dmesure\!x\right)+\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\right\rvert.

Since we assume that (
10.2
) holds, the sequence
\left(\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{0}\rangle\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is convergent, hence bounded. Thus, there exists
C\geqslant 0
such that, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\leqslant C\lVert\phi-\phi_{j}\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\chi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\right\rvert.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\lVert\phi-\phi_{j}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
. Equation (
10.2
) for
(i,j)
implies that the second term on the right-hand side goes to
0
as
n\to+\infty
. Hence, for all
n
large enough, we have

\left\lvert\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle-\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x\right\rvert\leqslant(C+1)\varepsilon.

This proves that
\langle\nu_{R_{n}}\,,\phi\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\Omega}\phi\dmesure\!x
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{0}(\Omega)
, under the assumption that (
10.2
) holds. This condition being satisfied almost-surely, this concludes the proof.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of Theorem
1.6
. It consists in putting together a special case of Theorem
1.13
with a monotonicity argument.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Let us consider a centered stationary Gaussian field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We let
\mathcal{R}=(0,+\infty)
,
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}=\Omega
and
f_{R}=f
for all
R>0
. Then, as explained in Section
1.4
, the sequence
(f_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}}
fits in our general framework. Moreover, under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.6
, it satisfies (
Reg
(
3
)
), (
ScL
(
3
)
), (
DC
(
3,2
)
) and either (
\nabla
-
ND
(
3
)
) or (
ND
(
3
)
), depending on whether
f
is gradient field or not.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel subset which is star-shaped with respect to
0
. We denote by
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
its indicator function. Recalling Equation (
1.4
), we apply Theorem
1.13
with
\phi=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
,
p=1
and
R_{n}=n^{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. This yields that, almost-surely,

\frac{\Vol_{d-k}(Z\cap n^{2}\mathcal{B})}{n^{2d}}=\langle\nu_{n^{2}}\,,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}}(x)\dmesure\!x=\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B}).

(10.3)

For all
R\geqslant 1
, let us denote by
n_{R}=\lfloor\sqrt{R}\rfloor
, so that
n_{R}^{2}\leqslant R<(n_{R}+1)^{2}
. Since
\mathcal{B}
is star-shaped with respect to
0
, we have
n_{R}^{2}\mathcal{B}\subset R\mathcal{B}\subset(n_{R}+1)^{2}\mathcal{B}
, and hence:

\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap n_{R}^{2}\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\leqslant\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\leqslant\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap(n_{R}+1)^{2}\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}.

Besides,
\frac{1}{(n_{R}+1)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{R}\leqslant\frac{1}{n_{R}^{2}}
, so that

\left(1-\frac{1}{n_{R}+1}\right)^{2d}\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap n_{R}^{2}\mathcal{B}\right)}{(n_{R})^{2d}}\leqslant\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\leqslant\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap(n_{R}+1)^{2}\mathcal{B}\right)}{(n_{R}+1)^{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{n_{R}}\right)^{2d}.

Since
n_{R}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}+\infty
, if (
10.3
) holds, we obtain that
\frac{\Vol_{d-k}\left(Z\cap R\mathcal{B}\right)}{R^{d}}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\gamma_{1}(f)\Vol_{d}(\mathcal{B})
. This concludes the proof, since we know that (
10.3
) holds almost-surely.
∎

10.3
Proof of Theorem
1.14
: Central Limit Theorem

In this section, we deduce the Central Limit Theorem
1.14
from our cumulants asymptotics, under the assumptions that (
Reg
(
q
)
), (
ScL
(
q
)
), (
DC
(
q,2
)
) and (
ND
(
q
)
) (resp. (
\nabla
-
ND
(
q
)
) in the case of gradient fields) hold for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. We start by proving the CLT for linear statistics.

Proof of Theorem
1.14
: the case of linear statistics.

We start by the proving the result for a single linear statistics, that is, for
n=1
. Let
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, we have:

\kappa_{1}\left(R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\right)=0.

Let
p\geqslant 2
be an integer. Under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.14
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, the
p
-th cumulant of
\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle
is well-defined, see Theorem
1.7
.
1
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
1
in the case of gradient fields). Using the multi-linearity of joint cumulants, and the cancellation property of Lemma
2.11
to remove the terms where an expectation appears, we have:

\kappa_{p}\left(R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\right)=R^{\frac{pd}{2}}\kappa_{p}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right).

Then, by Theorem
1.7
.
2
and
1.7
.
3
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
2
and
1.8
.
3
), we have:

\kappa_{p}\left(R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\begin{cases}\gamma_{2}(f)\displaystyle\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi(x)^{2}\dmesure\!x,&\text{if}\ p=2;\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence, we have:
R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\gamma_{2}(f)\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
by Proposition
2.15
.

Now, let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\Lambda=\gamma_{2}(f)\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\end{pmatrix}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
. For all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, let
X_{R}=R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle\right]\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We want to prove that
X_{R}
converges in distribution towards the centered Gaussian of variance
\Lambda
. By Lévy’s Theorem, it is enough to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\langle z\,,X_{R}\rangle}\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle z\,,\Lambda z\rangle}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
stands for the standard Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Let
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\phi_{i}\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. By the previous case, we have

\displaystyle\langle z\,,X_{R}\rangle

\displaystyle=R^{\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\left(\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi_{i}\right\rangle\right]\right)

\displaystyle=R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\gamma_{2}(f)\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right).

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\langle z\,,X_{R}\rangle}\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{2}(f)\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
. The conclusion follows, since

\gamma_{2}(f)\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\gamma_{2}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\phi_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\dmesure\!x=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}\gamma_{2}(f)\int_{x\in\Omega}\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{j}(x)\dmesure\!x=\langle z\,,\Lambda z\rangle.\qed

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving the functional version of the Central Limit Theorem
1.14
. First, we need to recall a few facts about generalized random fields. Note that we use the term “generalized function” instead of “distribution” in order to avoid any possible confusion with the concept of distribution of a random variable.

Definition 10.1

(Test-functions and generalized functions)
.

Recall that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open.

•

A
test-function
on
\Omega
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
with compact support. The space
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
of test-functions is endowed with its standard topology, which we do not need to describe for our purpose. We refer to
[
53
, Chap. I.2]
for more details on this matter.

•

A
generalized function
on
\Omega
is a linear form
T
on
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
, which is continuous in the sense that: for any compact
K\subset\Omega
, there exist
C\geqslant 0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\left\lvert T(\phi)\right\rvert\leqslant C\sum_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leqslant n}\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\phi\right\rVert_{\infty}
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
supported in
K
. We denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
the space of generalized functions.

•

The canonical pairing between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
and
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
is denoted by
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle
, so that we denote
\langle T\,,\phi\rangle
instead of
T(\phi)
for all
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
and
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
.

•

A
generalized random field
on
\Omega
is a random variable taking values in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, endowed with the Borel
\sigma
-field induced by its usual topology, see
[
21
, Sect. III]
for details.

Radon measures on
\Omega
are examples of generalized functions, see
[
53
, Chap. I.2]
. As discussed in Section
8.1
, under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.14
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough,
\nu_{R}
is an almost-surely well-defined Radon measure on
\Omega
. The characterization of
[
21
, p. 61]
shows that these
\nu_{R}
define generalized random fields in the sense of Definition
10.1
.

Lemma 10.2
.

(Mean measure)

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.14
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough, the linear form
\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]:\phi\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]
on
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
defines a generalized function.

Proof.

Let
R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
be given by Theorem
1.7
.
1
(resp. Theorem
1.8
.
1
in the case of gradient fields). Then,
\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]
is a well-defined linear form on
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
such that
R\geqslant R_{1}
.

Let us fix such a
R\geqslant R_{1}
. Let
K\subset\Omega
be compact and
\mathbf{1}_{K}
denote its indicator function. For all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
supported in
K
, we have
\lvert\phi\rvert\leqslant\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{K}
, so that

\left\lvert\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]\,,\phi\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\lvert\phi\rvert\rangle\right]\leqslant\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\mathbf{1}_{K}\rangle\right].

Thus,
\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
.
∎

A generalized random field on
\Omega
can be described by its characteristic functional, which play the same role as the characteristic function for random variables.

Definition 10.3

(Characteristic functional)
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
be a generalized random field. Its
characteristic functional
is the map
\Theta_{T}:\phi\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\langle T\,,\phi\rangle}\right]
from
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
to
\mathbb{C}
.

Proposition 10.4

(Standard Gaussian White Noise)
.

There exists a generalized random field
W
such that
\Theta_{W}:\phi\mapsto e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
, or equivalently such that
\langle W\,,\phi\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
. The field
W
, called the
standard Gaussian White Noise
on
\Omega
, is unique in distribution.

Proof.

Since convergence in
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
implies convergence in the
L^{2}
sense, the map
\phi\mapsto e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
is continuous on
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
. By
[
21
, Sect. II.5.4]
, this map is of positive type. Hence, by the Bochner–Minlos Theorem
[
21
, Thm. II.5.3.(a)]
, there exists a generalized random field
W
on
\Omega
such that
\Theta_{W}:\phi\mapsto e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
.

Let
W
be such a generalized random field. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
. For all
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\langle W\,,\phi_{j}\rangle}\right]=\Theta_{W}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\phi_{j}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\right).

Thus,
\left(\langle W\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
is a centered Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\begin{pmatrix}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\dmesure\!x\end{pmatrix}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
. By
[
21
, Prop. II.4.2.(b)]
, the distribution of
W
is uniquely determined by the distributions of its marginals
\left(\langle W\,,\phi_{i}\rangle\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
. Hence its uniqueness.

We have just seen that, if
\Theta_{W}:\phi\mapsto e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
, then
\langle W\,,\phi\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
. Conversely, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
, if
\langle W\,,\phi\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
, then
\Theta_{W}(\phi)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\langle W\,,\phi\rangle}\right]=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}}
.
∎

Now that we established the existence of the standard Gaussian White Noise and characterized its distribution, our functional CLT follows from the CLT for linear statistics and the Lévy–Fernique Theorem, which extends Lévy’s Continuity Theorem to the setting of generalized random fields.

Proof of Theorem
1.14
: the functional case.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.14
, by the preceding discussion and Lemma
10.2
, for all
R\in\mathcal{R}
large enough,
T_{R}=R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\nu_{R}-\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{R}\right]\right)
is a well-defined generalized random field on
\Omega
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)\subset L^{1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. By Theorem
1.14
in the case of linear statistics, we have:

\langle T_{R}\,,\phi\rangle=R^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\nu_{R}\,,\phi\rangle\right]\right)\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{\text{law}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\gamma_{2}(f)\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(f)}\langle W\,,\phi\rangle,

hence
\Theta_{T_{R}}(\phi)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\langle T_{R}\,,\phi\rangle}\right]\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(f)}\langle W\,,\phi\rangle}\right]=\Theta_{\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(f)}W}(\phi)
. By the Lévy–Fernique Theorem
[
21
, Thm. III.6.5]
, this pointwise convergence of
\Theta_{T_{R}}
implies that
T_{R}\xrightarrow[R\to+\infty]{}\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(f)}W
in distribution in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
.
∎
